# Implementation decision log

> Why the service is built the way it is. Every entry says what was decided, why, what was rejected, and where it lives.
>
> **The rule:** do not re-propose something rejected here without new evidence. If a decision has to change, ask the author and update this file.
>
> Product decisions live in [PRODUCT-V1.md, section 12.1](../PRODUCT-V1.md#121-settled) under the numbers О-…; this file holds implementation decisions, R01… The first entries were collected in T05 from the phase 0 spikes: [T03 — protocol and widget](live/01-spike-protocol.md), [T04 — the OAuth stub](live/02-spike-oauth.md). The prototype's decisions D01–D45 are in [docs/prototype/decisions.md](prototype/decisions.md) and are cited as "D43 of the prototype".

## Protocol and SDK

**R01. Go SDK — v1.8.0.** PRODUCT 7 named v1.7.0; as of 2026-09-16 the current release is v1.8.0 (14.09.2026), a patch release with no protocol changes: the same set of supported versions, with 2026-07-28 still the newest. It adds limits on SSE event size, body size and JSON depth, the `ServerOptions.SupportedProtocolVersions` setting, and an explicit `-32022` code in place of a bare 400. Spike T03 was built and verified on it. — *Why:* strictly newer and fully compatible; there is no reason to stay on the previous patch. — *Where it lives:* `go.mod` (T17), PRODUCT 7.

**R02. The server does not narrow the set of protocol versions.** *(A request that names no version is read by the library as one of 2025-03-26, which takes a batch of messages; R126 refuses a batch before the library reads it.)* We work only over 2026-07-28 (О-20, О-28), but `SupportedProtocolVersions` is left at its default: the SDK already prefers the newest version when negotiating. — *Why:* in strict mode Claude fails to add the connector. Before the main session the host sends two short legacy `initialize` requests with an empty version, and rejecting them by version breaks the "Connect" step (status 400); in the default mode the same runs go entirely over 2026-07-28 — not one real request negotiated an older version. — *Rejected:* a strict "2026-07-28 only" transport; a stateless 2025-11-25 fallback path (О-28). — *Where it lives:* T41; to be re-checked in ChatGPT in T63.

**R83. Every tool call is observed once, at the protocol boundary, and a failure reaches the model as one sentence of ours.** The MCP package wraps every method the protocol library dispatches in a middleware of its own, the boundary. There a tool call gets a span, `tools/call <tool>`, of the server kind and a child of the request's span; it is timed; and it is written in one `tool_call` line, however it ended — answered, refused with a `status` at the top of its payload, failed, or refused by the protocol before any tool ran. The frame that `Define` builds around every tool turns any error into a failure: one English sentence from one table, which becomes the error's text, because the library sends an error's text to the model as it stands. A panic anywhere below the boundary is caught there — in any method, and in the boundary's own work around a call — and answered as an internal error. In a tool call, an error of the library that is not a protocol error, such as a payload that breaks its own output schema, becomes the general failure. A protocol error goes out as the protocol's own, in its own words rather than in those of whatever wrapped it, and the one the protocol has for a fault on this side, an internal error, counts as a failure of ours. A tool whose name the protocol would refuse, or whose input or output the library cannot derive a schema from, refuses to be built rather than being dropped or crashing the start. A request that is not a tool call is passed on as it is, together with the library's answer: its errors describe the request in the protocol's terms, such as a pagination cursor that does not decode, and a client's own mistake is not an internal error of ours. — *Why:* much of what happens to a call happens where no tool runs. The library refuses arguments that do not fit and a tool nobody defined, and fails on a payload after the tool has returned. It also runs handlers on goroutines of its own, which the HTTP layer's recovery does not reach, so an uncaught panic there ends the process. A frame inside the tool alone would miss all of that. The span's parent is the request's, not the trace context a client may put in `_meta` as the OpenTelemetry conventions for MCP suggest: one request is one tree, as the task asks, and a client does not choose what is kept (R49). A refusal is read from the result's own `status` — the field that makes it a refusal for the model and the widget — rather than reported a second time by the tool. By R73's rule for what a caller chose, the client is written as the chat host it belongs to — a word read out of its name, never the name itself — and the protocol version is held to the library's list. The library's own logging is off, since its lines are held to no list of fields. The request's cancellation is not passed to the tool: a recorded answer must not be abandoned because the host hung up, and the tool's own outbound calls carry their own deadlines. — *Consequence:* the text of an argument refusal is the library's, and it reaches the model (SPEC remark 36). The request's line has no `user`, while the call's line has it (SPEC 12.1). The library sets `no-cache, no-transform` on every answer, and the endpoint rewrites it to `no-store, no-transform` as the headers leave (the author's decision; SPEC 9.2). A handler of ours for a method that is not a tool call — the widget's resource, in T42 — answers in the protocol's own errors, because the boundary passes such an answer on as it is. — *Rejected:* observing inside `Define` alone, for the reasons above. Turning every error of a method that is not a tool call into an internal error, which the first review of T41 showed to be wrong: a client's malformed cursor became an internal error and an error line, and any client could fill the log with them. Letting a tool return an error for the library to phrase, which would put `err.Error()` in front of the model. Reading a refusal from a field the tool reports beside its payload, which would say the same thing twice. — *Where it lives:* `internal/transport/mcp/boundary.go`, `failure.go`, `tool.go`, `labels.go`, `nostore.go`, SPEC 7.4, 12.2, 12.5.

**R215. Every tool says what it does to the parent's file by one choice of three — it reads, it adds, or it overwrites —, its hints follow from that choice, every hint is said outright, and no tool reaches beyond that file.** *(Decided by the author in the planning of T71.2, 2026-10-05, by the hosts' documentation and with no live check; carried out in T71.2.)* A tool's `Spec` names its `Effect`, `Reads`, `Adds` or `Overwrites`: `readOnlyHint` is true for a tool that reads, `destructiveHint` true for one that overwrites and false for the rest, and `openWorldHint` false for all; `idempotentHint` is the tool's own, as before. A tool with no title, or that names no effect, is not served, and every description says what the tool changes in the profile's file in the adult's Drive, or that it changes nothing. The tools, and what each does — the words ChatGPT's justifications of the hints are to be written from (T71.4):
- **Read:** `get_profile`, `get_progress`, `read_progress`, `get_package`, `read_task`. Each reads the profile's file — the profile's tool and the two of the progress also search for it and read its folder's name, to say where it is — and changes nothing, a damaged file included (R214). Each call leaves a line in the service's own log, as every call does: the service's record, not a change to anything of the parent's.
- **Add:** `next_task`, `submit_task`, `submit_answer`. Each writes to the profile's file only what adds to the record of the lessons — a request opened; an attempt spent, and the task once accepted; an answer, with the ratings and counts it moves — and never replaces what the adult set or deletes a state of the file. Two of them also close something as they add, which a reader could take for an overwrite: `next_task`, asked for another task while one is on the card, records the one left as skipped, the record growing by an entry; and `submit_answer`, given an answer to a task whose seal can no longer be opened, takes the task off the card and records that a new one is owed (R101). Neither loses anything the adult or the child set, and `request_id` names the request a task is written for, an identifier a later call passes back. `submit_task` is the one tool that is not idempotent: each call spends an attempt.
- **Overwrite:** `save_profile`, `edit_profile`. Each replaces what the adult set — the fields given —, and `save_profile` with `start_over` or `restore` replaces the whole profile, the old file kept in Drive (R120, R214). What they replace is gone from the profile, which is what both hosts call destructive.

— *Why:* Claude's directory policy asks every tool for a `title` and the hint that applies, read-only for a tool that only reads and destructive for one that modifies or deletes data, and Claude runs a read-only tool without asking and always asks before a destructive one (CL-27 in `docs/catalogs.md`). ChatGPT asks for all three hints as explicit booleans: read-only only for a tool that changes nothing, destructive for any overwrite or deletion, even through an indirect side effect, and false only for additive writes, and not open-world for a bounded private account (GPT-30); and it asks that side effects never be hidden (GPT-32). The MCP specification defines `destructiveHint: false` as additive updates only. One choice for each tool, which both hints follow from, keeps them from disagreeing, and refusing to serve a tool that has not made it keeps a new tool from going out with the defaults, which say that it writes, may destroy and reaches the open world. — *Rejected:* a live check in Claude and ChatGPT before deciding, which the author left out; marking the lesson's three tools destructive because Claude's word is "modify", which would have Claude ask before every answer a card sends and every task the model hands in, though nothing they write is lost — the MCP and ChatGPT definitions read them as additive; two flags, read-only and destructive, which could be set against each other. — *Consequence:* Claude asks before `save_profile` and before `edit_profile`, so the form and the choice of a topic on a card may wait for the person to allow them; what Claude does with a destructive tool a card calls is seen only after the release (SPEC remark 65). Claude's "modify" is wider than additive, and the lesson's three tools follow the narrower definitions; that is said here for a review that reads them otherwise. A test lists every tool with its title and all four hints as booleans, and two hold the effects to what the tools do over the stand-in Drive: the five reads ask Drive for no change in seven states of the file, a damaged one and a full history among them, and the lesson's tools delete no state of the file and leave the adult's settings as they were. — *Where it lives:* `internal/transport/mcp/tool.go` and the specs of the ten tools; `internal/transport/mcp/effect_test.go`, `profile_test.go`, `endpoint_test.go`; `internal/app/app_test.go`; SPEC 7.1 and remark 65.

**R216. The server tells hosts what it is and where its card may run and lead: `serverInfo` names the site and its icon, the widget's resource carries ChatGPT's own sandbox keys, and the domain's token for ChatGPT is served from a setting; `securitySchemes` on the tool itself waits for T63.** *(Decided in the planning of T71.2, 2026-10-05; carried out in T71.2.)* `serverInfo` gains a `description`, the site's origin, `MATHTRAIL_SITE_URL`, as its `websiteUrl`, and one icon, `<site>/assets/favicon.svg`, `image/svg+xml`, of any size, as R191 left to this task. The widget's resource, where it is listed and where it is read, gains `openai/widgetDomain`, the service's public origin, and `openai/widgetCSP`, with `connect_domains`, `resource_domains` and `frame_domains` empty, as `ui.csp` has them, and `redirect_domains` the site's origin alone; `ui.domain` stays unset. A copy of the page another server serves — the drawings of `cmd/drawings` — names neither origin. `MATHTRAIL_OPENAI_CHALLENGE`, at most 512 characters of visible ASCII with the white space around it dropped, is served as it is, in `text/plain`, at `/.well-known/openai-apps-challenge`, held to the pace of an address with the documents; with none set, the path is not served. T71.6 sets it through `settings`, as any value that is not a secret. The endpoint is not built without the two origins. — *Why:* ChatGPT asks for a sandbox origin of the plugin's own, "unique per plugin" (GPT-45 in `docs/catalogs.md`), and Claude makes its own and draws an error in place of the card for any `ui.domain` it did not make (CL-38): `openai/widgetDomain`, which ChatGPT's reference calls an alias of `ui.domain`, serves the one without breaking the other. The card's links lead to the site's pages alone (R186), and ChatGPT opens a destination its widget's `redirect_domains` names without a warning first (GPT-47). The token is how ChatGPT's submission checks the domain, as plain text at that address on the MCP host or a parent domain (GPT-08); a setting keeps it out of the repository and lets a copy set its own. The SDK's `Tool` has no `securitySchemes` field in v1.8.0, ChatGPT's list of submission errors names nothing of it, and a call made without a sign-in begins one by its `401` either way (GPT-48, SPEC remark 20). — *Rejected:* `ui.domain` answered per host, told apart by the client's name, a second answer to keep for a value the alias carries; the token as a file of the site at `mathtrail.app`, the eligible parent domain, which GitHub Pages would serve as `application/octet-stream` and which the check is not documented to take; a type that wraps the SDK's `Tool` for a field no host is known to need yet. — *Consequence:* whether ChatGPT takes the alias in place of `ui.domain`, and the token, is seen at the upload (T71.6, §10 of `docs/catalogs.md`). A link ChatGPT opens to the site may carry a `redirectUrl` it appends, which the site's pages read past. — *Where it lives:* `internal/transport/mcp/server.go`, `widget.go`; `cmd/drawings/main.go`; `internal/config/config.go`; `internal/transport/http/router.go`; `internal/app/container.go`; SPEC 7.1, 8.1, 8.10, 9.1, 10.1, 11.2 and remark 20; `docs/self-hosting.md`.

**R217. No result carries a moment, an age or the version of the instructions.** *(Decided in the planning of T71.2, 2026-10-05; carried out in T71.2.)* `last_answer` loses `answered_at`, and so do the latest answers of the progress; a request handed back loses `age_seconds`, and its words "since N seconds ago"; the package loses `instructions_version`. The identifiers a later call passes back — of a request, `req_…`, and of a task, `tsk_…` — stay, and so do the links to the parent's own file. A test takes a lesson through the tools and holds every result, its words and its payload, to no field of the kind, no date written with its time, and not the version itself. — *Why:* ChatGPT's guidelines turn away "session IDs, trace IDs, request IDs, timestamps, or logging metadata—unless they are strictly required to fulfill the user's query" (GPT-33 in `docs/catalogs.md`), and the model needs none of these: no card shows a time, the words of a request handed back say what to do whatever its age, and the version is the service's to log, which it does with every task from its own content. The guide to an MCP server asks for "stable identifiers in structured results so later tools can refer to the same records", which is what the ids are. — *Rejected:* keeping the age for the model to judge a request by: the service judges it, by the window. — *Consequence:* claim C007 of the research program, that the version travels with every package, no longer holds; `research/` changes it under its own plan. The profile's file keeps every moment it had. — *Where it lives:* `internal/transport/mcp/payload.go`, `progress.go`, `nexttask.go`, `metadata_test.go`; `content/package.go`; SPEC 7.3 and remark 38; `docs/architecture/03-flows.md`.

## Sign-in and permissions

**R03. Client registration is CIMD, with DCR kept as a fallback.** The authorization server metadata declares `client_id_metadata_document_supported: true`, and the server fetches the document itself over an HTTPS `client_id`; `/register` (RFC 7591) is still supported for backwards compatibility. — *Why:* as of 2026-07-28 DCR is formally deprecated, and Claude showed up live with CIMD (`client_id: https://claude.ai/oauth/mcp-oauth-client-metadata`) without ever falling back to DCR; the ChatGPT documentation also recommends CIMD, though that has not been verified live. — *Where it lives:* T07, T47–T49.

**R04. Insufficient permissions on a tool return HTTP 403 with a `WWW-Authenticate` header.** The `_meta["mcp/www_authenticate"]` field in the call result is sent in addition, for ChatGPT's sake, but it cannot be the only mechanism. — *Why:* the live run in T04: on a 403 Claude went to `/authorize` for the wider scope and retried the failed call by itself, with no human involved; a tool that answered only through `_meta` looked to it like an ordinary error. — *Consequence:* the model never sees the step-up — in its context there is a single successful call — so its account of the authorization state is unreliable and only the server log can be trusted. The same rule applies to every acceptance run. — *Where it lives:* T47–T49.

**R82. The MCP endpoint stands behind a sign-in from the start: one development account on a developer's machine, nobody on a deployment until the real sign-in.** *(Since R123 a bearer credential that is a name signs a request in as an account of that name. Since R222 one account signs in on a deployment without Google: the demo account, by the reviewers' password.)* `mcpserver.NewHandler` refuses to be built without a `SignIn`, the function that decides whose account a request acts for. There are two. `DevSignIn`, switched on by `MATHTRAIL_DEV_AUTH`, lets every request in as the account `dev`, with a credential or without one. `NobodySignsIn` is the SDK's own `auth.RequireBearerToken` with a verifier that refuses every token, so every request is answered `401` with `WWW-Authenticate: Bearer scope="mcp"`. The signed-in account travels from the sign-in to the tool in the request's context, under a key of the MCP package, and from the tool's frame onwards as an argument. — *Why:* the author's decision. A deployed service must not serve tools to nobody in particular, and until the real sign-in (T49) it can issue no token. So it refuses everything, in exactly the shape in which it will later refuse a missing token. The account does not ride in the SDK's `TokenInfo`, the SDK's own carrier. Only `RequireBearerToken` can put one there, and it demands an `Authorization` header. A custom connector in Claude sends none without OAuth, so a live check through a tunnel with the development sign-in would get `401` on every call. — *Consequence:* the live check of the widget moves from the deployed service to a developer's machine behind a tunnel — in T43, where the first tool that draws a card arrives, since T42 has none to draw one with (the author's decision) — and the deployment's smoke check asks for the `401`. T49 replaces `NobodySignsIn` in the container with `RequireBearerToken` and its real verifier, plus a step that turns the verified `TokenInfo` into the account. A verifier error that wraps neither `auth.ErrInvalidToken` nor `auth.ErrOAuth` is answered `500` with its text as the body, so its errors must wrap one of the two. R80 kept the credential out of the context for the store. Here the context carries the account only across the one gap that has no argument to carry it — from the HTTP layer into the protocol library — and the store still takes the account as an argument. — *Rejected:* an open endpoint whose tools refuse a request without an account, which would answer the protocol's methods from a deployment to anybody. And the development sign-in as a stub verifier behind `RequireBearerToken`, which needs the header above. — *Where it lives:* `internal/transport/mcp/signin.go`, `internal/app/container.go`, `justfile` (`run`, `ci-smoke`), SPEC 9.4.

**R41. `seal` hands out a key ring, not an interface.** `seal.NewKeyRing` returns the concrete `*seal.KeyRing`, against the convention that a constructor returns the interface its package offers. — *Why:* there is one way to seal a value and there will be one; the package is pure computation with no I/O, so a caller under test runs the real sealer and gets a real ciphertext, which is a better test than a fake that returns a fixed string. The convention exists so that mockery always has something to generate from, and here there is nothing worth faking. — *Consequence:* the configuration checks the two keys by building a ring and dropping it, and the container builds the same ring again at start. That is two derivations of a few subkeys once per process, and it keeps the rule for a key in one place — the ring — while the configuration is the one that can name the variable a refusal is about. — *Rejected:* an interface with a single implementation, which would be a name for the same thing and one more file of generated mocks nobody calls; a lighter check of the keys beside the ring, which would be a second rule for what a key is. — *Where it lives:* `internal/infra/seal`, `internal/config/config.go`, CLAUDE.md ("Interfaces").

**R42. The domain asks the catalogs for what it needs, and the content answers.** *(Its "the grade crosses that line and the level does not" is reversed by R79: a level is part of the ladder the ratings run on, so `rating` defines the levels, and `tutor.Catalog` asks which levels a topic is taught at and the traps of a topic at a level. The rest stands.)* A domain package that needs the catalogs declares an interface of its own — `tutor.Catalog` is the first, with three methods that speak in identifiers — and `*content.Content` satisfies it by gaining the matching accessors. No domain package imports `content`. — *Why:* the dependency rule of CLAUDE.md, applied to a package that is data rather than infrastructure: the rule of section 3 needs three facts about the catalogs and nothing else, and saying so in three method signatures is both the smallest contract and the honest one. It also buys a test: the rule's own cases are written against a catalog of three topics and four traps, which states what is being tested, where the four hundred and fifty reference tasks of the binary would only obscure it. The grade crosses that line and the level does not — the domain knows what school year a child is in, and what years are grouped into stays with the catalogs. — *Rejected:* importing `content` from the domain, which is simpler by one interface and would have made every rule test depend on the shipped content; an adapter type in between, which would be a name for the same three methods. — *Where it lives:* `internal/domain/tutor`, `content`, and the same shape is what T32, T35 and T36 use when they need the catalogs.

**R43. The tool for mocks is kept and the mocks are not written.** mockery v3 is pinned, configured and checked by CI, and `.mockery.yaml` lists no packages: an interface is tested against the real implementation where that implementation does no I/O, and against a hand-written fake of a few lines where a test has to force an outcome. — *Why:* the two interfaces the service has are pure computation, and for both a mock would prove less. `profile.Sealer` exists so that a sealed task is tied to one child and one task and refuses to open anywhere else; a mock returning a fixed string cannot show that, while the real key ring is six lines to build and shows it exactly. `tutor.Catalog` exists so the rule can be run against a catalog of three topics written out in the test, which states what the test assumes in one place — as expectations on a mock, the same assumption is scattered across every case and has to be reassembled by the reader. — *Rejected:* generating mocks for those two now, which would weaken both sets of tests; removing mockery along with its recipes and its CI job, which would have to be put back the first time an interface earns one. — *Consequence:* the CI job runs against an empty config and says so in a second; the recipes notice `packages: {}` themselves. — *What would change it:* the first interface whose real implementation reaches outside this process, and that is the storage. The profile lives in the parent's Drive (T50, T51), so `store.Storage` is the first interface here whose real half needs a network, a token and somebody else's service to answer. Two things about it do not change: its implementations are still held to one contract test set, and the Drive one still runs against `httptest`, because that is what shows the two are interchangeable and a mock shows nothing of the sort. What changes is the tools above it — a test of what a tool does when a write lost the revision race, when the file was deleted, or when the parent took the grant back has to make that happen rather than wait for it, and `EXPECT().Save(...).Return(store.ErrConflict)` says which case it is in one line where a hand-written fake grows a flag per outcome and a fake Drive has to speak Google's error bodies. That is when `.mockery.yaml` gains its first package and the committed mocks start being checked. — *Where it lives:* CLAUDE.md ("Tests"), `.mockery.yaml`, `justfile`, `.github/workflows/ci.yml`.

**R104. The authorization server lives in `internal/transport/oauth`, and the fetch of a client's document in `internal/infra/cimd`.** The authorization server is a transport of its own beside the MCP server: its metadata, its registration endpoint and the reading of a client identifier now, and authorize, consent, callback, token and revoke with T48 and T49. The one request the service makes to an address a stranger chose — a client's metadata document — is an infrastructure package, as Drive and the sandbox are. The router in `internal/transport/http` mounts the server's handlers the way it mounts the MCP endpoint, and `transport/oauth` calls `infra/cimd` and `infra/seal` directly. — *Why:* the author's decision. CLAUDE.md put the OAuth endpoints in `internal/transport/http`, RUN.md named a package `internal/oauth`, and note 1 of 01-context left the choice to whichever task came first. The router's package is the whole HTTP surface in one function; five endpoints of a sign-in beside it would give it a second reason to change. A fetch that has to hold off SSRF is infrastructure with rules of its own, tested against a fake server of its own, and it would be lost among handlers. Nothing sits between an OAuth endpoint and the seal or the fetch that is not the endpoint's own logic, so an interface in between would only forward calls. — *Rejected:* one package `internal/oauth` for all of it, which mixes the network fetch with the handlers; the handlers inside `internal/transport/http`. — *Consequence:* `transport/oauth` is the first transport package that imports `infra`. The layout in CLAUDE.md names both packages. — *Where it lives:* `internal/transport/oauth/`, `internal/infra/cimd/`, `internal/transport/http/router.go`, `internal/app/container.go`; CLAUDE.md (Layout); `docs/architecture/01-context.md` note 1; RUN.md (T47).

**R105. A client's document is fetched as narrowly as it can be, and kept only when it is one.** *(Narrowed by R126: port 443 alone, the first four addresses of a name dialled, the lookup, the dials and the connection itself ended with the attempt, and at most 32 redirect URIs.)* The client identifier has to be an HTTPS address with a host and a path of its own, with no credentials, no fragment and no dot segment, before anything is resolved. The name is resolved by the service, and it is refused whole when any address it stands for is not public: loopback, private, link-local with the metadata server, unique-local, multicast, unspecified, reserved, carrier NAT, or a way into IPv4 from IPv6. The addresses that were checked are the ones dialled, never the name again, each in an equal share of the attempt's time, with no proxy and no connection kept once the document is read. No redirect is followed, only a `200` is read, and only to 64 KB. An attempt has 10 s, and one more follows a network error, a timeout or a `5xx`, never anything that would fail the same way again. A document is a client's when its `client_id` is the address exactly, it names the client and lists where to send the parent back, and it carries no shared secret. Only such a document is kept in memory: for its own `max-age` less the `Age` a cache on the way gives it, never under 5 minutes nor over 24 hours, and at most 256 of them. Of the redirect URIs a document lists, the ones no sign-in may use are dropped rather than failing the document. — *Why:* it is the one request made to an address a stranger chose. A name that points one record at a public server and another at the metadata server is a private name. Dialling the checked address is what makes a second lookup unable to rebind it. The HTTP client lets a dial outlive its request, so no deadline reaches a dial on its own: without a share each, an address that swallows every packet — a broken IPv6 route — would take the whole attempt and leave a working one untried. A connection to a host a stranger chose, kept for a fetch that will not come, only holds a socket. The retry is the spike's: the first name lookup a fresh process makes can outlast an attempt. The rules of the document are the CIMD draft's own, and it forbids keeping a failure or an invalid document. A document that serves a mobile app beside the web lists a custom scheme no sign-in here can use; dropping that one lets the web sign in, and it could never be matched anyway. — *Rejected:* an allow-list of client domains, which any new host would first have to get onto — the consent screen is the human check (02-auth); following a redirect to an address checked in turn, since the document has to be at the address the client is; accepting a name when one of its addresses is public; keeping failures; refusing a whole document over one redirect URI it cannot use. — *Consequence:* a fetch ends in one of eight outcomes, which the `cimd_fetch` line names; a native client whose document lists only a custom scheme cannot sign in, as the MCP specification wants. — *Where it lives:* `internal/infra/cimd/`; `internal/transport/oauth/clients.go` and `redirect.go`; SPEC 9.3 and 12.2.

**R106. Registration keeps nothing and registers public clients only, and the server's metadata is written by the service.** *(R218: a client that names itself by its document is matched on any port of an address on the parent's own computer.)* A registration (RFC 7591) is sealed under the purpose `client`, bound to the issuer, and handed back as the `client_id`; nothing else is kept anywhere. It takes JSON of at most 64 KB, one to five redirect URIs a sign-in may use, and a name of at most 100 characters. Whatever the client asks for, it is registered as a public client for the code and refresh grants and answered `201`, saying so; a refusal is `invalid_redirect_uri` or `invalid_client_metadata`, `413` past the size. The authorization server's metadata is a type of the service's own, with no `jwks_uri`; the resource's metadata is the protocol library's handler with an hour's cache on it, served under the resource's own path and not at the root. A `redirect_uri` matches a registered one byte for byte. The refusal of `/mcp` names the resource's metadata. — *Why:* RFC 7591 lets a server register other values than the ones asked for, and this server has one kind of client, so saying which it registered lets more clients connect than refusing would. Binding to the issuer keeps an identifier from opening anywhere but where it was issued. The bounds keep an identifier short, since it rides in every authorization request's URL. The library's `oauthex.AuthServerMeta` writes `"jwks_uri": ""` (note 6 of 02-auth). The MCP specification asks for exact matching. RFC 9728 has the document at the root describe the host, which is not the resource: a strict client, the protocol library's own among them, refuses the resource's document there, and the MCP specification asks a server for the challenge or one well-known address, which it has. — *Rejected:* the same document at the root as well, as SPEC 9.1 first had it (the author's decision); refusing a registration that asks for a secret; the library's type for the server's metadata; accepting any port on a loopback redirect URI, which RFC 8252 asks for and the MCP specification does not (SPEC remark 49). — *Consequence:* the protocol library's own client registers here and reads both documents, which is what the tests use as a host. R82's refusal now names the resource's metadata, and the smoke check after a delivery asks for it and for the document it names. — *Where it lives:* `internal/transport/oauth/` (`register.go`, `metadata.go`, `clients.go`, `redirect.go`), `internal/transport/mcp/signin.go`, the `ci-smoke` recipe; SPEC 9.3, 9.4 and remarks 47–50; `docs/architecture/02-auth.md`.

**R107. Google's sign-in is a package of its own, and an ID token counts only with Google's signature.** `internal/infra/googleauth` sends the parent to Google and trades Google's code for the grant: `golang.org/x/oauth2` builds the address — `openid` and `drive.file`, `access_type=offline`, `prompt=consent`, PKCE S256, a `nonce`, the sealed request as the `state` — and exchanges the code with the client secret in the body, within 10 s; `github.com/coreos/go-oidc/v3` checks the ID token that comes with the grant against Google's published keys, RS256, the issuer, our client as the audience, the expiry with a minute of skew, and the package holds the nonce to the request's and asks for a `sub`. The keys are kept and fetched again when a token names one not seen yet. What goes wrong ends in one of five refusals — the code refused, the service's own client refused, Google not answering or asking for a pause, the identity not proven, no refresh token — and none of them carries a token or Google's words; the refused client is an error of ours in the line of the callback, since every sign-in fails the same way until the deployment is fixed. The authorization server takes it as an interface, `SignIn`, with the constructor returning it; `googleauth/googletest` stands in for Google over HTTP for the tests of both packages: a token endpoint that holds a code to its request, and keys it signs with. — *Why:* the author's decision on the signature: 02-auth asks for the ID token to be verified against the JWKS, and although the token arrives over a connection the service opened itself — which OpenID Connect lets a client trust without a signature — a signature is what keeps a wrong endpoint, a proxy or a test's stand-in in production from naming whoever it likes. go-oidc is a small, widely used verifier, Apache-2.0, that fetches keys again the way the specification asks; a JWT verifier written here would be protocol code from memory. The exchange is a network call and has to be faked in the authorization server's tests to force each failure, so it stands behind an interface. — *Rejected:* trusting the token without a signature, as the channel allows; `google.golang.org/api/idtoken`, which pulls in the whole of Google's API client; a hand-written check of the JWT. — *Consequence:* two dependencies, `go-oidc` v3.21.0 and `go-jose` v4.1.5, in the license list; `just run` without Google's client serves the consent screen and stops at a page after it. — *Where it lives:* `internal/infra/googleauth/`, `internal/app/container.go`; SPEC 9.3 and 11.2; CLAUDE.md (Layout); RUN.md (T48).

**R108. The user identifier is derived in `seal`.** `KeyRing.UserID(account)` is the first 16 characters of base64url of HMAC-SHA256 over the account — `google-sub:` and Google's `sub` — under a subkey of the current key derived with HKDF from the context `mathtrail/v1/user-id`, which names no purpose. The callback computes it and puts it in the code; the `sub` goes no further. — *Why:* 02-auth derives it from the key ring "like any other purpose", and the key never leaves `seal`: the subkey is derived when the key is read, as the purposes' are, and a caller gets the identifier and never the key. It is not a purpose because nothing is sealed with it. — *Rejected:* a key of its own in the configuration, a second secret to rotate; handing the subkey out of `seal`. — *Consequence:* a rotation changes the identifier of the next sign-in, as 02-auth accepts. — *Where it lives:* `internal/infra/seal/userid.go` and `key.go`; SPEC 9.3.

**R109. The sign-in's pages are the authorization server's own, and the site shares their tokens.** *(Since R157 the site's typeface is its own, Onest; the tokens still give it the stack to fall back on, its spaces and its widths, and the sign-in pages keep the system font.)* *(They are drawn in English alone, from one dictionary, since R139. The site's colours are its own since R142: it still ships the tokens and loads them first, for its fonts, spaces and widths, and its pages are drawn by `web/src/site/` since R143.)* The consent screen and the page a sign-in stops at are `html/template` pages embedded in `internal/transport/oauth/pages/`, with a stylesheet of their own, drawn in English or Russian by `Accept-Language` from one flat dictionary per language there; a test holds the two to the same keys and the same slots, and the service refuses to start when they differ. The design tokens are `internal/widget/tokens.css`, the draft's file with a comment added, and `widget.Tokens()` gives it to the pages, which inline it before their own styles. Every build of the site ships the same file as `assets/tokens.css`, its templates load it before `style.css`, and `style.css` has no tokens left: it reads the draft's names, `--text`, `--page`, `--border`, `--space-*`, `--font-sans`. A dictionary wording names its slots, `{client}`, `{host}`, `{terms}`, and the code fills them with text set apart, a link or plain text, so the template escapes everything. — *Why:* R94 put the tokens beside the widget so all three readers take one file, and RUN.md asks the site to move onto it in this task, so that the site and the consent screen cannot differ in colour before T66. The pages belong to the authorization server as the widget belongs to the MCP server; the widget's dictionaries in `web/locales/` are for twenty-two languages and a browser, and SPEC 8.9 narrows these pages to two. Filling slots in code rather than in the dictionary keeps a translator from writing markup and the template from trusting it. — *Rejected:* the site keeping its own tokens until T66; the pages in `internal/widget` or `content/`; `template.HTML` wordings with markup inside. — *Consequence:* the site's colours become the approved design's. The render package imports `widget`. — *Where it lives:* `internal/transport/oauth/pages.go` and `pages/`, `internal/widget/tokens.css` and `widget.go`, `web/src/site/Layout.tsx`, `web/scripts/prerender-site.ts`; SPEC 8.9 and 9.2.

**R130. The sign-in's pages choose their language by the widget's rule, reading `Accept-Language` an entry at a time.** *(Superseded by R139: the pages are in English alone, and read no `Accept-Language`.)* Of the languages the browser names, the pages take the one it wants most that they are written in, the first written of two it wants alike; a tag counts for the language it names, in the script it names or its language is usually written in, and for no other; a request that asks for neither is answered in English. Each entry of the header is read on its own, and one that does not read — a language nobody registered, a weight that is no number or lies outside 0 to 1 — is passed over; and no more than the first 32 entries are read. The choice is a function of the header alone, not of the pages. — *Why:* the pages chose with the CLDR matcher of `x/text`, which answers Kazakh, Kyrgyz, Belarusian, Armenian, Azerbaijani, Mongolian and others in Russian with high confidence: a language the browser never asked for, where SPEC 8.9 says English and the widget, by R129, says the same. And `language.ParseAcceptLanguage` refuses a whole header for one unknown tag, so `ru, xx` was answered in English. Read an entry at a time, the header loses that function's bound on its size, and the page a sign-in past its pace is shown reads it too, after the pace has refused the request: a header padded to the size a request may carry would cost that page a parse of every entry, so the entries are counted. — *Rejected:* keeping the matcher and documenting its neighbours, which makes the pages and the widget answer the same browser differently, and Russian to a reader who may not want it. — *Where it lives:* `internal/transport/oauth/pages.go`, SPEC 8.9, RUN.md (T54).

**R139. The sign-in's pages are in English alone.** *(Supersedes R130.)* The consent screen and the page a sign-in stops at are drawn in English whatever language the browser reads, with `Content-Language: en`, and the consent screen points to `/en/terms/` and `/en/privacy/`. Their words are `pages/en.json` alone: the Russian wording is gone, and nothing reads `Accept-Language` any more. A test holds the words to every one the pages ask for and to no other, and a browser that asks for Russian first, and for English not at all, is shown both pages in English. — *Why:* the author's decision (2026-09-30), closing О-51 before the dictionaries of T59 were written. The consent screen is what a parent agrees to, and the terms and the policy it points to prevail in English where a translation of them disagrees (О-50): the page that takes the agreement speaks the language the agreement is kept in, in one wording, and the page a sign-in stops at goes with it. The widget's languages are another matter, and stay. — *Rejected:* keeping `en` and `ru` (R130), whose one stated reason, a policy in a single language, no longer held; the pages in the widget's twenty-two languages now, twenty-two machine wordings of an agreement, pointing at a policy not yet translated; the pages following the policy into each language as the site gets it. — *Consequence:* reading `Accept-Language` an entry at a time, and the bound on how many entries are read, go with the choice they served, and so do the fuzz test of the header and the snapshots of the Russian pages. — *Where it lives:* `internal/transport/oauth/pages.go` and `pages/`, `testdata/pages/`; SPEC 7.5 and 8.9; PRODUCT О-51; the notes of [01-context](architecture/01-context.md).

**R110. What a sign-in carries, where its form may lead, and a sign-in without the Drive.** *(Extended by R126: the screen shows the path of the address beside its host, and a name with three marks at most on a letter; an address on the parent's own computer is asked about every time; the cookies carry the `__Host-` prefix.)* *(Amended by R224: a sign-in Google answers without the Drive shows the parent a page that asks them to go back and tick its box, and the client hears `access_denied` only when they cancel.)* The request in flight carries the digest of the `client_id` and how the client is known rather than the identifier, and so does the code, which carries no random id (SPEC remark 54). The browser remembers at most the newest 20 approvals, each for 180 days. The consent screen's policy lets its form lead to Google's origin and the client's, besides this server (remark 53). A grant without `drive.file` is `access_denied` to the client, with a description that asks the parent to sign in again and allow it. A server with no Google client configured shows the consent screen and stops at a page instead of going on, remembering no approval. The screen shows the redirect host in its ASCII form and the client's name without controls or invisible format characters, each isolated from the text around it. — *Why:* the author's decision on `drive.file`: a sign-in that cannot keep the file is no sign-in, and the parent, not a failure, took the permission away. An identifier this server issued carries its whole registration, and both the request and the code ride in an address. A cookie has room for a few kilobytes, and a family uses a few hosts. A browser holds the redirect that answers a form to the form's `form-action`. A lookalike letter of another script in a host, or a mark that turns text around in a name, would let a stranger's client read as a familiar one on the one screen that is to tell them apart. — *Rejected:* a sign-in that goes on without the Drive and fails at the first tool; `server_error` for it; the full `client_id` in the request and the code; `form-action 'self'` alone, which the browser would enforce against the redirect to Google. — *Consequence:* the exchange of a code (T49) compares the digest of the `client_id` it is given; whether a host shows the description of `access_denied` is for T53 (remark 52). — *Where it lives:* `internal/transport/oauth/` (`authorize.go`, `consent.go`, `callback.go`, `flight.go`, `approvals.go`, `code.go`, `pages.go`); SPEC 9.2, 9.3, 12.2 and remarks 52–54; `docs/architecture/02-auth.md`.

**R111. What the code scan reports about the sign-in is what the sign-in is for, and is answered rather than silenced.** *(Narrowed by R126: a refusal goes back to a registered address only once this browser has approved the client there.)* The sign-in drew six alerts. `go/unvalidated-url-redirection` at the redirect back to the client: an authorization server answers by sending the parent to the client's redirect URI (RFC 6749 4.1.2), and the one it sends them to is a URI the client registered, matched byte for byte before it is sealed into the request, and read back at the callback from a value only this server seals, bound to its issuer. `go/request-forgery` at the fetch of a client's document: fetching the address a client names itself by is what a Client ID Metadata Document is, and `/oauth/authorize` is where it now happens; the fetch is R105's. `go/log-injection`, four times, at the sign-in's lines: what in them comes from a request is a host, written in ASCII by `hostOf` with anything else escaped — and `url.Parse` refuses a control character in a host before that — and words this server sealed itself. Every other field is a closed word, an error of ours, or the cause of a failed exchange with Google — Google's error code, or the words its ID token was refused in — which Google's answer chose and a caller cannot; the JSON encoder escapes whatever a field carries (R50). The six are dismissed as false positives, citing this entry. T49 moved the lines of every step into one function, `ended`, and added the lines of the token endpoint and of a revocation, whose `user` is read from a code or a token this server sealed: the same two kinds of path — the host of a request this server sealed, and words it sealed itself — and the three alerts they raised on `ended` are dismissed the same way. R127 added the `limit_hit` line of a renewal pace, `limited`, whose `user` is read from a refresh token this server sealed and whose `limit` is a closed word; its alert is dismissed the same way. — *Why:* the barriers CodeQL knows are a comparison with a constant, a regular expression, a host held to a list, a line break replaced and `%q`; an exact match against a list the client registered, a value sealed under the service's key and a host held to ASCII are none of them. The evidence is the tests: an address the client never registered, or its own with a slash added, stops at a page (`TestARequestWithNoTrustedAddressStopsAtAPage`); whatever an authorization request or Google's answer carries, a redirect leads to Google or to the registered address with the issuer, and nowhere else (`FuzzAuthorize`, `FuzzCallback`); a request changed on its way does not open (`TestOnlyARequestOpensAsOne`); a host in another script, or one carrying a mark that turns text around, is written in ASCII, and whatever an address is, its host is written in printable ASCII (`TestAHostIsWrittenInASCII`, `TestAHostThatTurnsTextAroundIsWrittenEscaped`, `TestHostsHoldTheirProperties`); the fetch refuses every address that is not public (`internal/infra/cimd`). — *Rejected:* a regular expression over the redirect URI, and `%q` or a line break replaced in the log fields, which would silence the scanner and defend against nothing the code does not already refuse (R50, R76); dropping the hosts from the lines, as R76 dropped the path — SPEC 12.2 asks for them, and a host written in printable ASCII can carry no line break and no mark that reorders a line — what else of a caller's it spells, a quote or an angle bracket, the JSON encoder escapes like any other text. — *Consequence:* the CodeQL check of a pull request that adds such a path stays red until its alerts are dismissed by hand, with this entry as the reason; a new alert on this code is answered the same way only when its path is one of these. — *Where it lives:* `internal/transport/oauth/flow.go`, `events.go`, `redirect.go` (`hostOf`), `authorize.go`, `flight.go`, `token.go`, `revoke.go`; `internal/infra/cimd/fetch.go`; SPEC 12.2.

**R112. The resource lets in the access tokens the authorization server issued, through the protocol library's check and a reader of ours.** `mcpserver.BearerSignIn` replaces `NobodySignsIn` in the container: it is `auth.RequireBearerToken`, with the resource's metadata named in the challenge, the one scope required and a clock skew of a minute, around a verifier that asks a `TokenReader` — a function from a token to the account it signs in and the time the token ends. The authorization server's `Account` is that reader: the token opens as an access token this issuer sealed, has not ended with a minute of skew, is for the canonical resource by `oauthex.MatchesResource`, and grants the server's scope; the account is its user identifier, reached with the Google access token it carries. A refused token is answered with the library's own `invalid token`, and leaves an `auth_bearer` line whose reason says why — `unreadable`, `unknown_key`, `expired`, `audience` or `scope`. The account travels from the check to the endpoint inside the library's `TokenInfo`, under a key the MCP package alone knows, and from there in the request's context as before (R82). `NobodySignsIn` is gone. — *Why:* R82 left T49 exactly this: `RequireBearerToken` with a real verifier, and a step that turns the verified token into the account. A reader that returns the account and the token's end keeps the key between the check and the endpoint in one package: the authorization server needs no word of the MCP package, nor the MCP package a word of how a token is sealed. The library holds a token to its end by its own clock as well, so it is given the same minute the reader allows, and the two cannot disagree. The reason of a refusal is what tells an expired token from a key retired too early, and it belongs in the log and not in an answer to whoever sent the token. — *Rejected:* the Google access token in the `TokenInfo` as a string, which would print wherever the information is printed, where the account keeps it out of every print; a key of `TokenInfo.Extra` shared by the two packages; the library's check without a skew, which would refuse a token in the minute the reader lets pass. — *Consequence:* a deployment lets in whoever signed in, and `ci-smoke` still asks only for the refusal of a request with no token. The resource cannot yet tell a grant Google ended from a live one: the Google token inside is not asked about until Drive is (SPEC remark 55). — *Where it lives:* `internal/transport/mcp/signin.go`, `internal/transport/oauth/access.go`, `internal/app/container.go`; SPEC 9.3, 9.4, 12.2 and remarks 55, 57; `docs/architecture/02-auth.md`.

**R113. The token endpoint serves public clients by the rules of OAuth 2.1, and tells a host whether signing in again is what mends a refusal.** *(The minute an access token ends before the Google token inside it, and the five minutes under which that token is renewed, are three minutes and seven since R117. A verifier of another shape than RFC 7636 gives one is `invalid_request` since R126.)* `/oauth/token` takes a form of at most 64 KB, each parameter once but for the resource. A client names itself with `client_id` in the body; one that tries to prove itself in an `Authorization` header is answered `401 invalid_client` with a `Basic` challenge, and may then send the same request as a public client. The code grant holds a code to its minute — refused, too, when dated more than the skew ahead — to the digest of the `client_id`, to the verifier of its challenge, and to its `redirect_uri` and `resource` when the request names them; OAuth 2.1 drops the first from the exchange and lets a client leave it out. The refresh grant holds a refresh token to its end with a minute of skew, to its client, to a scope no wider than its own — the new tokens keep the whole of it — and to its resource. Both answer a new access token and a new refresh token for the session the code or the token carried: the access token ends in 15 minutes or a minute before the Google token inside, whichever comes first, the refresh token in 30 days, and neither later than 90 days after the sign-in; past those, a refresh is `invalid_grant`, within the skew too. The Google token inside is renewed at Google first when less than 5 minutes of it are left, and a refresh token Google gives in place of its own is carried on. A grant Google renews no more is `invalid_grant`; Google not answering, or giving a token too short to issue one with, is `503 temporarily_unavailable`; Google refusing the service's own client is `500 server_error`, an error in the line; a server with no Google client is `503` for a session that needs Google. `googleauth` renews a grant through `x/oauth2`, whose refresh keeps the refresh token Google did not replace, and a refusal of the grant is `ErrGrantEnded`. — *Why:* a public client can prove nothing but PKCE, and the protocol asks for `401` and a challenge of the scheme a client tried in a header; the library's own client, named by its document, tries a `Basic` header first and the body after a refusal, which is how it finds out (SPEC remark 56). OAuth 2.1 requires a server to accept a `redirect_uri` in the exchange and to hold it to the code's. The MCP specification has a client name the resource in the token request. `invalid_grant` is the refusal on which a client drops its tokens and signs in again — the protocol library's client does exactly that — and a failure the host may retry must not be told as one, or a moment of Google's unavailability would sign every family out. — *Rejected:* a refresh token narrowed to the scope a refresh names, which RFC 6749 6 forbids — a new refresh token keeps the scope of the one it replaces; a token issued within the 90 days that outlives them, which would stretch the one bound on a stolen refresh chain; taking `client_id` from a `Basic` header with an empty password, which would spare the library's client one refusal and give the endpoint a second way to name a client (remark 56 keeps it open for T53); requiring `redirect_uri`, which OAuth 2.1 removed; asking Google at every refresh whether the grant lives, a call every 15 minutes per family where the design asks for one an hour; `server_error` for Google not answering, which would read as a fault of ours. — *Consequence:* a code can be exchanged more than once within its minute, and a refresh token presented once still works until its own end — nothing records their use (02-auth, threats). — *Where it lives:* `internal/transport/oauth/token.go`, `grants.go`, `form.go`; `internal/infra/googleauth/grant.go`, `exchange.go`; `googletest`; SPEC 9.1–9.3 and 12.2, remark 56; `docs/architecture/02-auth.md`.

**R114. A grant ends at Google, with the Google token inside the token a host presents, and only when a host asks.** `/oauth/revoke` (RFC 7009) takes `token` and `client_id` in a form. It reads a token of either kind whatever its own end, and holds it to the digest of the `client_id` — a token issued to another client is `invalid_grant`. It asks Google to end the grant with Google's refresh token when the token is a refresh token, and with Google's access token when it is an access token; Google ends the whole grant either way. An access token whose Google token inside has ended can end nothing, and is answered `200` with nothing asked of Google, its line `ignored` as `google_token_ended`; a token that is none of this server's is answered the same. Google not answering is `503 temporarily_unavailable`, the grant not ended; Google refusing the request for another reason is `500`, an error of ours. `googleauth.Revoke` sends the token in the body, and tells Google's `invalid_token` — a token Google no longer honours — apart as `ErrNotHonoured`: nothing was ended, the host is answered `200`, and the line says `ignored` as `not_honoured` rather than revoked. The callback does not end the grant of a parent who unticked the Drive permission: it is left as Google gave it. — *Why:* a server that keeps nothing cannot take back its own tokens, so ending the grant at Google is the only revocation there is (02-auth). A token past its end may still carry a Google refresh token that is alive, and an access token past its Google token's end cannot say whether its grant lives, so a line that called it revoked would be wrong. RFC 7009 answers an unknown token with `200`, since a client can do nothing about it, and a client that is not the token's with an error, and has a client retry after a `503`. A token in an address may be kept by any hop on the way. Google ends every grant of a parent's at a service together, so ending the grant of a sign-in the parent only declined would disconnect the chats they had already connected. — *Rejected:* revoking only the tokens this server issued, which changes nothing; `200` for a token of another client; refusing a token past its end; ending the grant without the Drive at the callback, which SPEC remark 52 had left for T49 and which would cut the parent's other connections. — *Consequence:* ending a grant disconnects every chat the parent connected, and the host's own tokens keep working until the next renewal asks Google (SPEC remark 55). A refresh token always ends the grant; an access token does while the Google token inside it is honoured. A grant without the Drive stays in the parent's Google account until they remove it there or it goes unused for six months. — *Where it lives:* `internal/transport/oauth/revoke.go`, `callback.go`; `internal/infra/googleauth/grant.go`; SPEC 9.1, 9.3 and 12.2, remark 52; `docs/architecture/02-auth.md`.

**R117. An access token ends three minutes before the Google token inside it, so that a request it lets in reaches Drive in time.** *(The deadline its consequence leaves to T51 is R118's: the Google token's end travels with the account, and no call to Drive is started past it. The wait for a slot of the sandbox, which the margin below leaves open, is bounded since R122.)* The margin is three things added up: the minute the resource takes a token past its end, the minute by which the clock that checks a token may run behind the one that issued it, and the minute a tool goes on calling Drive after its request is let in — at most six calls of ten seconds each, or five beside two runs of a solver of two. A Google token's life is counted from the moment it was asked for, not from the moment the answer came, so the end the service keeps is never later than Google's. The Google token inside is renewed at either grant when less than seven minutes of it are left — the margin and four minutes more — so that an access token is good for four minutes at least whenever Google's own lasts seven. — *Why:* the author's decision, on the review of T50. With a minute of margin and a minute of allowance, the last requests of a token met a Google token that had ended, and Drive answers an ended token as it answers access taken back, with a `401`: the store reads that as `ErrAccessRevoked`, and the new sign-in T51 gives it would send a parent through Google again for nothing. The margin belongs where a token's life is set, so that every token the resource takes carries a Google token that outlives the request — and a Drive `401` then means the grant is gone. — *Rejected:* telling an ended Google token from a revoked one at the store, which Drive's answer does not say; renewing the Google token at the resource while a request waits, which needs Google's refresh token inside the access token, and 02-auth keeps it out so that one stolen buys minutes and no renewal — the refresh grant renews it ahead of time instead; a narrower allowance at the resource, which is what keeps a token from being refused by an instance whose clock runs ahead of the one that issued it. — *Consequence:* at fifteen minutes of access and an hour of Google, what a host sees changes only for a token issued near the end of a Google token, and it is renewed sooner. The minute of a tool is a fact about the tool rather than a setting: a request cut off, or answered late, does not stop it (R83), so a call to Drive given longer than its ten seconds, more calls to a tool — the retries T51 brings — or a long wait for one of the solver's slots, which nothing times, move the margin with them. What would hold the line whatever a tool takes is a deadline: the Google token's end carried with the account, and no call to Drive made past it — left for T51, which decides what a Drive `401` is told as. — *Where it lives:* `internal/transport/oauth/grants.go` and its tests (`property_internal_test.go`, `refresh_internal_test.go`, `token_internal_test.go`, `host_internal_test.go`); `internal/infra/googleauth/exchange.go`, `grant.go`, `grant_test.go`; SPEC 9.1, 9.3 and remark 55; `docs/architecture/02-auth.md`; R113.

**R123. The development sign-in names an account after the bearer credential it is given, and refuses nobody.** *(Amends R82.)* A request whose `Authorization` is `Bearer <name>` — the scheme in any case, the name 1 to 64 letters, digits, dots, dashes and underscores — acts for the account `dev-<name>`, which has a profile of its own in the store in memory and a pace of its own. Any other request acts for `dev`, as every request did before: one with no credential, and one whose credential names nobody. The switch is the same variable, and a deployment refuses it as before. — *Why:* the author's decision, taken while planning T52a. The load scenarios need several children on one instance — "one user past the limit, the others still served" cannot be shown with one account — and the real sign-in cannot issue a token without Google. A name read from the header every client already knows how to send is the smallest change that gives them, and a second switch would be a second thing to keep refused. A credential that names nobody still signs in as `dev` because `just run-signin` and `just run` serve the same address: a client that kept its token from the first would otherwise be refused by the second, try to sign in again where no Google client is configured, and stay locked out until the token was forgotten by hand. — *Consequence:* no client that was let in before is refused. Every name is a key of the account's pace, which keeps at most 4,096 of them, and of the store in memory, which lives as long as the process and only on a developer's machine. The name is written into lines as the `user`, which its alphabet makes safe. The service's own access tokens are sealed strings of hundreds of characters, so none of them is ever taken for a name. — *Rejected:* refusing a credential that names nobody with `401`, the first design, for the lock-out above — the author's decision after the review of T52a.1; minting access tokens of the real sign-in with the sealing key, which leads to Drive and needs Google; a header of the service's own, which no host's connector would ever send; a separate switch for many accounts. — *Where it lives:* `internal/transport/mcp/signin.go`, `internal/transport/mcp/devsignin_test.go`, `internal/app/container.go`, `justfile` (`run`); SPEC 9.4 and remark 24.

**R126. The security review closes what it found in the sign-in, the registration of clients, the MCP endpoint and the storage, and names what it leaves.** The code at rest was read by area, each finding checked against it, and a real one closed with a test that fails without the fix. What changed:
- The MCP endpoint refuses a body that is a batch of messages, `400`, before the protocol library reads it. The library still takes a batch from a request that names no version, and every message of one was a call of its own — logged, answered, held to the paces one by one — inside one request the paces saw once: a megabyte of them was fourteen thousand calls and as many lines of the log, and forty such requests at once held five gigabytes of an instance's memory (R02).
- The token endpoint is held to its address's pace alone. A host renews its tokens there every few minutes in the middle of a lesson, and the sign-in's pace of the instance is one a flood of anybody's could fill: renewals refused for a quarter of an hour stop every lesson (R121).
- A client identifier is fetched on port 443 alone, the first four addresses of its name are dialled, and the lookup, the dials and the connection itself — its handshake and whatever is read on it — end with the attempt. The HTTP client lets a dial outlive its request, and a server that took the connection and never answered held it, and a goroutine, for as long as it liked. A document lists at most 32 redirect URIs, since what the fetcher reads is what the cache keeps (R105).
- The consent screen shows where the parent goes back to by host and path, a long path cut in its middle so that its start and its end both show — a familiar host may serve pages anybody publishes — and a client's name with three marks at most on a letter, so that it cannot spill over the line naming the address. An address on the parent's own computer — an address of the loopback or the unspecified one, in any form a browser reads as one (`127.1` and `2130706433` are `127.0.0.1`), `localhost` or a name under it — is warned about and asked about every time: its approval is never remembered and never taken as given, since any program on that computer could wait there in the client's name (RFC 8252 8.6; R110).
- A request from a known client, at an address it registered, that the server cannot grant goes back to the client once this browser's parent has approved it there, or when the address is on the parent's own computer, which sends nobody elsewhere, and stops at a page otherwise: anybody may register a client at an address of their choosing, and a refusal sent there at once made a link to this server a way to send a parent anywhere (RFC 9700 4.11.2; R111).
- The sign-in's cookies are `__Host-mt_csrf` and `__Host-mt_consent`, on the path `/` the prefix asks for: a site under the same domain could otherwise set cookies of those names in their place and carry an approval past the consent's check.
- A verifier is 43 to 128 letters, digits and `-._~`, as RFC 7636 makes one, or the exchange is `invalid_request` with the reason `invalid_pkce`, whatever the challenge (R113).
- A task is sealed again when its answer is recorded, against the letter it was given, and an answered task opens only against that. An `answered` written into the file by hand told the answer before the child had given one (R101).
- A task's texts are held to what a card shows — the question to 1,500 characters, an option to 200, the hint to 500, the solution to 2,000, an explanation to 500; what is sealed is not escaped for a web page; and no profile is written past 1 MiB, the most a store reads back. A task whose solution was a megabyte of `<` was written, read back as damage and rolled back, and each time it pinned one more revision of the history.
- Every count of the profile is held below 2^30, and a file reads only with room in each for one more: a count set by hand to the most a number holds left a file that read and could never be written.
- The day's first write reads the file once more after making room in its history: the listing and the deletions ran between its check and its upload, and a write that landed during them was written over (R119).

What stays, and why:
- The seal of a task is bound to the student's id and the task's, which the file carries. Copied with both into another profile it opens there, so whoever can read a family's file and has an account of their own can learn the answer to the task on its card. Accepted by the author: the file is in the parent's own Drive, and whoever reads it is somebody the family let in.
- The search for the profile has no `'me' in owners`: `drive.file` lists only files this app created for the account or the account opened with it, and the service opens none; the clause would also lose a profile the parent moved into a shared drive. T53.2's live check shares a marked file from a second account to confirm it.
- An error made from the file quotes what it read — a topic, a date, a letter — and none leaves the process: a tool's failure is a closed sentence, its line a closed word, and no span records it.
- A file whose `schema_version` is past this build's, by hand or after a rollback, was answered "try again in a few minutes" for good and refused a new start. Settled by the author in R128.
- A restore uploads the earlier revision's bytes as they were, fields this build does not know among them, and the next write drops them; with one version of the file there is nothing else to drop.
- A refresh token stays good to its own end, and one issued an hour ago carries a Google token past its end, so every use of it renews at Google. Held to its address's pace alone, the token endpoint lets somebody with an account and many addresses make as many calls to Google as the addresses allow, where the instance's sign-in pace capped them before. The author chose to cure it at once: a pace for each account on renewals at Google, R127.
- The address a pace keys on is the last `X-Forwarded-For` entry. A request with two such lines is part of T53.2's live check. Every family behind one host address shares that address's pace at the token endpoint (remark 60), and the revocation endpoint stays under the sign-in's pace of the instance: a disconnect a flood holds back ends the grant at Google all the same when the parent removes the service there.
- What the fetcher keeps is at most 256 documents of 64 KB, 16 MB in all. A registration's sealed identifier grows with the characters JSON escapes, which only the registrant carries; a document's `Content-Type` is not checked, since the exact `client_id` it has to carry does that work; and the fetch can reach the service's own public endpoints, by GET and with no credentials.

— *Why:* the task asked for every finding to be closed or recorded with its reason, before the service meets a real family. — *Rejected:* the built-in review of a change, which reads a diff, not the code at rest (the author's decision); `TLSHandshakeTimeout` alone, which leaves a late HTTP/2 connection open; a page in place of the redirect after a decline — the host would never hear the parent said no, and the screen named where they would go; 04-profile's 256 KB as the ceiling of a write, which would leave a file edited by hand to 300 KB readable and never writable; pruning for size, since nothing grows past its cap; one bound on a count at both reading and writing, which leaves the file at the bound readable and unwritable; `'me' in owners` before the live check. — *Consequence:* the consent screen's snapshots show the path; an approval a browser remembered under the cookie's old name is asked for once more; the first write of a day costs one more download; a profile of an earlier build whose answered task was sealed without its letter is told nothing again, and the answer it recorded stays counted. — *Where it lives:* `internal/transport/mcp/batch.go`; `internal/transport/http/router.go`; `internal/infra/cimd/` (`address.go`, `url.go`, `document.go`); `internal/transport/oauth/` (`authorize.go`, `approvals.go`, `flight.go`, `pages.go`, `redirect.go`, `token.go`); `internal/domain/profile/` (`sealed.go`, `answer.go`, `json.go`, `validate.go`); `internal/domain/checks/structure.go`; `internal/store/drive/` (`write.go`, `drivestore.go`); SPEC 5.2, 7.3, 7.6, 9.1–9.3, 10.1, 12.2; `docs/architecture/02-auth.md`, `04-profile.md`, `05-storage.md`.

**R127. Each account's renewals at Google are held to a pace of their own.** The author's decision (2026-09-29), after the review of R126. `MATHTRAIL_RATE_RENEWAL_PER_MIN`, 12 by default and at least 1, is how many times in a minute one account's grants may be renewed at Google through one instance, counted by the user id in the instance's memory like every other pace of R121: a third of it at once, 4, and the rest at its pace. Only a renewal that would call Google is counted — a token endpoint request whose Google token has 7 minutes or more left costs nothing — and one past the pace is answered `503 temporarily_unavailable` with `Retry-After`, the reason `renewal_rate` in its line, with Google not called; the first of a flood writes `limit_hit` naming `renewal_rate`, and the account's user identifier, as the other paces of an account do. — *Why:* a refresh token stays good to its own end, and one issued an hour ago carries a Google token past its end, so every use of an old one renews at Google; held to its address's pace alone since R126, the token endpoint let somebody with an account and many addresses have the service call Google as often as the addresses allowed. A grant needs a renewal about every hour, and a family has a few grants — a chat or two, a device or two — so twelve a minute — four at once, for the hosts of a family that renew together — holds nobody back but a replay. — *Rejected:* the instance's sign-in pace back on the token endpoint, which a flood of anybody's could fill again (R126); counting every request of the token endpoint by account, which would stop renewals that cost Google nothing; a pace by grant, which somebody with many grants of one account would multiply. — *Consequence:* a host refused for the pace asks again later, as it does after Google failed to answer. — *Where it lives:* `internal/transport/oauth/grants.go` (`freshen`), `token.go`, `events.go`, `server.go`; `internal/config`; `internal/app/container.go`; SPEC 9.3, 10.1, 10.2, 11.2, 12.2.


**R218. A client that names itself by its document is sent back to an address on the parent's own computer on any port.** *(Revises R106 and SPEC remark 49 on evidence they did not have; decided in the planning of T71.2, 2026-10-05; carried out in T71.2.)* An address the client's document lists that begins `http://localhost`, `http://127.0.0.1` or `http://[::1]`, written so, matches a requested one that begins the same way and differs from it in its port alone: a port of one to five digits or none, and the rest byte for byte. Every other address, and every address of a client registered here, is matched byte for byte as before. The code still carries the address it went to, port and all, and its exchange refuses another. — *Why:* Claude's documentation for connectors asks for it: Claude Code "is a native client and uses an RFC 8252 loopback redirect on an ephemeral port that varies per session", lists `http://localhost/callback` and `http://127.0.0.1/callback` in its own document, and needs the port ignored for both (CL-34 in `docs/catalogs.md`); RFC 8252 7.3 asks it for the addresses written as numbers. A document is fetched, not registered, and cannot name a port its client learns only as it starts; a registration can, and is made with the port the client has. — *Rejected:* leaving Claude Code unable to sign in, as R106 and remark 49 had it; any port for every client, which a registration does not need; `localhost` in any case, or a name under it, which Claude Code does not send. — *Consequence:* a parent can connect MathTrail from Claude Code. The consent screen is shown for such an address every time and never remembered (R126), so another program on the parent's computer, waiting on another port, gains nothing the screen does not ask the parent about first. — *Where it lives:* `internal/transport/oauth/clients.go`, `redirect.go`; SPEC 9.3 and remark 49; `docs/architecture/02-auth.md`.

**R222. A directory's reviewers sign in with a password of their own, as the demo account, and Google is not asked.** *(О-62, decided by the author in the planning of T71.3, 2026-10-05, as option B of `docs/catalogs.md` §3; it amends R82, SPEC 9.4 and SPEC remark 24.)* Two secrets switch it on together: `MATHTRAIL_REVIEWER_PASSWORD`, and `MATHTRAIL_REVIEWER_GRANT`, the demo account's `sub` and its Google refresh token. With neither, nothing changes. With both, the consent screen offers "Reviewing MathTrail for a directory?", folded under the parent's way in, with a password field. The right password renews the demo account's grant at Google, at the account's pace as every renewal is (R127), and sends the client the code a sign-in through Google ends in. The code is for the demo account, whose user identifier is derived as a parent's, and carries no country; the browser remembers no approval. A wrong password stops at a page, and Google is asked nothing. A revocation of the demo account's tokens ends nothing at Google. The authorization server names the demo account by an identifier under each key of the ring, the current one first, since a session's tokens may be sealed under either while a rotation runs; the container hands those to the lessons, whose lines carry no `learner` for it, so its child is counted nowhere. `just reviewer-grant` captures the grant through the service's own client and callback, which leaves the code unspent; `just reviewer-password` makes a password of 32 random characters; Terraform keeps both secrets empty, mounts each once its version is named, and refuses either among `settings`, where it would sit in the state in the clear. — *Why:* ChatGPT rejects an app whose reviewer meets an extra login step, and Google may put one to a sign-in from another country or device even without 2-Step Verification. A sign-in Google is not asked about cannot meet one, and the demo account keeps its 2-Step Verification, since only the author ever signs in to it. A revocation is skipped because Google ends every grant of an account at a client together, the one the reviewers' sign-in renews among them, and a reviewer disconnects once a review is over. Leaving the demo child uncounted is the author's decision (2026-10-05): its answers are reviewers', or wrong on purpose to fill its progress, and the counts kept for years and the calibration on the page "Research" are about children. — *Consequence:* the deployment has a way in that is not Google, which R82 ruled out. What keeps it safe is what it reaches, the demo account's one file, and that it is off unless its secrets are set. Guessing is held to the pace of an address, 20 a minute, against 32 random characters.
