A leaked password opens the demo profile alone and is changed by a new version; sessions already given go on for up to 90 days, and only ending the grant in the demo account's Google account cuts them short. The sign-in lasts while Google honours the refresh token: six months unused, a revocation in the demo account or a hundred newer refresh tokens end it, and a line at error level and the reviewer's error say so. A reviewer who presses "Allow" leaves an approval in that browser, which then goes straight to Google; the reviewers' instructions say not to. — *Rejected:* option A, a demo Google account without 2-Step Verification, for the check above. A page of its own for reviewers, which no host's sign-in leads to. A mark sealed into the tokens of the reviewers' sign-in in place of the identifier, which would leave the demo account's own sign-ins through Google revocable. Ending at Google the demo account's grants given through Google's own sign-in, and sparing the captured one alone: Google ends an account's grants at a client together (R114), the captured one with them. A mark on `store.Account`, which every call to Drive copies and which no store reads: the lessons are told the demo account once, as they are told the key children are counted under. — *Where it lives:* `internal/transport/oauth/reviewer.go`, `consent.go`, `callback.go` (`googleAccount`), `revoke.go`, `flow.go`, `server.go`, `pages/`; `internal/infra/seal/userid.go` (`UserIDs`); `internal/config/config.go`; `internal/transport/mcp/service.go` (`Parts.DemoAccounts`), `activity.go`, `submitanswer.go`; `internal/app/container.go`; `internal/report/events.go`; `infra/terraform/secrets.tf`, `service.tf`, `variables.tf`, `outputs.tf`; `justfile` (`reviewer-grant`, `reviewer-password`); SPEC 9.1, 9.3, 9.4, 11.2, 12.1, 12.2 and remark 24; 02-auth; [self-hosting](self-hosting.md); [reviewers](reviewers.md).

**R223. The logo is not uploaded to Google's consent screen before the directories publish MathTrail.** *(Decided by the author in the planning of T71.3, 2026-10-05; finding 2 of `docs/live/05-login-drive.md`.)* The app is in production at Google, published on 2026-09-30, with its name, its support address and its three links, and no logo. — *Why:* a logo sends the app to Google's brand verification, which takes days and may ask for changes. The reviewers' sign-in (R222) never shows Google's screen, so a logo would change nothing a reviewer sees, and a verification under way is a risk the submissions do not need. — *Consequence:* a parent signing in sees Google's screen without the logo, as now. After publication the question comes back, with the directories' listings to show for the brand. — *Where it lives:* Google Auth Platform, Branding; [self-hosting](self-hosting.md) §5.

**R224. A sign-in Google answers without the Drive asks the parent again, and the host hears nothing of it.** *(Amends R110; 2026-10-05, after a family's first connection in Claude failed.)* When Google's grant lacks `drive.file`, the callback no longer sends the client `access_denied`. It sends the parent on, with `303`, to a page at an address of its own, `/oauth/drive`, whose address carries the request and no code of Google's — the callback's own address, drawn again by a reload or by a return from Google's screen, would spend Google's code a second time and end the sign-in. The page says: Google's box for the Drive was left unticked, the child's progress is kept in that one file, MathTrail sees no other, and the parent is to press "Back to Google" and tick the box about their Google Drive files — worded in their Google account's language, and quoted on the page as Google words it in English, "See, edit, create, and delete only the specific Google Drive files you use with this app". Its form is answered by `/oauth/consent`: "Back to Google", the decision `again`, goes on to Google and approves nothing, since the page names no client; "Cancel", the decision `cancel`, sends the client `access_denied`, as declining on the screen does. The request goes on readied for a way through Google of its own — a new verifier and nonce, and ten minutes from the page — under the host's own `state`, challenge and redirect URI, so the host sees one sign-in, which ends in a code. The browser keeps the sign-in's cookie for those ten minutes, so the first way still finishes for a parent who returns to Google's screen with the browser's Back. The callback's line is `auth_callback` with the outcome `retry` and the reason `no_drive`, at info level; going back leaves an `auth_consent` line with the outcome `again`, and cancelling one with `denied` and the reason `no_drive`, so that the sign-ins the box still loses can be counted apart from the clients a parent declines; a refusal at the page is an `auth_reject` at the step `drive`. The consent screen now asks the parent to tick the box for Google Drive on Google's screen, and so do the steps of connecting in the README and on the site. — *Why:* asked together with `openid`, `drive.file` is a box Google shows unticked, and a parent who presses "Continue" past it grants `openid` alone (Google's granular consent; the same report in google/google-api-javascript-client#754). Both live runs met it on their first sign-in. On 2026-10-05 a family's first connection in Claude ended in Claude's own words, "mathtrail wasn't connected. If you didn't cancel the sign-in, ask your mathtrail admin for access", and the second went through. The log names the first `auth_callback` `denied` `no_drive`, at 22:06:03 UTC, and the second `ok` a minute later, with no consent screen before it, since the approval was remembered. A host shows no description of `access_denied` (SPEC remark 52), so the parent heard nothing of the box and was sent to an admin there is none of. R110's reason, that the parent took the permission away, holds for a box the parent unticks, not for one they never saw ticked. — *Rejected:* the consent screen's warning alone, which a parent may skim, and which a browser that approved the host before never shows. Asking Google for `drive.file` without `openid`, for which Google's documentation shows no box: no ID token would prove who signed in, and every user identifier is derived from the one it carries. `enable_granular_consent=false`, which Google honours only for clients made before 2019. `include_granted_scopes`, which leaves the box as it is. Going back with the first way's lifetime, verifier and nonce, which would leave a parent who took most of ten minutes too few, and send Google a second request with the first one's proof. A new cookie for the way back, which would stop a parent who returns to Google's screen with the browser's Back at a refusal. The consent screen's `allow` for "Back to Google", which would remember an approval of a client the page does not name. The page drawn at the callback's own address, for the reload above. — *Consequence:* a parent who leaves the box unticked goes through Google twice while the host waits, and the grant of the first way, `openid` alone, is left at Google as R114 leaves it. Each way back gives the request ten minutes more, so a parent who keeps leaving the box unticked keeps one request alive; every round is a sign-in at Google in the same browser, and nobody else can take it on. Older lines name the same event `auth_callback` `denied` `no_drive`. — *Where it lives:* `internal/transport/oauth/drive.go`, `callback.go`, `flight.go` (`readyForGoogle`), `authorize.go`, `consent.go`, `server.go`, `pages.go` (`showDrive`), `pages/drive.html`, `pages/en.json`; `internal/transport/http/router.go`; `internal/app/container.go`; SPEC 8.9, 9.1, 9.2, 9.3, 12.2 and remark 52; `docs/architecture/02-auth.md`; README; `site/content/*/index.yaml`.

## Tools and environment

**R05. There is no separate tool-versions file.** Each exact version is pinned where the tool is installed: an `ARG` in `.devcontainer/Dockerfile`, a literal in `devcontainer.json` and `devcontainer-lock.json`, a module version in `go.mod`, an exact version plus a lockfile for npm, a commit SHA for GitHub Actions. — *Why:* one source of truth per tool; a separate versions file would have to be kept in sync with those same places by hand, and the build reads the versions from them anyway. — *Rejected:* a shared versions file; checksum verification beyond what `go install` already does. — *Where it lives:* CLAUDE.md ("Exact versions only"), T01.

**R47. The ceiling on copying a value is moved once rather than excepted at every site.** `gocritic`'s `hugeParam` fires at eighty bytes, which is exactly what the five option texts of a task weigh, and they are passed by value from the rule through the sandbox and into every test around it. `.golangci.yml` moves the ceiling to eighty-eight. — *Why:* the five options are one value on purpose — nothing between the rule and the interpreter can change the task under whoever asked about it, and the array states in the type that a task has exactly five options. Writing the same exception out at eight call sites would say less than one line saying it once. Eighty-eight is where the next value up in this repository sits, so `Answered`, `Student` and `Corridor` keep the exceptions they already carry and nothing else changed verdict. — *Rejected:* a `//nolint` at every site, which is the noise above; switching the check off, which would stop catching the values it was put there for; a slice instead of an array, which saves fifty-six bytes and loses both the count and the promise that nobody else holds a reference to it. — *Where it lives:* `.golangci.yml`.

**R81. SonarQube for IDE is in the development environment, offered a binding to the SonarCloud project, and the image installs no Java for it.** `SonarSource.sonarlint-vscode` is pinned in `.devcontainer/devcontainer.json` like every other extension. It analyses the files open in the editor. `.sonarlint/connectedMode.json` offers to bind it to the SonarCloud project, so that the editor uses the project's rules and exclusions; only the organization, its region and the project key are in the repository, and each developer enters their own token through the extension. Its telemetry is off. Its Linux x86-64 package carries its own Java runtime, pinned by the extension's version. There is no such package for arm64, and the image installs no Java for it, by the author's decision. On arm64 the extension therefore asks to download a runtime of its own at whatever version is current. That runtime is not pinned, and a developer who declines it gets no analysis on arm64. Once bound, the extension also takes its analyzers and rules from the SonarCloud project rather than from its own version. — *Why:* an issue shows up while the code is being written, not in SonarCloud after a merge. This does not contradict R31: that entry keeps out of the image the scanner that analyses the repository for the quality gate, while this is an editor extension that analyses open files and sends its findings nowhere. — *Rejected:* a pinned Java runtime in the image for arm64, by the author's decision. — *Where it lives:* `.devcontainer/devcontainer.json`, `.sonarlint/connectedMode.json`.

**R124. The load tool is a Go module of its own that drives the service through the protocol library's client, at a pace and with percentiles from vegeta.** `tools/load` has a `go.mod` of its own: `github.com/MathTrail/mathtrail-standalone/tools/load`, the product required through `replace ../..`, and `github.com/tsenart/vegeta/v12` at v12.13.0 as its one dependency of its own. Every call goes through the protocol library's own client — one session for each child of a run, opened with `server/discover` at 2026-07-28, each call with a deadline of its own — and the tool writes no message of the protocol by hand. A session the library finished at an older version, because the service refused the newest, counts as no session. vegeta gives the constant pace (its `Pacer`, driving a runner that sends every call in a goroutine of its own when it is due, so that a slow service gets more calls in flight rather than fewer) and the percentiles (its `Metrics`, one for each kind of answer, every call timed from the moment it was due). Children sign in through the development sign-in by names of their own (R123). An answer is told apart by what it says: a refusal by the status and code of its payload, a failure by its first sentence, the busy sandbox and a pace by theirs — and a pace that holds back a message other than a tool call by the words of its error of the protocol — and a call that got no result by the status it got, the protocol's error or the network; a call the run itself abandoned is told as stopped. The report is Markdown: the verdict first, every kind of answer with its share and times, and what one unit of the run's work cost as the platform bills it — every moment at least one call was under way, in steps of a tenth of a second, times the vCPU and the GiB of the instance, and every request the service answered, the library's own among them — against the free tier's 180,000 vCPU-seconds, 360,000 GiB-seconds and two million requests a month, naming the allowance that runs out first. What fails a run outright is what the service should never do under any load: a status of 400 or more, told by its status whatever error of the protocol rode on it, a call with no answer, an answer that belongs to another call, another version of the protocol, a failure of its own other than the busy sandbox, an error of the protocol, a kind of answer nobody has seen before — and whatever the scenario expected and did not see. A run stopped before its end is reported for the part that ran, with no verdict and held to no expectation, since it did not get to meet them, and ends as one that could not run. The tool's checks run on every pull request: `just load-lint` — `go mod tidy -diff`, `gofmt`, the linter with the root's configuration, the license check against the root's list, `govulncheck`, and the versions it shares with the service held to the service's — and `just load-test`. Dependabot proposes vegeta alone for the module: what vegeta brings moves with it, and `govulncheck` names any of it that turns out vulnerable. — *Why:* the author's decisions, taken while planning T52a. A module of its own keeps vegeta and what it brings out of the service's dependencies and out of `THIRD_PARTY_LICENSES`. It imports the product so that its tests run every scenario against the real service in the same process, and the tasks a lesson hands in are proven against the real checks, on every pull request; and so that the protocol library it speaks is the service's own, at the service's version. The library's own client, rather than requests of the tool's making, is what keeps the protocol written once: 2026-07-28 asks for headers and fields of its own on every request, and answers in a stream. Billing is counted from the time requests are under way because that is what request-based billing charges for: the allotted processor and memory while a request is served, not what the process happened to use of them. — *Consequence:* a change to the product's `go.mod`, a dependabot pull request among them, is followed by `just load-tidy`, or `just load-lint` and `just load-test` fail; that is the price of the tests against the real service. The requests a report counts are the tool's own, and a chat host sends more for every call it makes (SPEC remark 62). The billed time is counted from the client's side, and includes the network between it and the service. The runner has no user yet in this part; the scenarios that load the sandbox and the limits use it (T52a.3), and the container, the cgroup and the cold start come with T52a.4. — *Rejected:* raw HTTP through vegeta's `Attacker`, which would have the tool write the protocol's messages and read its streams itself; a load tool in a second language (k6, Locust), which would write the protocol a second time; a module without the product, whose tests would meet only a fake of the service; closing a session a call found broken at once, which would fail the calls of the same child still under way on it — it is kept until the child is done. — *Where it lives:* `tools/load/`; `justfile` (`load`, `load-test`, `load-lint`, `load-tidy`); `.github/workflows/ci.yml`; `.github/dependabot.yml`; `sonar-project.properties`; `CLAUDE.md`; `CONTRIBUTING.md`. — *Extended in T52a.3:* three attacks, each followed by a recovery — the probe answering within the timeout of a call, then a child new to the service walking a lesson of one task — reported as a run of its own. `saturation` hands in the costly `loop` at a constant rate, shared among ten children, each hand-in for a request nobody opened: the sandbox runs both runs and the service writes nothing, and a hand-in turned away as busy is the service keeping its answers in time rather than a failure. `limits` runs a greedy child and a greedy address past their paces beside three children walking lessons and three quiet addresses fetching the document once every five seconds, within theirs, and reports the four apart, each held to what its pace should have done to it; the children's calls carry no address, since only the sign-in and its documents hold an address to a pace. The greedy address fetches at the greedy child's rate — three a second, not the two first planned — so that one rate drives both. `adversarial` hands in the costly solvers one after another. Each spends 90 to 99 % of the step ceiling it is sized to (`-steps`, the service's default unless told otherwise) and points at the one right option, so both runs go whenever the clock lets the first one end: a loop, appends, sums of a million, the widest tuples the ceiling holds taken widest first, pairs, and a product of a hundred thousand numbers only the clock stops. Pairs are there because a tuple takes memory of its own that the budget does not charge: at the default ceiling one run of them holds 824 MiB, against 442 for the widest tuples the same budget buys — two products of nineteen and one of eighteen — and 381 for the two alone, which is what SPEC 6.6 measured, and with four runs at once the process reached 5.6 GiB where nothing limited its memory (SPEC remark 63, the author's decision). On the development machine the `loop` takes 0.1 s, not the 0.4 s planned, so saturation fills four slots only on a smaller processor, the one T52a.4 runs it on; the sums of `helper` run past the clock of two seconds, which stops them before the budget does. An attack that sends no call, or examines no hand-in, has tested nothing and fails; a recovery's lesson that a pace held back is walked again, by a new child after a pause twice the last, for as long as the probe may take to answer, since a pace shedding what is left of an attack is the service doing as it should. The addresses of `limits` are what the tool writes into `X-Forwarded-For`, which the service reads as the address only with nothing in front of it: against the deployed service they are all the tool's own, and the quiet ones are held back with the greedy. — *Extended in T52a.4:* a run starts the service itself, from an image, in a container of an instance's size — one vCPU and 512 MiB by default, and no swap, as the platform gives none — its port published on this machine alone, the development sign-in on, a sealing key made for it passed in the command's environment rather than on its command line, and a label by which a container a killed run left behind can be found. Its cgroup is the one the line `0::` of its process's `/proc/<pid>/cgroup` names, as docker's own driver and systemd's alike write it, read every quarter of a second; cgroups of the first version, or a daemon whose processes this machine does not see, leave the instance unmeasured rather than unrun. A container is stopped in one order: the last count of its cgroup, which goes with it; its state, which says whether it ended before; the stop, with the platform's ten seconds; its log, for the lines of a panic and every `solver_run`; and its removal, always. What the instance spent while a run went on is given to a run that went on alone — the cgroup counts the instance as a whole — and the life of an instance to the last run it served, which it fails for a panic, a kill for memory, an end of its own, or a peak above `-memory-ceiling`. The scenarios get their services from one function type, `Launch` — an image, a URL, or in the tests the service in the same process — and `adversarial` gives every variant an instance of its own; `cold` starts the service five times, and only from an image. A service that never answered its probe fails the run as a hard fact, and a container that could not be started at all is a run that could not go on. `just load <scenario>` builds the image first unless it is given `-url` or `-image`. Measured on the development machine at the default size: a lesson spends 0.017 CPU-seconds a task against the 0.36 billed; saturation at three hand-ins a second fills no slot, since the loop takes 65 ms on one processor; `appends` peaks at 436 MiB with four runs at once; and `tuples`, `pairs` and `product` each end the instance for want of memory (SPEC remark 63). A service whose instance ended is not waited for: its recovery fails at once. One that ends as it starts is a service that never came up, and its last lines go with the life of its instance; the docker commands run in a group of processes of their own, so that an interrupt at the terminal reaches the tool, which removes what it started. — *Rejected:* the Docker Engine's Go library, which would bring a large module for the six commands the tool gives; memory read from the service's own process, which is not what the platform counts or kills by. — *Extended in T52b:* an instance started from an image is told what a deployment of its size is told — a solver slot for every whole vCPU, and never fewer than one, and nine tenths of its memory as `GOMEMLIMIT` — unless `-env` names either, and its memory is 1 GiB unless `-memory` says otherwise. Two costly solvers join the others: `sets`, the tables a helper builds, among the defaults of `adversarial`; and `dicts`, dictionaries written in a loop, the memory the language takes by itself where no price reaches, handed in only when it is named. The costly solvers are sized to the price of R125. `just ci-load` runs every scenario against the image, each held to its memory and each report under the command that ran it, and exits with the worst code of them; `.github/workflows/load.yml` runs it every Monday, by hand, and on a pull request that changes the workflow, with the reports as the run's summary.

**R151. The devcontainer carries uv and a pinned Python, for the editor's Python extensions.** *(Decided by the author, 2026-10-03.)* The image copies `uv` and `uvx` from Astral's own image, `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:0.12.13`, pinned by tag and digest — the uv the golden export's image runs with — and that uv installs Python 3.12.14, the interpreter that image runs the export on, under `/opt/uv/python`, with `python` and `python3` in `/usr/local/bin`. uv checks each interpreter it installs against the hash it carries for that build, so the image's digest pins the interpreter too. Where the install goes is set for that step alone, so a Python a person installs later goes into their own home, as uv puts it by default; a download cut short is tried again. — *Why:* the Python extensions the image lists, finding no Python, offered to install uv and a Python into the home directory at whatever versions were current, and a rebuilt container lost them; the author asked for both in the image, pinned by hash. The export is the repository's one Python program, so the editor reads it with the interpreter it runs on. — *Rejected:* uv fetched from its release tarball by version, as this file's other tools are, since the author asked for a hash and uv publishes an image to pin one by; Python 3.14, which the extensions picked, and with which the editor would accept what the export's 3.12 refuses; a Python from `apt`, as R28 rejected it. — *Consequence:* R28's premise, that the image has no Python, no longer holds word for word: it has the editor's, pinned. gcloud stays left out on arm, where nobody here builds yet; its archive could be pointed at this interpreter with `CLOUDSDK_PYTHON` when someone does. — *Where it lives:* `.devcontainer/Dockerfile`, CLAUDE.md.

**R168. The load signs in on a deployed service the way a host does, with accounts a parent signed in, and two scenarios are written for that service.** *(T64.1, 2026-10-04: the author left the way the tool signs in and the accounts it uses to the executor.)* A deployed service refuses to start with the development sign-in, so there the load tool signs in through the protocol library's own sign-in, `auth.AuthorizationCodeHandler` of go-sdk v1.8.0: it registers itself as a public client whose redirect is the loopback of this computer, and the parent opens the address it prints, signs in with Google and allows it on the consent screen. The browser comes back to a listener on 127.0.0.1, or the parent pastes the address it ended up at; a sign-in asked for again in the same run is answered with what the first came to, never with a second address. The tokens are kept in `mathtrail-load/<name>.json` of the user's own configuration directory, readable by the owner alone and written whole through `os.Root`, so that no name leads out of the directory, and only once a session has opened with them; the tool renews them as they run out and writes the renewed ones back, and a renewed token that cannot be written is used all the same, with a word after the run. The way back answers only the sign-in under way, by the state the parent was sent with. Before a run starts, a token that would run out within the run and two minutes more is renewed; a token lasts fifteen minutes at most, so a run longer than that renews in its middle, and the tool says so before it starts. Two scenarios run there: `paces`, the parts of `limits` a deployed service lets a run try — a greedy account, other accounts walking lessons, and the tool's own address past its pace — and `ceiling`, an account failing requests until the day's ceiling of failed generations refuses it while another reads its profile. — *Why:* T64 loads the deployed service, and an account's limits are reached only by an account that signed in. The library's sign-in is the one a host goes through, so the tool proves that path rather than a shortcut of its own, and no protocol code is written by hand. The renewal goes before the run because the token endpoint is held to the pace of the address alone, and `paces` spends that pace on purpose. `limits` cannot run there as it is: its quiet addresses are written in a header the platform writes the real address after, so behind the platform they are held back with the greedy one, and its recovery walks a lesson for a child new to the service, which an account signed in for real never is. The ceiling of failed generations is the limit kept in the profile, which every instance reads, so it is the one that shows whether a limit holds across instances. — *Consequence:* `golang.org/x/oauth2`, already in the service's graph, is a direct dependency of the tool. The order of `-accounts` decides which profile is written: in `paces` the first account only reads, and those after it walk lessons whose profile becomes the load's own; in `ceiling` the first fails its requests, its profile the load's own as well, and those after it only read (`docs/load.md`). An account walks the lessons of `paces` once: its profile keeps every task it was handed, and the load's five come back refused as near-copies until the profile is deleted. The ceiling leaves its account without new tasks until midnight in UTC. Every request of a run comes from one address, so an address beside the greedy one is seen served only from another network, by hand. A signed-in child whose account cannot renew its tokens sends nothing, and its calls are told as unsigned. — *Rejected:* a second deployment with the development sign-in, which the service refuses under the platform on purpose; an access token copied by hand from MCP Inspector, which lasts fifteen minutes and is copied again for every run; `limits` with a branch for a deployed service, which would be a second scenario inside the first. — *Where it lives:* `tools/load/account/`, `tools/load/accounts.go`, `tools/load/scenario/deployed.go`, `tools/load/session/session.go`, the `load-signin` recipe, `docs/load.md`, SPEC 10.5.

## The full checks

**R38. What blocks a merge, and what only reports.** Three checks can turn a pull request red on their own: the coverage of the changed lines, held at 90% by `codecov.yml`; CodeQL with the `security-extended` queries; and Trivy over the runtime image at HIGH and CRITICAL, run through `just ci-image-scan` from its own pinned image so the same command gives the same verdict on a laptop. Everything else stays a reading: SonarCloud as R31 left it, OpenSSF Scorecard as a public measure of how the repository is run, and the total coverage, which moves for reasons a single change does not control. — *Why:* the service is written around children's data, and the bar has to hold without depending on anyone remembering it. Coverage of the patch rather than of the project is what a change can actually be held to: `cmd/sitegen` and `cmd/sitecheck` sat at 19–26% — the first left in T66 and the second leaves in T66c — so a project-wide floor would either be set so low it proves nothing or block work it has nothing to do with. CodeQL follows values across functions and files, which is the one thing the linter's own security checks cannot do, and it is what will matter once sign-in, Drive and the HTTP surface are real. — *Consequence:* Trivy reports a vulnerability of the Go standard library from the version stamped in the binary, without asking whether the code reaches it, so it can go red where `govulncheck` stays silent; the fix is a newer Go in the `Dockerfile`, and anything that cannot be fixed that way goes into `.trivyignore` with its reason. The patch status needs the repository's branch protection to require it, which is a setting rather than a file. A pull request from a fork gets no Codecov upload and therefore no patch status, and so does a run Dependabot starts while the repository keeps no Codecov token among the secrets kept for Dependabot — keeping one there is what gives such a run its patch status. — *Rejected:* a floor on total coverage, for the reason above; Snyk, which needs an account and a token for what CodeQL and `govulncheck` already answer; gating on the Sonar quality gate, which R31 settled. — *Where it lives:* `codecov.yml`, `.github/workflows/codeql.yml`, `.github/workflows/scorecard.yml`, `justfile`, `README.md`.

**R39. Invariants are tested with gopter, and a check is proven by breaking it.** Computation that holds an invariant — the ratings, `seal`, trigram similarity, readability, the solver contract, and the copies the content hands out — is tested by naming the invariant and letting the library hunt for the input that breaks it. The library is `github.com/leanovate/gopter`, and a new check is not called covered until its test has been seen to fail with the check switched off. — *Why:* worked examples prove what somebody already thought of. The shallow copy in `content` is the case in point: three hand-written examples passed while a caller could still rewrite an option inside the binary's own content, and the property that a copy shares nothing finds it by itself. Breaking the check by hand is the same idea applied to the tests: it costs a minute and catches the test that passes because it never ran the code it names. — *Rejected:* `pgregory.net/rapid`, whose API and shrinking are better but whose licence is MPL-2.0 against a list of five permissive ones — a test-only dependency never reaches the shipped binary and `go-licenses` does not even see it, but an exception to the licence rule would have to be explained for as long as the repository exists, and gopter costs ten more lines per test instead; `testing/quick`, which cannot shrink a counterexample, so a failure arrives as a wall of random input; a mutation-testing tool — `go-mutesting` is abandoned and `gremlins` is slow and noisy, and the rule above buys the same thing where it matters. — *Where it lives:* `content/property_test.go`, `go.mod`, CLAUDE.md ("Tests").
**R20. The checks run inside the development image, published to GHCR and pinned by digest.** `.devcontainer/Dockerfile` has two stages: `toolchain` — Go, Node, `just`, `golangci-lint`, `mockery` — and `devcontainer`, which adds the editor tooling, cloudflared, the compose plugin and the Claude Code CLI on top. A first job installs `just` and calls `ci-toolchain-image`, which tags the image with a hash of the Dockerfile, publishes it if nothing is published for that hash yet, and prints the digest to run the checks in; every other job runs inside that digest. `govulncheck`, `gitleaks` and `go-licenses` stay Go programs run straight from their modules at versions pinned in the justfile. Every step of the workflow is a `just` recipe or a pinned action, with one line of wiring to carry the reference between jobs. — *Why:* the author's decision, replacing an earlier arrangement where a script installed tools onto the runner. One description of the environment now serves both a laptop and a pull request; a tag computed from content means a change to the environment needs no second commit to point CI at it; and a recipe rather than a block of shell in the workflow is what the other services in the platform do. — *Consequence:* the first run after a change to `.devcontainer/Dockerfile` pays for one image build; a pull request from a fork cannot publish, so such a change has to reach `main` before a fork can be checked against it. The `image` job stays on the runner, because the docker client reaches the development environment through a devcontainer feature and a plain build does not apply features. A checkout inside a container belongs to a different user than the one running the job, and Go looks for its caches under a home directory the job does not control, so `safe.directory` and the two cache locations are set once for the whole workflow through the environment, where they hold for every step whatever user and home it gets. — *Rejected:* `latest` or any moving tag, which the rule of exact versions forbids; building the image on every run; a copy of the tool versions inside the workflow; a build cache for an image that is only ever built when its one layer has already been invalidated. — *Where it lives:* `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, `.github/actions/just/`, `.devcontainer/Dockerfile`, `justfile`, T18.

**R21. `THIRD_PARTY_LICENSES` is generated from the dependency graph, and the checks compare the two.** `just licenses` rewrites the file; `just ci-licenses` fails when a dependency carries a license outside the permissive list (MIT, BSD-2, BSD-3, Apache-2.0, ISC) or when the committed file no longer matches the graph. — *Why:* the repository owes its readers a current list of what it ships and under what terms (PRODUCT 8), and a list that is written by hand stops being true at the first dependency bump; this is the same contract the generated mocks already have. — *Consequence:* each line links the license text of the exact version in `go.mod`, so a dependency update fails the check until the file is regenerated — one command, and it is what keeps the links pointing at the text that actually ships. The standard library and the runtime are listed too, as `std`, at the Go release `go.mod` names, which the releases are built with (T61): every binary carries them under BSD-3-Clause, whose notice has to travel with a binary, and no module graph reports them. — *Rejected:* a list without versions, which would never need regenerating and would never be exactly true either. — *Where it lives:* `justfile`, `THIRD_PARTY_LICENSES`, T18; the npm side is `web/scripts/licenses.ts`, T42 (R85).

**R85. Every npm package the widget's lockfile pins is held to the license list, and the bundler's CSS minifier is the one exception.** *(Since R157 the npm check also admits the SIL Open Font License, `FONT_LICENSES`, for the site's typeface.)* `just ci-licenses` runs `web/scripts/licenses.ts`, which reads the lockfile and holds every package in it — what the widget is built from and the tools that build it — to the same permissive list as the Go modules, reading each license as an SPDX expression. `lightningcss`, under MPL-2.0, is excepted by its name and its license, together with its per-platform binaries, and only while it stays a development dependency. `THIRD_PARTY_LICENSES` lists, after the Go modules, every package the lockfile needs outside development. `just ci-vuln` holds those same packages to the published advisories, at high and above, with `npm audit`. — *Why:* the author chose Vite 8, which depends on lightningcss and cannot be installed without it. MPL-2.0 is copyleft file by file: it binds lightningcss's own files, not the CSS it minifies, and none of lightningcss reaches what is shipped. R39 declined an MPL-2.0 library for tests because a permissive one did the same work for ten more lines per test; here the alternative was the previous major version of the bundler. Holding the tools to the list too turns "only permissive dependencies" into something a check keeps rather than something a reviewer remembers, which on the Go side `go-licenses` cannot do: it sees only what is linked. The list says "built from" rather than "contains", because the lockfile's production tree is wider than the bundle — the MCP client's Node-only dependencies are in it, and the bundler drops them. `npm audit` is there because nothing else would look: `govulncheck` reads only Go, and Trivy sees no JavaScript inside the Go binary. — *Consequence:* a package moving to a license outside the list fails the check, and so does lightningcss the day it becomes something the widget is built from; a new tool under another license needs a decision of its own. `npm audit` does not ask whether the code reaches a vulnerable function, so it can go red where the widget is not affected; the answer is a bumped package, like any other. — *Rejected:* Vite 7.3.6, whose whole tree is permissive, by the author's decision to take the current major; listing only what the bundle holds, which needs a plugin in the bundler for a list the lockfile already bounds. — *Where it lives:* `justfile` (`ALLOWED_LICENSES`, `NPM_LICENSE_EXCEPTIONS`, `licenses`, `ci-licenses`, `ci-vuln`), `web/scripts/licenses.ts`, `THIRD_PARTY_LICENSES`, T42.

**R22. Nothing checks the wording of the repository.** There is no list of forbidden terms, no secret holding one and no job searching for them. This withdraws one half of the prototype's D36, the half about never naming the contest the project started from; its other half — difficulty as a level from 1 to 5 inside the grade level — stands, and lives in SPEC 1. — *Why:* the author's decision. That was a note about how the research preceding the project was written, not a rule this repository inherits, and a check with a secret list was machinery for a problem that does not exist. — *Where it lives:* nowhere; the rule is gone from CLAUDE.md and from T18.

**R31. SonarCloud reads every branch and pull request, and gates nothing.** A job sends the sources and the coverage profile the tests already measured to the organization the platform's other services report to, and the five measures in the README's second row are what it answers. It runs on the runner rather than inside the development image, because the scanner installs itself and has no business in the environment the code is built in; it takes the coverage as an artifact instead of running the tests a second time; and on a pull request whose run was given no token — one from a fork, which is given no secrets, or one Dependabot starts while the repository keeps no token among the secrets kept for Dependabot — the analysis is skipped rather than failed, since nobody could pass it: what such a pull request changes is analysed once it reaches `main`, where a missing token fails the job as it always did. Automatic Analysis is switched off on the project's side: it reports no coverage and refuses to coexist with an analysis that arrives from a workflow. — *Why:* the author's decision — the same badges and the same organization as the platform's other services, so that a person moving between them reads the same summary of a repository in the same place. The coverage was already being measured and uploaded; this gives it a second reader that also says something about duplication, complexity and the shape of the code. — *Consequence:* a merge is not blocked by the quality gate — the job fails only when the analysis itself fails, and the gate arrives as its own check from Sonar's GitHub app — so blocking on it would be a decision of its own. A third party now reads the sources: they are public and MIT, and neither a child's data nor a secret has ever been in this repository. The project has to exist in the organization and `SONAR_TOKEN` has to be among the repository's secrets, or the job is red. — *Rejected:* Automatic Analysis, which needs no workflow and sees no coverage; running the tests again inside the analysis job, which would double the slowest check for a number that already exists. — *Where it lives:* `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, `sonar-project.properties`, `README.md`.

**R40. Every green `main` publishes signed binaries, and nobody tags anything by hand.** *(Since T62.7 the version is the next patch of the line `release.yml` names, `v0.2.0` the first of it: R149. Since 2026-10-03 the service is delivered after the release, from its tag, and no longer on the push: R154.)* `.github/workflows/release.yml` waits for the checks to pass on `main`, works out the next version — the newest tag with its patch number raised, `v0.1.0` when there are none — builds the server for `linux/amd64` and `linux/arm64` through `just release-artifacts`, writes `SHA256SUMS`, signs every file with keyless cosign, and publishes the lot as a GitHub release whose tag it creates itself. A button with a version field exists for the release that deserves a number of its own. The service itself is still delivered the way it always was: a merge to `main` builds an image and Cloud Run serves it. — *Why:* the author's decision, taken while raising the repository's OpenSSF Scorecard, and shaped by the reference service, which releases from every push to `main` and asks nobody to type a git command. The number it moves is Signed-Releases, but the thing underneath is worth having on its own: the certificate records which workflow, which repository and which commit produced a binary, so "this is the code that was running" stops being a claim and becomes something anybody can check. A side effect earns its keep too — with tags in the repository, `git describe` finally returns a version, so `/health` and the startup log say `v0.1.4` instead of a commit nobody can order. — *Consequence:* a release per merge, which is forty of them before RUN.md is finished; the version is semantic but its patch number counts merges rather than fixes. Signing happens before anything is published, because a release that appears without its signatures scores zero on a check that would otherwise not apply at all — worse than having no releases. The release runs from the commit the checks passed on, not from whatever `main` points at by then, and only when that commit is this repository's own: a pull request from a fork runs the checks here too, and the branch filter matches on the name of the branch the run came from, so a fork whose branch is called `main` would otherwise have had its code built, signed and published under our identity — the job requires the triggering run to be a push from this repository, which is what closes it. — *Rejected:* a tag pushed by hand, which is the manual step this replaces; the reference's own scheme of naming a release after the commit, which fails the questionnaire's `version_semver`; the SLSA generator, whose reusable workflow must be referenced by tag rather than by commit — it would break the rule that every action is pinned by SHA and lower Pinned-Dependencies to buy a stronger provenance format; a third-party action for creating the release, where `gh release create` with the workflow's own token adds nothing to the supply chain; signing the image that actually reaches production, which is the more useful thing and remains open — Scorecard does not look at it, and it belongs with the deployment rather than with a release. — *Where it lives:* `.github/workflows/release.yml`, `justfile`, `docs/openssf-best-practices.md`.

**R149. Releases are numbered in a line the workflow names, the patch counting up within it.** *(Decided by the author in the planning of T62.7, 2026-10-03.)* *(Since 2026-10-05 the line is `0.3`, raised by the author, `v0.3.0` the first of it.)* `.github/workflows/release.yml` names the line, `RELEASE_LINE: "0.2"`, and `just release-version` works the next version out of it: the next patch of the line's newest tag, or the line's first, `v0.2.0`, when none of it is out yet. A line older than the newest release is refused, since it would publish a version below one already out; so is a version already released, or one that is not `vMAJOR.MINOR.PATCH`. A version asked for on the workflow's button is published as asked. The recipe takes its arguments as arguments, never as its text, since the button's version is typed by a person. The line is raised by hand, in the change that deserves a number of its own. — *Why:* the author asked for the release of T62.7 to be 0.2.0: a profile made in the chat and changed on a card is a change a parent meets. Under R40's rule the merge of T62.7 would itself publish `v0.1.58` before anybody could press the button, and every release after a version asked for by hand would follow it only by luck; a line in the workflow makes the merge that raises it publish the line's first, and the rule stays a rule. The recipe can be run and broken on its own, where a script inside a workflow cannot. — *Rejected:* `v0.2.0` asked for on the button after the merge, which races the release the merge starts; the line written in a file of its own, a second place for one number; the newest tag alone, R40's rule, which can only ever raise the patch. — *Where it lives:* `.github/workflows/release.yml`, `justfile` (`release-version`); RUN.md (T62.7).

**R154. The service is deployed from the release's tag, after the release, and the header of a card names the version it runs.** *(Decided by the author, 2026-10-03.)* *(Since R178 a card drawn on a page of the site named no version, the site being published before its commit's release was tagged; since R195 the site is published from the release, and its cards name it.)* *(Since R195 the version is a frame of two parts: the word for it in the card's language, and the number without its "v".)* The header MathTrail opens a card with — the task, the task on its way, the first sign-in and the task that could not be read — closes its line with the version of the build the widget came with, the one it already gives the host in the handshake: `buildVersion()`, or `dev` when the build named none. For that version to be a release's number, the delivery waits for the release: `release.yml`, once it has published a release, calls `deploy.yml` with the release's tag, and the delivery checks the tag out and stamps the image with the release's number, so that the binary, the widget and `/health` all say `v0.2.1`. A push to `main` no longer deploys by itself. Only the commit `main` still ends at once its release is out is deployed: a release of an older one — its checks finished after a newer one's, or were run again — or of another branch is published and deployed by nobody, so the service never goes back to older code under a newer number. `deploy.yml` started by hand still delivers any branch as it stands, named by how far past a release it is. — *Why:* the author asked for the version in the card's header, and chose the release's number over what the build knew. The delivery ran on the push, from a shallow clone with no tags, before the release that numbers the commit existed, so the service named a bare commit — `/health` answered `8ff72f8` — where R40 expected `v0.1.4`. — *Consequence:* nothing reaches the service before the checks have passed on it, where the delivery used to run beside them; a delivery takes as long as the checks and the release before it, and a release that fails stops it. A green commit that `main` moves past before its release is out waits for the next one: if that one's checks fail, the service stays on the code before both until `main` is green again. A release is stamped with its own number rather than whatever `git describe` says, since a commit released twice — its checks run again, or a number asked for on the button — carries two tags. A delivery that failed is started again with the run's failed jobs alone: all of its jobs would publish the commit once more, under the next number. The releases queue for the release job alone, so a delivery does not hold the next release back, and the deliveries queue on their own, as they did on the push. A called workflow's runs belong to its caller, so the README's delivery badge reads the release workflow, renamed "Release and deploy". The layout check measures the header holding the longest version a build gives itself, and the README's pictures show the newest release. — *Rejected:* the commit, which the author declined; delivering every release, which rolls the service back the day an older commit's checks are run again; `deploy.yml` started by the release's completion, which knows only the commit the release's run started on, not the tag it made; the next version worked out in the delivery as well, which races the release and could name a number nobody published; the version handed to the widget by the server in a tool's result, a second path for a value the widget's own build already carries. — *Where it lives:* `web/src/widget/version.ts`, `web/src/widget/CardRoot.tsx`, `web/src/design/thread.tsx`, `web/src/design/mathtrail.css`, `web/scripts/layout.ts`, `justfile` (`web-preview`, `web-screens`, `ci-image-push`), `.github/workflows/release.yml`, `.github/workflows/deploy.yml`, `README.md`, `SECURITY.md`, `docs/self-hosting.md`.

**R161. CI measures the widget's layout in six parts at once, on six runners.** *(Decided by the author, 2026-10-03.)* `web/scripts/layout.ts` takes `--shard 2/6`, the second of six parts of a run: of each engine's pages — a language at a width, listed width by width — every sixth, from the second on, so that the six parts between them measure every page once, no part measures more than one page more than another, and every part measures at every width while there are at least as many languages as parts. `just web-layout` passes it through, and a run with no shard measures every page, as before. The layout job of `ci.yml` is a matrix of six, `Widget layout 1/6` to `6/6`: each is told its part by the matrix and the count of parts by the matrix's size, runs to its end whatever the others find, and keeps its findings and its pictures as `widget-layout-<part>`; `gh run download <run> -p 'widget-layout-*'` fetches them all. — *Why:* on one runner the check took 17 to 20 minutes, while every other check was done in about five, so it alone set how long a pull request waited, and how long a merged change waited for its release and delivery, which wait for the whole of CI (R154). The time is the browser's, not the script's. Nearly all of a page's time is the first drawing of its 45 cards, which one page does on one thread: 4.3–4.8 s a page at four processors, against 0.3–0.5 s for the second look after 125 s. Asking the browser about the cards in parallel moved the time from finding the frames to reading them and saved nothing, and the two engines on one four-processor runner slowed each other by some 40% — 39 s for four pages in both, against 27–28 s in either alone. Sharing the pages out between runners is the lever that divides that time. — *Rejected:* fewer pages — languages, widths or an engine —, which measures less; a larger runner, which is billed even for a public repository; queries to the browser in parallel, measured above to save nothing; parts named in the workflow by language or width, which a language added to the widget would silently fall out of — the parts are counted out of the pages the preview offers, so every page belongs to one part however many there are; the pages listed language by language, which with three widths in six parts left each part a single width, and the pictures, taken at 360 px, to two parts of the six. — *Consequence:* about four minutes a part — a sixth of one runner's twenty, and half a minute to pull the image and install the packages —, below the five the other checks take; the first runs say how close. A run of the checks takes fifteen or so runners at once, of the twenty a free organization is given. None of the six is a check `main` requires, as the one before them was not, so no rule of the repository changes with their names. — *Where it lives:* `web/scripts/layout.ts` and `layout.test.ts`, `justfile` (`web-layout`), `.github/workflows/ci.yml`.

**R207. Under the race detector a test gives a run of the sandbox ten times the service's clock, and the same steps.** `starlarktest.Clock` is the wall clock a test gives one run where the service gives a limit: the same without the race detector, ten times over with it. The service's tests that run programs in the sandbox with its limits take their clock from it: the content package's bench of the reference solvers and the solver templates, the site's examples, the lesson cases of the MCP package, the container the app package's cases build, whose wait for a slot is stretched alike so that it still outlasts a run, and the sandbox's own cases. The research module, a module of its own with checks of its own, is not among them. The steps a run may take stay the service's, and a test about the clock itself sets its own. The bench still reports its longest run against the service's two seconds. — *Why:* under the detector the clock measures the machine rather than the program. On an idle machine the detector costs this interpreter five to seven times the time, and the runner the checks run on shares its four cores with the tests of every package at once. `sets-56-d4-1`, 0.14 s a run under the detector on an idle machine, ran past two seconds there on 2026-09-28 and again on 2026-10-05, on the push of a merge whose tree had passed on its pull request. Run again, the job failed on the sandbox's own case `more sequences than a tuple may hold`: the refusal it expects came after the clock had run out, as the steps `a loop with no end` expects had on 2026-09-23, before that case was made to take either limit. A red `main` holds its release back (R40). The steps hold a program to the service's limits on any machine. — *Consequence:* a case that runs into a limit on purpose, such as `a loop with no end`, may take up to twenty seconds under the detector on a busy runner. Under the detector, which every run of the checks uses, the bench catches a moved clock only once it has moved several times over; the run without it, `just test` and the pre-commit hook, holds the solvers to the service's own clock, and the steps are held on every run. — *Rejected:* re-running the job whenever it fails, which leaves `main` red by chance; taking these packages out of the race detector, against the rule that tests always run with it; retrying a run that timed out, which would hide a program that really is slow; a constant in each package's tests, the same reason written five times. — *Where it lives:* `internal/infra/starlark/starlarktest/clock.go`, `race.go` and `norace.go`; `content/solver_test.go`, `site/examples_test.go`, `internal/transport/mcp/lesson_test.go`, `internal/app/app_test.go` and `internal/infra/starlark/starlark_test.go`.

## Widget build

**R06. The `@modelcontextprotocol/ext-apps` package, version 2.0.0; the library is the reference, not the text of the specification.** *(Raised to 2.0.3 by a patch release, which `web/package.json` pins.)* Where the MCP Apps 2026-01-26 specification disagrees with the library — in `ui/message` and `ui/update-model-context` its `content` field is always an array of blocks, not a single object — we follow the library. — *Why:* its code is the actual runtime the hosts run; the discrepancy was found in T03 while building the widget. — *Where it lives:* T42, `web/package.json`.

**R84. The widget is built by Vite 8 into one page, with Preact and no plugin for it; TypeScript checks its types, Biome formats and lints it, and Vitest tests it in happy-dom.** `web/` is an npm project with exact versions and a lockfile, and its `.npmrc` keeps new versions exact and lets no package run an install script. `vite-plugin-singlefile` inlines the script and the styles, so the page loads nothing. JSX is compiled for Preact from the settings in `tsconfig.json`, which the type checker, the bundler and the test runner all read. TypeScript 7.0.2 compiles nothing; it only checks, in two configurations: `tsconfig.json` gives the widget the browser's globals alone, and `tsconfig.node.json` gives the scripts and the build's configurations Node's, so that code meant for the page cannot lean on something only Node has. The build's configuration is `vite.config.widget.ts`, so that the site's can stand beside it. `@modelcontextprotocol/client` and `core`, which `ext-apps` imports, are exact versions like every other package, and they move with the updates Dependabot proposes. The widget's tests talk to the library's own host, `AppBridge`, over an in-memory transport, so what the widget receives in them went through the protocol. — *Why:* the author's decisions on the bundler and the linter. Biome is one tool for formatting and lint, as `gofmt` and `golangci-lint` are on the Go side, and it works with TypeScript 7, which the spike already used; it can also forbid imports by pattern, which the site's checker needs later. Client and core are not held to the pair `ext-apps` was built against. The widget's tests run `ext-apps`'s own `App` on the installed pair, which is where an `ext-apps` that no longer fits its client would show, on the paths the widget uses; they cannot show whether a real chat host still understands the widget, since both ends of a test are made of the same packages — but no pin decides that either, because the host runs a version of its own, and a live run is what shows it. — *Consequence:* no lint rule yet catches a promise nobody waits for — Biome has one only as an experiment; the type checker and the tests are what stand there. — *Rejected:* ESLint with typescript-eslint, which supports TypeScript only up to 6.0 and needs Prettier beside it for formatting; `@preact/preset-vite`, which brings Babel for a hot reload no chat host offers; jsdom, heavier and depending on mdn-data under CC0, outside the license list; holding client and core at the pair `ext-apps` 2.0.0 was built against, where they were first pinned: a pin keeps the pair its authors tested, but it cannot tell whether a real host still understands the widget any better than the tests can, and it leaves the widget behind every fix of theirs — so the author took the pair's first update once the tests had passed on it. — *Where it lives:* `web/`, `justfile` (`web-*`, `ci-web`), `.github/dependabot.yml`, T42.

**R86. The built widget lives in a Go package beside a committed placeholder; every build that ships builds the widget first, and the checks read it back out of the image.** The web build writes `internal/widget/widget.html`, which git ignores, and `placeholder.html` beside it is committed, so the package compiles and its tests run without Node. `widget.Page()` is the built page when there is one and the placeholder otherwise. `just build`, `just run` and `just release-artifacts` build the widget first, and `web-build` refuses a page that does not name `ui/initialize`, the handshake a host starts a widget with. The image builds the widget in a Node stage of its own, pinned by tag and digest at the development image's version, and copies the page into the Go stage. `just ci-image-widget` starts the image with the development sign-in and reads the widget the way a host does, refusing the placeholder or anything that is not a widget; the image job runs it, and so does `ci-image-push`, so the image that is pushed is the image that was read. The release workflow installs Node, at the development image's version, before it builds the binaries. — *Why:* `go test` must not need Node, and a committed page that every build overwrote would leave the tree changed and `git describe` reporting a dirty version. The fallback to the placeholder is silent by design, so the checks are what keep a build that ships from carrying it — and a binary cannot be checked by its bytes, since it carries both pages. — *Rejected:* committing the built page, as the spike did — a quarter of a megabyte of generated code in every diff; a committed placeholder that the build overwrites, for the reason above; a build tag that chooses the embedded file, which makes a second build that the linter and the editor do not see. — *Where it lives:* `internal/widget/`, `web/vite.config.widget.ts`, `Dockerfile`, `justfile` (`web-build`, `build`, `run`, `release-artifacts`, `ci-image-widget`, `ci-image-push`), `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, `.github/workflows/release.yml`, `.github/actions/node/`, T42.

**R87. The widget's resource tells a host what it needs both where it is listed and where it is read, and a tool that draws a card names the widget under both keys hosts read.** `resources/list` and `resources/read` both carry `_meta.ui`: a content security policy whose four lists are empty and sent as empty lists, and `prefersBorder: true`. A tool defined with `Spec.DrawsCard` carries `_meta.ui.resourceUri` and the flat `_meta["ui/resourceUri"]`. Resources are declared without `listChanged`, and every read of the widget is answered with a result of its own. — *Why:* the library the hosts run takes the policy that comes with the page and falls back to the listing (R06), and the Go SDK copies neither onto the other; the library's own helper for tools that draw a card writes both keys, for hosts older than the nested one. A server that keeps nothing between requests has nobody to tell that a list changed. The SDK writes into whatever result a handler returns, so a result shared between reads would be written by several at once. — *Where it lives:* `internal/transport/mcp/widget.go`, `tool.go`, `server.go`, SPEC 7.3 and 8.1, T42.

**R88. Dependabot brings the widget's protocol packages in a pull request of their own.** `@modelcontextprotocol/ext-apps`, `client` and `core` form one group in `.github/dependabot.yml`, and every update of theirs arrives in its pull request, major versions included; the group of routine bumps names them among the packages it leaves out, and every other npm package keeps to it, its major versions arriving one by one. — *Why:* the three are what the widget speaks to its host, and `ext-apps` runs on the other two, so a change to one is read with the others rather than as one line among the tools' bumps. A major version of `ext-apps` is likely to need one of its client and core, and a pull request of this group is read before it is merged whatever it moves, which is what keeping major versions apart is for elsewhere in that file. — *Consequence:* a security update is not grouped: Dependabot opens it for the package with the advisory alone. A pull request that moves `ext-apps` moves the version that R06, SPEC 8 and 03-flows name, and has to change them with it; nothing checks that, as nothing checks the wording of the repository (R22). — *Rejected:* a group for the routine versions of the three and another for their major ones, which rests on how Dependabot places an update that the patterns of two groups both match — its documentation says only that the first group matching takes it; the whole `@modelcontextprotocol` scope as the pattern, which would pull any later tool of that scope into the protocol's group. — *Where it lives:* `.github/dependabot.yml`.

## Instructions for the model

**R07. Text written for the model never relies on an exact tool name.** Claude prefixes tool names with the connector name (`MathTrail:show_widget`). The instructions describe tools by purpose and by their unprefixed name, without requiring an exact match; text shown to the child and the adult mentions no tool names at all. — *Why:* the prefix is added by the host and differs from host to host, so an instruction tied to an exact name breaks when the host changes. Found during the live run in T03. — *Where it lives:* T36; to be re-checked in ChatGPT in T63.

**R196. The guide says what grows from difficulty 1 to 5 inside a level: the steps, the search, the conditions and how well the trap the task turns on is hidden.** *(T72.10, 2026-10-04. Since R205 the guide takes a side on ideas: a task is none of the reference tasks, and is built on the idea the package names.)* The model is asked for a level and a difficulty, and the guide used to say only to take how hard the reference tasks are. A section of its own, "The difficulty", now says that the level sets the mathematics, the numbers and the words, and the difficulty how much work the task asks within them. It names four things that grow from 1 to 5, at 1, at 3 and at 5: the steps from the question to the answer; the cases to try, from none to a list kept in order; the conditions, from two or three stated directly to more of them, some negative but stated plainly ("not first", "except the last"), working only together and given out of order; and the trap the task turns on, from plain sight to a detail the child has to notice — an end counted twice, a case the conditions only seem to allow, an exception at the end of a series. A harder task is not a longer story or bigger numbers. The reference tasks carry their difficulty: those at the brief's, as a rule all three, are the measure, and one a step away shows it from there. The guide says so because the level `5-6` has reference tasks at difficulties 2 to 4 alone, so a brief there at 1 or at 5 is shown tasks a step away. The task's first rule now sends the model to the section rather than to how hard the reference tasks are, and the guide's test holds the names it gives — `brief.difficulty`, the difficulty every reference task carries — to the package. The negations are stated plainly so that the self-check, which counts a negation that is easy to miss as a fault, does not undo them; the trap's examples name no plot, so that the section tells the model what to do rather than what to write about. It ties the difficulty to work and says nothing of taking a reference task's idea: whether the guide should send the model to ideas of its own is a question of its own, О-59, raised by the variety run of T64a ([10-variety](live/10-variety.md)), and this section adds weight to neither side. The guide grows by about 1.5 KB, to about 11.9 KB, and so does every package: a child at every limit gets 36.2 KB at most in the characters JSON has to escape, against the budget of 64 KB. SPEC 4.1 now gives the sizes measured with this guide; the other figures there had fallen behind before it, the guide already 10.3 KB against the 7.5 written, and the packages 1.1 to 1.4 KB larger than written but for the characters JSON has to escape, as the catalog grew. — *Why:* the reference tasks show a difficulty but do not say what makes it, and a model left to guess may make a task harder by its numbers or its length, which the level's limits already hold. The four were read off the reference tasks of every topic across their difficulties, 1 to 5 at the two younger levels and 2 to 4 at `5-6`, and are what the tasks of one topic and level differ in. No effect is claimed: the log counts tasks and answers, not how hard a task truly is, so whether the model now hits the difficulty it is asked for is for the research program to measure. — *Rejected:* thresholds in numbers, such as so many steps at each difficulty, which differ by topic and would be read as rules to count rather than a scale to keep; a scale for every topic and level, many times the size for the same idea; a worked task at every difficulty, which the reference tasks already are; examples of traps from the reference tasks' own plots, which would steer the model to those plots. — *Where it lives:* `content/instructions/task_writing.md`, `content/package_test.go` (`TestTheGuideNamesOnlyWhatThePackageHolds`); SPEC 4.1 and remark 29; RUN.md (T72.10).

**R205. A task is built on an idea of its own, which the package names by a number that moves with every task of the topic; the reference tasks give its level, its difficulty and the kind of reasoning, not its idea.** *(Decided by the author in the planning of T80, 2026-10-05, settling the first half of О-59.)* A package carries a part of its own, `idea`: `number`, `of` and `round`. `of` is ten. The tasks of the brief's topic the child has left behind, answered or skipped — `topics[t].answers` and `topics[t].skipped` added up — give `number` as that count modulo ten, plus one, and `round` as the count divided by ten, plus one. The guide asks the model to open `core_idea` with a numbered list of exactly `idea.of` problems of the topic at the brief's level, each on an idea of its own and none on the idea of a reference task, the best known first, to build the task on the one at `idea.number` and no other, and to set it at the brief's difficulty: in its usual form in round 1, and from round 2 as a variant of it — what the usual form gives asked for, or something else counted — so that an idea that comes round again is not the same task again. The first rule of "The task" no longer says to take the reference tasks' structure: they show the level, the difficulty and the kind of reasoning, and the task is none of them, not one of them with a new story, new names or new numbers. The guide's worked example, which was the idea of two reference tasks of ordering told with the names of one of them, is now a task of its own, with its list. — *Why:* T64a found that the words of the tasks of one topic never run out and their ideas do: four to seven ideas in a series of twenty, one of them six to ten times, and the same task with the same numbers back at a similarity the check cannot see ([10-variety](live/10-variety.md)). Three tasks of four dressed a reference task their package showed, as the guide asked; and the model's own ideas repeated too — three-digit codes from the digits 1 to 5, even, came four times in one series with the answer 24 each time — because a model in a new chat sees none of the tasks it wrote before and reaches for the same favourite every time. A guide that only forbade the reference tasks would leave that favourite in their place. A number that moves with every task moves the model along a list of its own making, and the service keeps no text of any task for it: the setting and the reference tasks already turn with the answer count in the same way. The count is the topic's own, because a count over every topic would show a topic that comes back every fifth task two ideas of ten; skips are in it, so that a task left for another moves the next one on; ten, so that twenty tasks of one topic meet each idea twice; and the round is there because a model handed the same idea again in a new chat writes the same task with the same numbers. The list is of the level and not of the difficulty, because one idea can be set easier or harder, and a topic's difficulty moves every few answers: a list of each difficulty would change under the number every time it did. A variant asks for something else rather than adding or dropping a condition, which the guide counts as difficulty (R196). The list opens `core_idea`, the first field the model writes, so that it is written before the idea is chosen rather than around a favourite already chosen; the best known first, so that lists written in different chats keep much the same order. The reference tasks' ideas stay off the list: a first draft let them on it as the best known, and in the first task of the run the model listed four ideas, those of the three reference tasks first, and wrote the fourth though the number was 1, since the task may be none of them. The part stands beside the brief rather than in it, because the brief is handed back, and a member it does not have is refused. — *Consequence:* the guide grows by about 1.8 KB, to about 13.7 KB, and so does every package: a child at every limit gets 38.1 KB at most, in the characters JSON has to escape, against the budget of 64 KB. The count of skips per topic, which no rating reads, now feeds the package (R98). The count is the topic's at every level, so a child moved to another level meets that level's list where the count stands, and from the second round a variant of an idea not met before in its usual form. The lists are the model's own, written afresh in every chat, so the number moves the model along its list rather than promising which idea comes; how much the lists of one series agree is part of what is measured. A request that runs out of attempts moves nothing, so the next task of the topic is given the same idea; after a mistake the topic and the child's own trap in the brief carry the consolidation, and the idea moves on as after any task. Whether the model follows the number, and what it does to the ideas of a series, the refusals, the time and the difficulty, is measured in [11-variety-ideas](live/11-variety-ideas.md) against the same guide without the change. — *Measured (T80, 2026-10-05):* the model followed the number in 57 tasks of 58 and dressed a reference task its package showed in at most 4 of 58, against 37 without the change; the series held twice the ideas, and the same task with the same numbers came back 3 times against 22. Criterion 11.9 was met by one series of three. The list the number points into is written afresh in every chat, so the model's favourite stands at a different place of it each time, and in enumeration the same code came back three times under three numbers. Writing the list doubled the time to write a task, a median of 129 seconds against 66. What next is О-67. — *Rejected:* a memory of ideas, the model naming the reference task its task builds on and the profile keeping the last few of each topic, which is a new field of `submit_task` and of the profile and at `5-6` gives no more ideas than the three reference tasks of a cell; more reference tasks at `5-6`, content work that leaves the guide's instruction in place; a lower near-duplicate threshold, which refuses different tasks before it catches the repeats (R56); the guide alone, for the favourites above; a count over every topic; a list with no rounds. Taking `target_concept` from the request instead of asking for it back, which T64a raised beside this question, the author left as it is: a brief handed back without it costs an attempt, about a minute. — *Where it lives:* `content/package.go` (`ideaOf`), `internal/transport/mcp/getpackage.go`, `content/instructions/task_writing.md`, SPEC 1.5, 4.1, 4.1.1 and 4.3, `docs/architecture/03-flows.md` and `04-profile.md`, RUN.md (T80).

## Ratings and the choice of a task

**R79. The grade sets only where a child starts, and every task stands on one ladder of grades 1–6.** A task is still a level — `1-2`, `3-4`, `5-6` — and a difficulty from 1 to 5 inside it, but the fifteen pairs stand on the one scale θ is measured on: β = the level's shift + (difficulty − 3), with `1-2` at 0, `3-4` at 2.5 and `5-6` at 5, so that the two hardest difficulties of a level overlap the two easiest of the next and no two points coincide. The grade picks the child's starting point θ₀, the shift of its level, which is written into `ratings.start` when the profile is created and never moved again; a grade changed later is a label, and neither the ratings, nor the start, nor the trial series notice it. The first five answers are a **trial series**: each on a topic the child has not met, no failure worked over again, and after every answer θ is set to the most likely level given the start and all the trial answers at once — a normal prior centred on θ₀ with a spread of one level shift, times the chance of each answer by the formula of SPEC 2.1 — while the corrections of the topics stay where they are and every counter, trap and run is written as usual. From the sixth answer on, `Update` moves θ and δ as it always did. The estimate is found by evaluating that posterior on a grid of step 0.05 over θ₀ ± n · 2.5², where the maximum has to lie — no answer pulls with a slope steeper than 1, and the prior pulls harder the further it is — and by halving the best cell on the sign of the derivative. A topic is **within reach** when its easiest point, difficulty 1 of the lowest level it is taught at, is solved with a chance of at least 0.70 at θ + δ — the corridor's own floor — and a child below every topic is given those whose easiest point is the easiest the catalog has. The corridor chooses among the points of the topic the one whose chance is nearest 0.775, by the rule and with the marker it always had, and a topic is mastered at a level: it returns to the rotation when its recommended point moves above that level, a run completed at the higher level masters it again there, and nothing moves mastery down. A run is spent once it completes, whether it earns mastery or finds the topic mastered already, so the next level takes a run of its own; without that, a topic mastered at `1-2` and carried on a long run would be mastered at `3-4` on the first task of that level it met. The readability limits follow the task's level: the sentence limit of the level as before, and Flesch–Kincaid at the youngest grade of the level plus three, 4, 6 and 8. The package still tells the model the child's grade, as their age, for the words and the plot. There is one rating scale for everybody, on which the number shown is a place on the ladder; the ranks keep their width of one corridor, 166 points, and cover the ladder in eleven steps; and during the trial series neither is shown, only how many of its five tasks are done. — *Why:* the author's decision (О-56). The grade used to decide everything — which topics exist, what a difficulty means, the readability limits and the reference tasks — and that had three consequences. A child for whom the tasks of their grade were too hard had nowhere lower to go: there were no tasks below difficulty 1 of their own level. A change of grade carried the ratings across unchanged, so θ stood too high at the next level; the child was given tasks they mostly failed, and only a run of failures could correct it, with a step already narrowed by the answers behind it. And a topic mastered at a younger level stayed mastered at an older one, where it is harder, and dropped out of the rotation without ever being met there.
