On one ladder a child moves down when the tasks are too hard and up when they are easy, and the trial series finds the place quickly when the grade entered misplaces them. The spread of one level shift was chosen by simulation, a child answering by the formula of SPEC 2.1. A sweep over the ratings alone, three thousand children a case and twenty answers each, weighed the spreads: at 1.25 a child whose grade was entered a level too high still failed four tasks running in 31 % of cases and a child entered a level too low was still 1.3 off after twenty answers, while at 2.5 those were 17 % and 0.7; a spread of 3.75 bought the first child little, 15 %, and cost both the second child and the correctly graded one more. The simulation kept in `internal/domain/tutor` then runs the rule, the ratings and the profile together on the real catalogs, two hundred children of grade 3 a case, twenty answers each, every child played with the series and without it from the same seed: a child placed a level too high ends the series 0.87 from where they stand against 2.19 without it, and fails four tasks running in 6 % of cases against 50 %; a child placed a level too low ends it 0.92 off against 2.37. The price is paid by the child whose grade was right: 0.84 off after the series against 0.10 without it, and a run of four failures in 6 % of cases against none, because a single slip early in the series moves the estimate a long way — which the test holds to at most 1 and 15 %. A grid rather than Newton's method from the start, because the posterior can have two peaks: five right answers at difficulty 5 of `5-6` from a start at 0 put one near 0.1 and a higher one near 7.7, and a method that climbs from the start stops at the lower. The most likely level rather than the average of the posterior is the author's wording, and the grid finds the highest peak wherever it is. The levels are defined once, in `rating`, beside the shift that places them, and the content and the rule use those: a level is now part of the arithmetic a rating runs on, and two definitions of which levels exist could drift apart. — *Consequence:* the prototype's numbers, SPEC 2.6–2.7 and `ratings.json` stand as they were, because the shift of `1-2` is zero. A child just above a start can be given a point of the level below: a child of grade 3 at θ = 2.79 gets `time.clocks` at difficulty 5 of `1-2`, at P = 0.75, rather than difficulty 2 of `3-4`, at 0.83 — both are in the corridor, the first is nearer its middle, and the grade is not asked. The corridor holds up to two points where the levels overlap. Flesch–Kincaid is stricter than it was for grades 2, 4 and 6 on the tasks of their own level. The profile gains `ratings.start`, a `grade_level` in the brief, in the current task and in every answer of the window — the trial series reads the window back — and `mastered_level` beside `mastered_since`, and θ now means a place on the ladder; none of that raises the schema version, because no file of version 1 had been written by the service when it changed: the tools that write one arrive in T43 and the storage in T50. The fixtures of `testdata/profiles` moved onto the ladder, θ of the children of grades 3–4 up by 2.5. Two of the prototype's five reference briefs no longer match, and the rule's test names them with the reason: `masha`, three answers in, is in her trial series and moves to a new topic instead of going over the one she failed; `petya` is given difficulty 5 of `1-2`, with the traps of that level's reference tasks. The catalogs speak in levels, which reverses R42's "the grade crosses that line and the level does not". — *Rejected:* carrying the rating across a change of grade unchanged, as before, for the reasons above; recomputing θ when the grade changes, which would make a label move a number only answers should move; counting the rating shown from the child's own start, which would give two children at one place on the ladder different numbers; the grade as the boundary of the topics, which is what left a struggling child nowhere to go; a start without a series of its own, which is slow — the 50 % above; Newton's method, above. — *What would change it:* the acceptance runs (T62–T64) showing the levels further apart or nearer than 2.5 — children who do well at difficulty 5 of `1-2` failing difficulty 2 of `3-4`, or finding it a warm-up — or the series moving correctly graded children too far; the shift and the spread are one constant each in `rating`, and SPEC remarks 34 and 35 hold the questions. — *Where it lives:* `internal/domain/rating` (`ladder.go`, `trial.go`, `corridor.go`, `scale.go`), `internal/domain/profile`, `internal/domain/tutor` (the simulation in `simulation_test.go`), `internal/domain/checks/readability.go`, `content`, `testdata/profiles`, SPEC 1.1, 2, 3, 4.1–4.2, 5.5, 7.2–7.3, 8.2, [04-profile](architecture/04-profile.md).

**R153. The line about an answer carries the chance its task was handed out at, who chose the task, and where the answer falls in the child's count.** *(Since R199 `just report` weighs a line the log repeated once, sets each child's answers 101–200 against its own 6–50, and follows every topic a child was shown as mastered until it is taken back; what the live check, T72.14, checks is the floor under the step of R187, not a growth of the step.)* *(Since R193 a task may also be chosen by a person — `tutor_mode` `person` —, and `just report` leaves its answers out of the weighing as a kind of their own.)* *(T72.1, 2026-10-03.)* `answer_recorded` adds `chance`, the chance of a right answer the task was handed out at, to two places; `tutor_mode`, who chose the task as the task kept it from its request — `rule`, `llm`, or `unknown` for a task handed out before `current_task.tutor_mode` was kept, an optional field that raised no version; `trial`; and after the trial series `answers_bucket` — `6-20`, `21-50`, `51-100`, `101-200`, `201+`, the first range starting after the series. `just report` weighs the answers to tasks the rule chose, after the series and without the hint, against their chance, each cell with the number of children its answers came from: by the chance, whether it came true; by the range of answers, whether the estimate keeps up with the child — the mean of right less promised, with its standard error counted by child, since one child's answers lean together and an error counted by answer would make a few children look like many. — *Why:* the student model's numbers came from a simulation; these are the live ones a service with no database can have — whether its chances come true, and whether the estimate falls behind a learning child, which is what the growth of the step is tuned against (T72.11). How precisely the chat's model hits the difficulty it is asked for is not among them: each task is answered once, and a miss of 0.25 or 0.5 logits flattens the curve of promise against outcome by almost nothing. **What the weighing leaves out leans one way.** A child opens the hint, or leaves a task unanswered, mostly on a task that looks too hard, so leaving those out reads the share right a little high, and the mean above zero by as much. The report counts the answers it leaves out by why and the tasks left unanswered, so a reader can weigh the lean; whether answers with the hint belong in the weighing is the author's call. **The rating in a line.** No line carried the rating before, or a number that told it more finely than a whole difficulty; `chance` tells it to about a twentieth of a logit near the corridor, and beside `user` it follows the child's rating answer by answer. The topic, the level, the difficulty and whether each answer was right let the rating be rebuilt too, but only from the child's first answer on, with the start guessed from the first tasks, and only while the log keeps every answer since; the chance tells it outright. That is the trade: a pseudonymous child's rating, in a log only the operator reads, for the one live check of the student model. It stays out of every span, every label of a measurement and every other line. — *Rejected:* the chance's range in place of the chance, since the mean of right less promised needs the chance itself and the middle of a range would bias it by up to half the range's width; the child's level, which tells more than the weighing needs; and reading the chance from the `task_requested` line of the task's request, which the call of an answer cannot be joined to. — *Where it lives:* `internal/transport/mcp/submitanswer.go`; `internal/domain/profile` (`task.go`, `request.go`, `answer.go`, `validate.go`, `profile.schema.json`); `internal/report/answers.go`; SPEC 12.2–12.4; `docs/architecture/04-profile.md`.

**R155. The student model is measured on a bench of simulated children, `tools/learners`, carried over from the research and drawing the paper's children unless told otherwise.** *(T72.2; the seed and the references to the research decided by the author, 2026-10-03.)* `tools/learners` is a Go module of its own — `github.com/MathTrail/mathtrail-standalone/tools/learners`, the product through `replace ../..` and no dependency of its own — whose simulated children answer through the product's own `profile.New`, `tutor.Next`, `Ask`, `Issue` and `Record`. It is `research/experiments/learnersim` carried over word for word, but for the command's name; the seed and the run's name, which `-seed` and `-experiment` set and which are the paper's, 20261001 and E-A3, when neither is given; comments that spoke of the chain; and what only the paper needed, which stays in the research: Urnings, Glicko-2 without guessing, the Markov chain of mastery, the gradient step, the rules in one structure or the other, the floors of 0.01 and 0.02, K₀ halved and doubled, the corridor's other targets, the model's other slopes and floors, the bias of the corridor, the figure and the numbers file, with the knobs of the step and of the rule that only those turned. Seven rules run on all nine generators — the service's, the constant step, the slow constant step, the floor of 0.05 under the step, the step with no trial series, and Glicko-2 with guessing with one level and with a level per topic — 63 cells, under the names the research gave them, from which every seed of the bootstrap is drawn. The four groups of comparisons set the service against every other rule. A run writes `cells.csv`, `comparisons.csv`, `run.txt` and `summary.md`, the numbers a change is judged by, and prints the summary; `tools/learners/results/` keeps the whole run of the service's rule as it stands. `just learners-lint` and `just learners-test` run on every pull request; the lint is the load tool's, now shared by both as `_tool-lint`. — *Why:* the student model is chosen by numbers, and the numbers have to measure the product as it is. The research module is to pin the product at the paper's commit before the rule changes, so the bench cannot be the research's, and importing its package would carry the pin and the paper's whole design; a copy that follows the product carries only what choosing a rule needs, and its test of the step against `rating.Update` turns red the moment the service's step changes, which is when the bench has to follow. The paper's children by default make the bench's numbers for the service's rule the paper's, so that the paper and the site's research page cite the same numbers; this replaces the decision taken when the work was planned, a seed of the bench's own and no reference to the research, made before the research was public. Checked on amd64: every number of the 42 cells and of the 21 comparisons the bench shares with the research run is the research's to the last digit. — *Consequence:* a change to the rule, the rating, the profile or the catalog's topics moves the bench's numbers. `TestASmallRunIsSummedUpAsItsSnapshot`, which holds the summary of ten children a cell to `tools/learners/testdata/summary.md`, then fails until the snapshot is rewritten with `-update`; the rewritten snapshot, and a whole run when the change is to the student model, are read in review. Nothing holds `results/` to the code, since a whole run takes two minutes. On arm64 the last places of a number may differ from amd64's. A change to the product's `go.mod` is followed by `just learners-tidy`. — *Rejected:* the research's package imported as it is, which would pin the product with it; the research's numbers copied as the line to beat, which would not move with the product; a seed of the bench's own, the first decision. — *Where it lives:* `tools/learners/`; `justfile` (`learners`, `learners-test`, `learners-lint`, `learners-tidy`, `_tool-lint`); `.github/workflows/ci.yml`; `docs/learners.md`; `CLAUDE.md`; `CONTRIBUTING.md`.

**R163. A new rule of the student model is chosen by a criterion written down before any candidate ran, on a bench that knows its ceiling.** *(T72.3; the plausible learner, the weights, the reading of better and of not worse, and the bound on the screen decided by the author, 2026-10-03, with the refinements below.)* `tools/learners` runs ten more generators beside the nine it was carried over with — no miss of the model, a miss of 0.25 and of 1.0; G2-half, a learner at half G2's speed, one logit over 200 answers, the main learner, the speed the author takes as plausible; learning that fades, as 50/(50 + k); a drop of 1.0; spreads around the start of 0.5 and 2.0 and of topics of 0.3 and 1.0 — and an eighth rule, the oracle, which stands where the child truly stands and is the ceiling of every rule that learns from answers. A generator's children are drawn from seeds named after it and its spreads are factors on the same draws, so every cell the bench was carried over with gives the same numbers to the last digit: `testdata/carried-over/` holds them, and a test holds every run to them. New measures: `r3_reachable`, the share of tasks whose topic's ladder held a point in the corridor at all; and R8, what the child is shown, read off the rule's own estimate through the product's `rating.Elo`, `Shown` and `Rank` — the 95th percentile of the move of the card's rating, and the changes of the overall rank and of the answered topic's in a hundred answers, in answers 6–20 and 150–200. The criterion, in `docs/learners.md` and in `criterion.go`, read for every rule into `results/criterion.md` and `criterion.csv`: **constraints** — on G2-half a lag of at most 45 % of the service's and a corridor at least c* of the way from the service's to the oracle's, c* = (0.35 − 0.257) / (0.605 − 0.257) = 0.27, the share G2's own goal closes; on G3 at most 25 % not caught up; on every generator not worse than the service in the error after 200 answers, the corridor and false masteries, the worse end of the paired interval within the author's tolerance (0.01 logit, 1 and 2 points) but never under 4.3 standard errors of the paired difference between the slow constant step and the service in the same run; on G0 and G2-half the card's move and the rank's changes no more than the service's in answers 6–20, in either window — and a **score**, the share of the way from the service to the oracle closed, over G0 (1), the learners (2: G2-half 1.25, fading 0.5, G2 0.25), the jump and the drop (1) and G1 (0.5), with the way fixed from the whole run so the score is a weighted paired difference with the service. Better is an interval above zero; the chosen candidate is the simplest of the best and its equals; the backup with stored uncertainty must win by 0.05 of the way; a decision runs 4,000 children a cell and is confirmed once on the held-out seeds (20261003, `held-out`, `-held-out`), and failing that, or with no candidate through, the exit is a floor under the step of 0.02, 0.05 or 0.1. — *Why:* a rule chosen by numbers is chosen honestly only if what counts as better was fixed before the numbers were seen, and only if the numbers reach where the model can be wrong; the oracle shows how much there is to win at all, and makes the scores of measures in different units one scale. The author's decisions, refined where the bench showed them out of joint: the tolerances of 0.01, 1 and 2 points are finer than the bench resolves on 1,000 children — half-widths of 0.005–0.013 logit, 0.2–0.6 and 0.7–1.9 points — so an equal candidate would have failed some of the fifty-odd checks by chance, hence the floor of 4.3 standard errors and the decision size of 4,000; and the main learner's corridor carried over as 1.35 times the service's would have asked for 48 % where G2's own goal, as a share of the way, asks for 42 %. — *Consequence:* a full run takes four to five minutes. The oracle's error is 0, so on the error the share of the way is a relative saving and small: a static child is held by the constraints rather than the score. The oracle is a ceiling for the mastery rule the service has, not for a new one; pairing it with a candidate of mastery needs a way to put that rule in the service's place, left to the choice of mastery. Nothing runs the held-out seeds before the step's confirmation. The criterion of mastery is written down beside the step's and enters the code with its candidates. — *Rejected:* the score as a sum of raw differences, which mixes logits, shares and answers; a step chosen without a ceiling, which cannot say how far from the best a candidate stands; tolerances below the bench's resolution; trying a second candidate on the held-out seeds. — *Where it lives:* `tools/learners/` (`child.go`, `cells.go`, `estimators.go`, `measures.go`, `screen.go`, `aggregate.go`, `criterion.go`, `criterionreport.go`, `main.go`, `report.go`); `docs/learners.md`.

**R166. The step of the rating is held no worse than the service's on the error and the corridor, and the rule of mastery on false masteries.** *(Decided by the author in T72.4, 2026-10-03; amends R163.)* The step's checks of not worse are the error after 200 answers, `r1_rms_200`, and the corridor, `r3_inside`, on every generator, with the tolerances and the resolution R163 gave them. The share of masteries declared falsely, `r4_false`, moves to the checks of not worse of the rule of mastery, on every generator, with its tolerance of 2 percentage points or the bench's resolution, whichever is larger. The step's candidates' false masteries are still read, and shown for every rule in `scenarios.md`. — *Why:* under the service's rule of mastery — three right answers in a row on the harder half of the corridor — false masteries grow the closer an estimate follows the child. On the run of T72.3, on G2-half, they were 52 % under the service, 54 % under the floor of 0.05, 56 % under the slow constant step, 59 % under the constant step and 73 % under the oracle; on G2, from 40 % to 66 %. At the decision size of 4,000 children the tolerance comes down to the author's 2 points, so the check would fail every step that follows a learner better, the exit's floor of 0.05 among them (+2.9 points on G2), for a fault of the rule of mastery — which T72.5 chooses next, over the step chosen, holding false masteries to at most 20 %. — *Rejected:* keeping the check on the step, which would end the choice in the exit of a floor of 0.02 or nothing, for a reason the step does not own; choosing the step and the rule of mastery together, which would move the choice of the step into T72.5 for no gain over reading false masteries where the rule that makes them is chosen. — *Where it lives:* `tools/learners/criterion.go` (`stepNotWorse`); `docs/learners.md`, "Choosing the step" and "Choosing mastery"; RUN.md T72.5.

**R175. The rule of mastery is chosen against the step chosen under the service's own mastery, after a pilot that can stop it.** *(Decided by the author in T72.5, 2026-10-04; amends R163.)* In the choice of the rule of mastery, "the service" every comparison is with is the baseline: the step T72.4 chose, the floor of 0.05, under the service's own rule of mastery. Its numbers bound the wait for mastery and the screen, every check of not worse reads the difference from it, the score's way runs from it to perfect, and it is the exit, kept as it is without any check when no candidate meets the criterion. Before the decision run the candidates are run once on the sweep's children, a thousand a cell; a candidate is stopped when on some generator the whole 95 % interval of its difference from the baseline in masteries never declared lies above the author's 2 percentage points, not widened by the bench's resolution; if every candidate is stopped, the decision run waits for the author. — *Why:* the rule of mastery is chosen over the step already chosen, so its choice should measure what the rule gives by itself. Under the service's rule of mastery the floor of 0.05 alone already moves the numbers the choice of mastery reads: in the decision run of T72.4, masteries never declared to a child who learns rose by 2.4 points on G2 and 1.9 on G2-half, and false ones by 3 points — against a tolerance of 2, so the service as the comparison would charge every candidate with the step's doing. The pilot is there because the check of masteries never declared and the goal of at most 20 % false meet head on: the service declares generously — 63 % of its masteries false, 22 % of a child's true ones never declared — and any stricter rule leaves more undeclared within 200 answers; if no candidate can pass that check, the author decides with the numbers in hand, before the working children are used. The pilot stopped every candidate. The cautious estimate at z = 1 cut false masteries from 65 % of those declared to 4 % — 4 % of those the child is shown too — and declared in half the answers; it was stopped only by masteries never declared to children who learn (on G2-half 34 % against the baseline's 19 %), where the step chosen trails the child and the margin comes on top of the trail, while on a child who stays put it left fewer undeclared than the baseline (18 % against 24 %). *(Decided by the author after the pilot, 2026-10-04.)* The check of masteries never declared is read on G0 and G0-topics1 alone, where the goals of mastery are read: there whatever is left undeclared is the rule's own doing. The decision run then stopped every candidate all the same and took the exit: the cautious estimate on the step's check of the error after 200 answers, on children who learn or change, and on masteries never declared on G0-topics1; Wald's test on masteries never declared; a run of five on the goals; the author then accepted the cautious estimate's costs (R182). — *Rejected:* the current service as the comparison, as the criterion first read; dropping the check of masteries never declared, or folding it into the wait for mastery, before any candidate was seen; after the pilot, dropping it altogether, which would let a rule wait less by declaring less, and keeping it on every generator, which leaves the service's rule of mastery, 65 % false. — *Where it lives:* `tools/learners/criteria.go` (`masteryCriterion`), `tools/learners/masterysets.go`; `docs/learners.md`, "Choosing mastery".

**R182. In the choice of mastery, a rule that declares only what it is sure of may cost the estimate a little, and leave a little more undeclared on the widest spread of topics.** *(Decided by the author in T72.5, after the decision run of mastery, 2026-10-04; amends R175.)* The decision run stopped every candidate and took the exit, the service's rule of mastery, 65 % of whose masteries are false. The cautious estimate at z = 1 met every goal with room to spare — 3.7 % false on G0, and a mastery declared in 4.6 answers against the baseline's 9.8 — and was stopped by two costs, which the author accepts for that gain: an error after 200 answers on children who learn or change up to 0.018 logit [0.014, 0.022] above the baseline's, 1 to 3 % of their error, against a tolerance of 0.010 to 0.013; and 5.3 points [4.2, 6.3] more masteries never declared on G0-topics1, against a tolerance of 2. In the choice of mastery the check of the error after 200 answers is read with a tolerance of 0.03 logit on every generator, and that of masteries never declared on G0-topics1 with 8 points; on G0 it stays 2 points, the corridor and false masteries keep theirs, and the step's criterion is as it was. Each tolerance is what the cautious estimate costs in the decision run plus 4.3 of its standard errors, the width every tolerance of not worse keeps — 0.026 logit on G2-fading, 7.6 points — rounded up: a rule that costs as much passes a run of new children 99 times in 100, where tolerances just above the costs, 0.025 and 7 points, would fail it about one time in three. The decision run is read again under the amended criterion, and the model it chooses is confirmed once on the held-out children, which no run has drawn: the tolerances were set after the working children were seen, so the confirmation is what tests them. Read again, the decision run chose the cautious estimate at z = 1 over the floor of 0.05, the one candidate meeting every constraint, and the held-out children confirmed it: all 71 constraints met, 0.690 [0.686, 0.695] of the way, 3.8 % false on G0 against the baseline's 65 %, its costs as before — at most 0.015 logit of error, 5.4 points more never declared on G0-topics1. — *Why:* false masteries are what a rule of mastery is for, and the cautious estimate cuts them seventeenfold while declaring sooner. Its costs are small and have a cause. A topic held mastered leaves the rotation, and for a child who moves on its estimate stays where it was, while on a child who stays put the error does not move (0.488 under both); a topic far above the child's level clears a margin late. The step's tolerance on the error would keep out every rule that declares true masteries sooner. — *Rejected:* keeping the exit, which leaves 65 % of the masteries declared false; dropping the step's checks from the choice of mastery, which would let a rule that sets more topics aside cost the estimate without bound; reading masteries never declared on G0 alone, which drops the widest spread of topics the margin was to be chosen on. — *Where it lives:* `tools/learners/criteria.go` (`masteryErrorTolerance`, `wideNeverTolerance`, `masteryNotWorse`); `docs/learners.md`, "Choosing mastery".

**R187. The student model: the service's step with a floor of 0.05 under the overall level's, and a topic mastered by a cautious estimate at z = 1.** *(T72.6, 2026-10-04: how the product takes the choices of T72.4 and T72.5 — R163, R166, R175, R182; answers О-53 and changes the criterion of О-32. That the masteries of the earlier rule are cleared was decided by the author.)*

**The step.** K_θ = max(0.2 / (1 + 0.05·n), 0.05), and K_δ = 0.4 / (1 + 0.05·m) as before (SPEC 2.2). The floor binds once n passes 60, from the 62nd answer. A topic's step comes down to 0.05 only at its 141st answer, and keeps no floor.

**Mastery** (SPEC 2.5). It is judged after a right answer without the hint, in a topic with at least five answers, on θ and δ as the answer left them. The topic is mastered at the highest level it is taught at, at or below the task's, whose middle task the child answers with P ≥ 0.775 at the cautious level λ = θ + δ − √v. Here v = 1/(1/2.5² + I·n) + 1/(I·(20 + m)): n and m are the answers in all and in the topic, this one included, and I = 0.149981 is what an answer at the corridor's middle tells of a level. Mastery is declared only above the level held, and lost after two wrong answers in a row, as before.

**What the bench measured** — the learners' bench, 4,000 working children a cell, the model against the service:

| Measure | The model | The service |
|---|---:|---:|
| False masteries, G0 | 3.7 % | 63.5 % |
| Answers until a mastery is declared, G0 | 4.6 | 9.9 |
| Error after 200 answers, G0 | 0.488 | 0.493 |
| Tasks in the corridor, G0 / G2-half | 43.0 % / 37.3 % | 42.6 % / 35.6 % |
| Lag of the main learner, G2-half | −0.543 | −0.614 |
| Children not caught up after a jump, G3 | 58.6 % | 72.4 % |
| Overall rank changes per hundred answers, answers 150–200, G0 | 1.7 | 0.7 |

Late in a run the overall rank changes 1.7 times in a hundred answers, still within the 3.7 the service shows at the start. The held-out children confirmed the model (R182).

What each part gives:
- the floor closes 0.056 [0.055, 0.058] of the way from the service to the ceiling (T72.4);
- the cautious estimate over it closes 0.688 [0.684, 0.693] of the way from the floor under the old mastery to perfect, and 0.690 on the held-out children;
- the margin of z = 1 against none closes 0.035 [0.032, 0.037] more of that way, with 3.7 % false instead of 15.4 %.

The goals of T72 the step does not reach — the lag of the main learner, the jump, the corridor of a child who learns — stay unreached. No step built from an uncertainty met the criterion, and the floor is the exit T72 named, not a failure (О-53).

**What else this decides:**
- **The screen after the trial series does not change:** the floor binds tens of answers later.
- **The golden vectors.** `config_example` replays as before: its three steps are at n = 0, 1 and 2, where the floor does not act. `doc_example` stays out of the replay. The property that every answer leaves a smaller step is restated: θ's step shrinks until it reaches 0.05 and stays there, and δ's always shrinks.
- **The masteries of the earlier rule are cleared** (decided by the author). A file not marked `ratings.mastery_rule` counts no topic mastered. The first answer the service records into it clears every topic's mastery and marks it, before the answer is judged. The version of the file stays 1: the field is optional, and an older build reads past it and drops it when it writes. So after a rollback and a new rollout, the masteries the older build declared are cleared again — they are the older rule's.
- **`top_streak` is counted as before, and read by no rule.** Removing it, or no longer counting it, would change what a file of version 1 holds. An older build reading the file masters by it as it did. It goes with the next version of the file.
- **The guard of T72.9** runs about 300 children each of G0–G3 and holds each of these to its recorded value within two half-widths of its 95 % interval:
  - on G0, the error after 200 answers, the corridor, false masteries, the answers until mastery, masteries never declared, and the overall rank's changes in answers 150–200;
  - on G1, the error of the overall level after ten answers;
  - on G2, the lag and the corridor;
  - on G3, the children not caught up after the jump.

— *Why:* the step and the rule of mastery were chosen by the criterion of R163, written before any candidate ran, and the confirmation on the held-out children stands; what is written here is how the product takes them. D17 stands: the rating is moved by code, Elo-style, by whether the answer was right and by nothing else. D37 stands too. The two K₀ stay, and the floor needs no limit on a move: from the 62nd answer, two failures move θ by at most 0.1, a tenth of a difficulty. Two in three of the earlier masteries were false on the bench, and clearing them keeps the progress and the rotation from carrying them.

— *Rejected:*
- For the step:
  - the main candidate, a step from an uncertainty counted by answers, with growth q and a limit on a move. It followed the main learner within the goal (a lag of 0.212), but missed the error of a child who stays put (0.512 against 0.493) and moved the rank more often late in a run (4.6 against 3.7);
  - the backup, an uncertainty kept in the profile, which changed the rank 12.6 times in a hundred answers early;
  - a floor of 0.02, which closes nothing (0.0001), and one of 0.1, worse on the error on six generators;
  - the full filter, the estimate over the whole history and two speeds, which stay on the bench as comparisons — the last near the "recent results" D17 rejected.
- For mastery: the earlier run of three (65 % false); a run of five (68 % false, 77 % of the masteries never declared); Wald's test (61 % never declared); the cautious estimate at z = 1.28 and 1.64 (more never declared on G0).
- For the earlier masteries: keeping them; judging each again at its topic's next answer, which needs the earlier ones marked and lets a child see "mastered" go.

— *Where it lives:* SPEC 2.2, 2.5, 2.6, 2.8, 2.12 and remarks 68–69; PRODUCT 4.5, О-32, О-53; `docs/architecture/04-profile.md` (`ratings.mastery_rule`, `top_streak`); `docs/learners.md`; `tools/learners/candidates.go` (`chosenStep`) and `tools/learners/masterysets.go` (`chosenMastery`).

— *Extended in T72.9:* the bench measures the product, and guards it.
- **The chosen model is the product on any sequence of answers.** A property of the bench runs a hundred random sequences of 150 answers, under the service's own path and under the chosen model, the bench's copies of the step with its floor and of the cautious estimate at z = 1. The sequences have any grade and three topics in any order, tasks at any point of their ladders, right as often as three times in ten to almost always, and the hint one time in five. After every answer the overall level and every topic's agree to within 1e-12, and every mastery held, declared and lost agrees exactly. The tests that held the copies to the product before did so on the children the bench draws; this one holds them where no child goes.
- **The service as it was is a rule of the bench,** `earlier/both`: the service's step with no floor, after the trial series, and mastery by a run of three in the service's rule's place. A small run of it gives, value for value, the 344 numbers the service gave as the bench was carried over (`testdata/carried-over/earlier.csv`, without the intervals, which are drawn per cell name), so the earlier rule is the service before this decision and not a likeness of it. Every run of the bench shows the service beside it, and `docs/learners.md` keeps the comparison of a whole run.
- **The guard.** `just ci-learners`, a job of its own in CI on every pull request, runs the service's path on the first 300 children of each of G0–G3 of the paper's run, 200 answers each, and reads the ten numbers named above with no interval. It holds each within two half-widths of the 95 % interval recorded with it in `tools/learners/testdata/guard.csv`, the half-width half the width of the bootstrap interval at 300 children, drawn as a run of the bench draws the same cell's. The guard is a subcommand of the bench, `learners guard`, with `-update` as its only flag; a run of the bench refuses words after its flags, so a guard asked for there is not run as the whole bench. It takes about four seconds on the development machine. Rolled back, the model turns it red: with the floor taken away, the lag on G2 (−1.127 against −1.065 to −0.932) and the children not caught up after a jump (70.3 % against 41.7 to 65.0 %); with mastery by a run of three, the false masteries (65.1 % against 1.8 to 5.4 %) and the answers until a mastery (9.9 against 4.1 to 5.3). `-update` records the bands again, for a change meant to move the model, its whole run read and its reason written down; the diff of `guard.csv` is read in review, since recording turns a red guard green.
- **The bench counts the traps of the reference tasks once a run,** as the rule's simulation does, which changes none of its numbers.
- *Why:* the copies were held to the product only on the bench's own children, and nothing kept a change of the product from losing the model's gain unseen: the bench's snapshots hold ten children and are rewritten by whoever changes them. Three hundred children make bands of about four standard errors either way that still catch the rollbacks above, in seconds rather than the minutes of a whole run. A subcommand with a flag set of its own refuses every flag of the bench without a list to keep.
- *Rejected:* the earlier rule's numbers copied from the history into `docs/learners.md`, which no run could make again (the author's decision); the guard as a test of the bench under the race detector, minutes rather than seconds; one-sided bands, which would let an unmeant change pass for being an improvement.
- *Where it lives:* `tools/learners/` (`guard.go`, `guard_test.go`, `property_test.go`, `candidates.go` (`earlierServiceRule`), `cells.go` (`cellsOn`), `loop.go` (`take`), `main.go` (`cachedCatalog`, the subcommand), `testdata/guard.csv`, `testdata/carried-over/earlier.csv`); `justfile` (`ci-learners`); `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (`learners-guard`); `docs/learners.md`; CLAUDE.md; CONTRIBUTING.md.

**R199. The student model is held to live children by two tables of the report, which the learners' bench reads off its own children the same way, under a rule of reading written before the data.** *(Prepared in T72.11, 2026-10-05, the day after the release that carries R187 rolled out and before any of its numbers was read; T72.14 reads them once there are enough, split off by the author's decision of the same day. It narrows the live check to the floor under the step and the cautious mastery of R187, and says how remarks 68 and 69 are read.)* *(Since R212 both tables end with the row of every version, which the reading reads, in a window from the rollout to the first release that changes the model, and the share taken back and its error are to three places.)*

**The later answers set against the earlier.** `just report` gives the weighed answers of R153 once more, of each child with answers in two ranges of its own: the earlier, 6–50, and the later, 101–200. In each range the mean of right less the chance over its answers, m_E and m_L, and D = m_L − m_E, with its standard error counted by child, a child's two ranges together: u_c = (s_Lc − n_Lc·m_L)/N_L − (s_Ec − n_Ec·m_E)/N_E and SE = √(C/(C − 1)·Σu_c²), over the C children with answers in both, where s and n are a child's summed differences and answers in a range and N a range's answers. The rows are by instructions version and host, and for each version over every host, `(every host)`, which leaves out the calls of the load tool and MCP Inspector and every child either handed a task to; a child who answered the two ranges in different hosts is set against itself over every host alone. The numbers are to three places, and a range of fewer than 30 answers says too few. The two ranges rest on the ranges `answers_bucket` names (12.2): a range the lines name that crosses where either begins or ends, which the service writes only if it counts in other ranges, is named under the table rather than read in neither unseen. The weighing now takes a line the log repeated once, as the counts of children always did: a repeat is the same text again, and counted twice it would weigh one child's answer twice.

**The masteries taken back.** The report follows each topic a child was shown as mastered — a `topic_mastered` line, read with the `answer_recorded` line of the same call, child and topic — through the child's later answers in that topic, in the order they were given, and takes it back at the second wrong answer in a row there, as the service takes it back: an answer with the hint and "I don't know" count as what they are, and a right answer ends the run. A mastery the lines stop following first, at their end or at the topic shown as mastered again at a higher level, was not taken back while they followed it. The table counts the masteries shown and the children, those taken back by the 5th answer, from the 6th to the 10th and later, those not taken back, and those settled by the 10th answer, taken back by then or followed to it; the share taken back by the 10th answer is read by Kaplan–Meier, with a jackknife error by child. A share that fewer than 30 settled masteries stand behind says too few. The report reads the topic a line names for it.

**The bench reads the same** (R9 and R10 of `docs/learners.md`), by the report's own definitions: the ranges of answers, the answers the share is read within, and the following of a mastery, `report.Followed`, are exported by `internal/report` and imported by the bench, so the two cannot drift apart. `r9_kept_up_6_50`, `r9_kept_up_101_200` and `r9_kept_up_later_less_earlier` weigh the answers after the trial series and without the hint against the chance the service promised, written to two places as a line writes it. `r10_shown` counts a mastery shown exactly as the service decides to write `topic_mastered` — declared on the answer, not shown by `tutor.Mastered` before it and shown after it —, and `r10_taken_back_5` and `r10_taken_back_10` read the share taken back by Kaplan–Meier, as the report does. A property holds the report's sign to the product: on any sequence of answers under the service's own path, a mastery the report's `Followed` follows is taken back on exactly the answer the service records as losing it. The new measures move no number the bench had: the guard reads its recorded values to the last digit, and every row `results/cells.csv` held stays as it was, in its order.

**The rule of reading**, in full in `docs/learners.md` ("Reading the live numbers"), with the service's numbers on every generator, a thousand children a cell:
- the author runs `just report <whole days since the release rolled out>d`, at most 30, and reads the release's instructions version over every host;
- **the floor** — enough to read when both ranges hold at least 20 children and 300 answers, and the error of D is at most a third of D(G2) − D(G2-half), 0.084 − 0.054, so 0.010. It holds when D, two errors either way, meets the band from 0.006 to 0.077: from the lowest D of the generators whose children's levels do not move (G0-exact), to G2-half's plus the spread of those generators, 0.006 to 0.029. Above the band the floor is chosen again; below it the cause is found first, and the bench, given a generator that repeats it, decides which way the floor moves;
- **mastery** — enough to read when at least 20 children are shown masteries, 100 masteries are settled by the 10th answer and the error of the share is at most 0.03. It holds when the share less two errors is no higher than the highest of the service's on G0, G0-topics1 and G2-half: 39.4 %, G0-topics1's. Above it, the margin z is chosen again;
- choosing again is a decision of its own: a generator on seeds of its own that repeats the live number; the criterion run over floors of 0.02, 0.05 and 0.1, or over margins z, on 4,000 children; a confirmation on held-out seeds no run has looked at, since those R187 was confirmed on are spent; then SPEC 2.2 or 2.5 brought to it, the guard's bands recorded again and the carried-over rows of the service rewritten by hand.

— *Why:* the table of how the estimate keeps up cannot tell a floor too low by itself. Its range 51–100 holds answers on both sides of the 62nd, where the floor begins to bind, and the children of one range are not those of the next: those who stop early are not those who go on, and a difference of ranges would read who stays as an estimate that falls behind. Setting each child against itself takes that out, and with it whatever leans the same way in both ranges — the answers the weighing leaves out, a model that misses the difficulty it is asked for by the same each time. The earlier range ends at a boundary the line already names, before the floor binds; the later one is past it and ends where the bench's children stop, so the bench reads the same. Even a child who stays put shows a D above zero, 0.013 on G0, since near the corridor's middle an estimate off either way costs more chance than it gives, and early on it is further off: the band starts from the bench, not from zero. A fall in `topics_mastered` is no sign of a mastery taken back, since the count also falls when the child's level moves a topic's tasks above the level it was mastered at; two wrong answers in a row in the topic after `topic_mastered` is the service's own rule of losing one, as the property shows. A mastered topic leaves the rotation for a while, so many masteries are followed for a few answers only when a window closes; counting those as held would read the share low, and leaving them out would read it high. One child's answers and masteries lean together, so both errors are counted by child. A true mastery is taken back now and then too — 34 % of those shown within ten answers on G0, where 3.6 % of the service's masteries are false —, so the live share is read against the bench's, never alone. The rule is written before the data, as the criterion of R163 was written before any candidate ran, so that what the numbers turn out to be cannot shape how they are read.

— *Rejected:* the ranges past the 61st answer read alone, as remark 68 first had it; `201+` as the later range, past what the bench's children reach; a fall in the count of topics mastered as the sign of a mastery taken back, as remark 69 first had it; a plain share of the masteries taken back; an error counted by answer or by mastery; a band from zero; a store of its own for the log of windows past a month, which the counts kept for years are for (the author's decision, 2026-10-04).

— *Open:* a reading past thirty days. A child is paired only within a window that holds both its ranges. The log keeps every line a month; the bucket `activity` of the counts for grants (R192) keeps the lines of answers and masteries whole, `user` among their fields, for 62 days, but `just report` does not read it yet and it holds no tool call; the tables kept for years hold no identifier and cannot pair a child. `just report` taught to read the bucket, pairs gathered from successive windows, or a comparison without pairs with its lean named would each be a decision of its own. A window also ends at the first release that changes what the instructions version is a hash of — the instructions, the solver's templates, the drawing frames —, since a child's answers then split between two versions the report does not pair across, and at the first that changes the student model, which the version does not tell apart; unless the report learns to take the versions that run the same model together.

— *Where it lives:* `internal/report/` (`later.go`, `masteries.go`, `answers.go`, `children.go`, `report.go`, `tally.go`, `write.go`, their tests, `testdata/lines.jsonl`, `testdata/report.md`); `tools/learners/` (`live.go`, `live_test.go`, `measures.go`, `aggregate.go`, `mastery_test.go`, `results/`); `docs/learners.md`; SPEC 12.4 and remarks 68–69; RUN.md (T72.11, T72.14).

**R212. The live check of the student model reads every version of the instructions together: both tables of R199 end with a row, `(every version)`, that sets a child against itself across the releases that changed the instructions alone.** *(T72.14.1, decided by the author on 2026-10-05 in the planning of T72.14, which it splits in two, T72.14.1 and T72.14.2; it revises R199's rule of reading before any live number was read.)* Both tables of R199, the later answers set against the earlier and the masteries taken back, gain a last row that takes every version of the instructions the lines hold together, over every host, printed whenever the table has a row. A child is paired there across versions; a mastery counts under the version of the answer that showed it, as before, and is followed whatever the version of the answers after it. The load tool's and MCP Inspector's children and calls are left out of it, as of every row. The reading reads that row, in a window that starts after the model's rollout, `just report <N>d` with N the whole days since then and at most 30, or `<H>h` within the first day, and ends before the first release that changes the student model itself, since `just report` reads up to the moment it runs. The share of the masteries taken back and its error are printed to three places, as the bands they are read against are. The lean the row admits is named by each reading: a child's earlier answers fall more under the older instructions, and a change of them that moved how far the chat's model misses the difficulty it is asked for leans the difference, so the reading lays the versions' rows of "How the estimate keeps up" side by side (`docs/learners.md`, "The lean of the versions taken together"). The rows of each version stay, and every line keeps its version (О-21): the row of every version reads the student model, which runs alike under each.

— *Why:* the instructions version, the hash of the instructions, the solver's templates and the drawing frames, changes with nearly every release, and the model does not. v0.2.6, the release of R187, rolled out at 23:37:52 UTC on 2026-10-04, and v0.2.7 kept its version, `7ed3c64f063d`; v0.2.8 brought `38d142f805be` at 14:17:38 UTC on 2026-10-05, with the model as it was, and the next release brings another. A child's answers 6–50 and 101–200 fall under different versions, and the report paired a child within one version alone, so the window of R199's rule closed after some fifteen hours, in which no child answers a hundred tasks. The versions of a window run one model as long as no release in it changed the model, and that is what the row reads. A row printed whenever its table has one is the same row for every reading.

— *Rejected:* holding back the releases that change the instructions, the solver's templates or the drawing frames until enough has gathered, weeks without fixes such as R210's; a field of the student model's version on the lines of answers and masteries, since a hash of the model's code moves with a rename, as with v0.2.8's of the corridor's bounds, a number kept by hand is forgotten, and either reads only the windows after the release that brings it, while it changes the log's fields, their rules and the counts kept for years; reading one version's row, which pairs almost no child; a row of every version for each host; the row only when the lines hold more than one version.

— *Consequence:* the last reading of a model is taken before a release that changes the model rolls out; a reading after it, or a window that ends before the moment `just report` runs, is a decision of its own. The thresholds of R199 stand. At the spread the bench reads, an error of the difference of 0.010 wants some hundred children past their 101st answer, and an error of the share of 0.03 some hundreds of masteries, so a reading past thirty days (R199, *Open*) is likely to be needed.

— *Where it lives:* `internal/report/` (`later.go`, `masteries.go`, `answers.go`, `later_internal_test.go`, `masteries_internal_test.go`, `testdata/report.md`); `docs/learners.md` ("Reading the live numbers"); `docs/live/13-student-model.md`; SPEC 12.4 and remarks 68–69; RUN.md (T72.14.1, T72.14.2).


**R193. The child or the adult may keep the lessons to one topic: kept in the profile, set by the rule once the trial series is over, and recorded as chosen by a person.** *(Decided by the author in the planning of T78, 2026-10-04; carried out in T78.1, the server's part, after the author's mockup `draft/ui/next-task/topic-choice.html`.)* The profile gains `student.lesson_topic`, a topic of the catalog by its id, left out while the rule chooses. The card of a task writes it with `edit_profile`, and the model with `save_profile` when the child or the adult asks, never on its own; an empty one gives the choice back to the rule. An edit takes a topic of the catalog only, `not_in_catalog` otherwise; the file is held to 64 characters alone, as a country is, and a topic the catalog does not have counts as none where it is read. Once the trial series is over, `tutor.Next` sets the chosen topic in place of its own, at the topic's recommended point — as a topic the model names alone is set — with the rule's goal, and records the request's `tutor_mode` as **`person`**; the rationale says the lessons are kept to the topic and at which point, before the rule's own account. The model may still name a level or a difficulty with a reason, which makes the mode `llm`; another topic of its own is refused, `topic` `not_one_of`, naming the topic chosen and `save_profile`; naming the chosen topic itself asks for nothing, needs no reason, and sets nothing aside on a request already open. During the trial series the choice is kept and waits. "Next up" follows it — `recommendation.chosen` — and so does the review, which leads its topics to develop with it and prefers it as the topic to begin, as it does the topic the rule sets next. `read_task`, which only the card reads, carries `topic_choice` with a task on the card once the series is over: the topic chosen or null, up to three topics the review's steps are for, in their order, and `site`. `just report` names the answers to a person's tasks as a kind of their own and leaves them out of the weighing of chances. — *Why:* the author's decisions of 2026-10-04: the choice stands until it is given back, across days and chats; the trial series keeps moving to a new topic each time, so a choice made in it waits; the suggestions are the review's; and the new chooser is `person`, beside `rule` and `llm`, since a value names who chose rather than what was chosen. Text mode is mandatory (О-10), so the model can make and undo the choice as well as the card, or a topic chosen on a card would hold a chat with none invisibly. The child's choice is the stronger one because the model would otherwise undo it with a reason nobody asked for. The weighing measures the rule's own choices: a person's topic stands at the rule's point too, but which topics are chosen is the person's. The value was written at once: no profile was in any Drive when it came, the author having deleted every one, so no file that holds it was ever read by a build that refuses it. — *Rejected:* the choice held by the card alone, lost with every new card; the model passing the topic and a reason on every request, which records the model as the chooser and forgets the choice when the model does; a new tool for the choice, where the two tools of the profile already write what is the child's; the choice applied during the trial series; a two-step release that first reads `person` and only then writes it, needless with no profile to read; `chosen` as the value, which describes the topic rather than who chose it. — *Consequence:* `profile.Change` and `profile.NewStudent` take a catalog of skills and topics, `profile.Catalog`, rather than a function for skills alone. `save_profile` has 54 characters of its description left, so `lesson_topic` is explained in its schema, pointing at the topics `next_task` lists. — *Where it lives:* `internal/domain/profile/` (`student.go`, `edit.go`, `task.go`, `validate.go`, `profile.schema.json`), `internal/domain/tutor/` (`tutor.go`, `rationale.go`), `internal/domain/progress/` (`progress.go`, `review.go`), `internal/transport/mcp/` (`lessontopic.go`, `editprofile.go`, `profile.go`, `readtask.go`, `nexttask.go`, `payload.go`, `progress.go`, `submitanswer.go`), `internal/report/answers.go`, `content/instructions/mcp_instructions.md`; SPEC 2.11, 3.1, 3.4, 3.6, 7.2, 7.3, 12.2; PRODUCT 3, 4.1, 4.3; `docs/architecture/04-profile.md`, `03-flows.md`; `docs/parents.md`; the privacy policy; RUN.md (T78.1).



## Content for the model

**R08. Solver templates live in `content/solvers/`, one or two per topic.** A template is a short program following the solver contract, with marked slots for the numbers of the task, generalized from a solver already written for a reference task; the id of that reference task is in a comment. The package builder includes only the templates of the chosen topic and stays within the size budget, and the instructions call a template a sample one may depart from. — *Why:* the solver is the only programmatic guarantee that there is exactly one correct option (PRODUCT 4.3), and an error in it costs the child a whole attempt; a worked-through example of the typical enumeration removes the most common cause of `solver_error` for a weaker model. — *Rejected:* a mandatory skeleton, which would have made tasks bend towards whatever the template can do; and having no templates at all (О-43). The risk of sameness is countered by the rule rotating topics (T27) and by the duplicate check (T32). — *Where it lives:* T36a, T36.

**R09. Text drawing frames live in `content/drawings/`.** A frame is generic, carries no numbers from any particular task, and comes with a structural description of its objects and their relations; the model fills in the numbers and the labels. Every frame passes the format check at the default limits itself — otherwise it is not a frame. — *Why:* the drawing stops depending on what the model happens to remember about box-drawing characters, and the format check (О-11а) passes on the first attempt more often. — *Rejected:* drawing rules in the instructions with no ready-made frames (О-44); free-hand drawings remain allowed and are checked against the same limits. — *Where it lives:* T36b, T36; once the limits are calibrated, the frames are re-checked in T58.

**R16. The child's notes are data, never instructions.** `child.notes` is written by the parent and read by the chat's model, so it travels in the package inside a delimited block that the instructions introduce as information about the child, followed by the rule that nothing inside it changes what the task must be; `save_profile` strips control characters and anything that could close the block, and checks the 500-character cap after stripping. — *Why:* it is the only field in the product written by a person and read by a model, which is the shape of a prompt injection; found in the external review of 04-profile. — *What makes it survivable:* the rule never reads the notes, so a note cannot choose the topic or the difficulty, and the solver decides what is correct — the worst a note can do is steer the tone (О-31). — *Rejected:* dropping the field (it is what lets the model pitch a task at the child); trying to detect instructions in it by pattern. — *Where it lives:* SPEC 4.1.2, [04-profile](architecture/04-profile.md), T36, T43.

**R52. A reference task's solver is a file, not a field.** `content/examples/solvers/<id>.star` holds the program that proves one task's answer; the loader reads it as the task is read and fills `Example.Solver`, and the field is not part of the task's JSON at all, so a `solver` key written there is refused rather than silently ignored. A file naming no task is refused too, because nothing would ever run it — the other half of the pairing, a task with no file, is refused by the bench. — *Why:* SPEC 1.5 originally put the program in the task as a JSON string, and the first fifty showed the cost: every newline escaped, the whole program one line several hundred characters wide, no syntax highlighting, and a change to one constant rewriting the entire line in the diff. The reference solvers are the sandbox's regression suite and are meant to be read and edited for years; a form that makes a one-line change unreadable in review is the wrong form for them, and fifty tasks in is the cheapest moment to say so rather than four hundred and fifty. Naming the file after the task id is what keeps the two halves findable from either side, and it needs no index. — *Consequence:* a solver is no longer visible beside the question it answers, which is what a review needs; `just solver <id>` prints the two together. The tasks' JSON files go back to being data a person edits, with no code inside them. — *Rejected:* keeping the string and living with the diff, which is the cost above paid four hundred and fifty times; `content/solvers/`, which is already the generalised templates shown to the model (R08) and would put two different things under one name; one file per topic holding all fifty programs, which brings back the search for the right one and gives a merge conflict the whole topic to touch. — *Where it lives:* `content/example.go`, `content/examples/solvers/`, `content/solver_test.go`, `justfile`, SPEC 1.5 and 6.8.

**R37. The JSON schemas are the written contract, and Go does the checking.** `content/schemas/` holds `brief.json`, `task.json` and `self_check.json`, embedded in the binary and checked at startup for being schemas at all; what a submitted task is actually measured against is Go — a strict decode that refuses a field the format does not have, plus the explicit checks of T32 and T35. The reference tasks get no schema of their own: the `Example` type and the loader in `content/` are their format. — *Why:* a schema library would have to earn a dependency, and here it would buy the weaker half of the work. The checks that matter — five options a child can tell apart, a distractor for exactly the four wrong ones, a trap from the catalog, a level the topic is taught at — are not expressible in JSON Schema, and the refusal a model is sent has to name what to fix in words it can act on (SPEC 5.9), which a schema error is not. The schemas stay because they are the one place the three formats are written down in full, and because a later consumer — a tool description, a generated document — needs them machine-readable. — *Consequence:* the schemas and the Go types can drift, and nothing but review catches it; they sit next to each other in the same package for that reason, and a format change touches both in one commit. — *Rejected:* `santhosh-tekuri/jsonschema` or `kaptinlin/jsonschema` as the single source of truth, which leaves the catalog and option checks in Go anyway and so buys two validation paths instead of one; dropping the schemas, which SPEC 4 requires to be in the binary. — *Where it lives:* `content/schemas/`, `content/`, T23, T32, T35.

**R72. A solver template searches only what the question leaves open, and every program runs under the one step budget.** A number the question gives stands at the top of a template as a capital like any other, and one it leaves out is `None` there and is tried at every value it could take; the search then walks the unknowns alone. The template of three groups works this way for those in all three and for each pair, as the one of two groups does for the size of a group. — *Why:* tried over every overlap, three groups cost the fourth power of the smallest one: a class of sixty took 59 million steps against a budget of 25 million, and a school of a hundred asked `product` for more than the million elements it will build. A question of this kind almost always gives the pairs or those in all three, and each number given takes a loop out: with every pair given, three hundred pupils take 24 thousand steps. — *Consequence:* a question that gives none of the overlaps still searches all four, which takes a third of the budget for a class of sixty and more than twice the budget for a school of a hundred; there the model is told that the solver ran out of steps and to count the cases rather than list them. — *Rejected:* a step budget of its own for a template, which would bind nothing, because the model writes its own program from a template and the budget is what guards an instance every request shares; raising the budget for everyone. — *Where it lives:* `content/solvers/logic.sets/three-groups.star` and `two-groups.star`, `content/solver_test.go`.

## Task checks

**R10. The explanation behind a wrong option is checked deterministically, with no judgement of meaning.** Four conditions: the texts of the four distractors are pairwise distinct; a text neither equals nor is a prefix of the solution or the hint; a text is not the verbatim description of the trap from the catalog; and a text is not shorter than the minimum for its writing system (words, or characters for CJK and Thai). A rejection gets its own code and points at the explanation that needs fixing by its trap, never by its option's letter (R57). — *Why:* "a wrong answer is a diagnosis" is one of the three pillars of the product (PRODUCT 1), and in text mode the explanation is the main thing the child gets. — *Rejected:* instructions and reference tasks alone with no programmatic check; and any heuristic for "meaningfulness" — with a median generation time of 69 seconds, extra strictness costs the child another attempt (О-46). — *Where it lives:* T32a; the rejection code is defined in T12.

**R44. The solver's contract is a domain package and the interpreter is infrastructure.** `internal/domain/solver` holds the statuses, the five option texts, the shift that relabels them, the comparison a computed answer is looked up by, and the rule that turns two runs into one verdict; it declares a `Runner` interface of one method. `internal/infra/starlark` is what implements it. — *Why:* what two runs mean together is the whole point of the check, and it is arithmetic over two results — it can be written down, read and tested with no interpreter anywhere near it, which is what its properties do against two fakes of four lines each. It also keeps the dependency rule honest rather than aspirational: nothing under `internal/domain` may import `go.starlark.net`, and the linter says so. — *Rejected:* one package under `internal/solver`, as the line in RUN.md first said, which would have put a rule of pure arithmetic behind an embedded language and made every test of it a test of the sandbox as well; leaving the two runs to the pipeline of T35, which would give the bench of T29 no way to run a reference solver the way a submitted one is run. — *Where it lives:* `internal/domain/solver`, `internal/infra/starlark`, `.golangci.yml`, RUN.md T28.

**R45. Everything the sandbox predeclares caps itself at a million elements and charges the step budget for them.** *(Its price is overtaken by R125: what a built-in builds is paid a step for every sixteen bytes rather than a step for every value, so the step ceiling bounds what the built-ins of a run keep.)* The cap and the charge cover our own helpers and twelve built-ins of the language besides — `list`, `tuple`, `sorted`, `set`, `dict`, `reversed`, `zip`, `enumerate`, `min`, `max`, `any` and `all` are declared again with the cap on them — and the charge happens before the call does any work, so a call nothing could pay for is refused rather than half-built. The ceiling and the price are counted differently: the ceiling counts the elements of the list a solver ends up holding, and the price counts the values inside them, so a hundred thousand tuples of twenty costs two million steps rather than a hundred thousand. — *Why:* the interpreter counts its own instructions and looks for a cancellation only between them, so neither the step budget nor the clock can reach inside a single call. A lazy range is the way in: `range(1000000000)` costs nothing to make, `list` of it is sixteen gigabytes built where nothing is watching, and `min` of it is ten seconds of Go with both limits asleep. The length is known before the walk starts, which is what makes a refusal possible at all. — *Consequence:* the operators stay open. `[0] * 100000000` allocates through the language's own `*`, where there is no name to stand in front of, and starlark-go's own guard there stops only at 2³⁰ elements — more memory than an instance has — so a solver written that way takes the instance down rather than the request. There is no power operator in this dialect, which closes the other half of the same hole, and nothing else turns something small into something huge in one step. The budget counts elements and not digits, so `sum` and `prod` also read the run's clock as they walk (T39a.2): whole numbers grow as they are multiplied, and a product of a hundred thousand of them ran four seconds past a two-second limit inside one call, where a loop written out by hand would have been stopped between its steps. — *What would change it:* the gap ever being seen in the aggregate log; the fix is a solver in a process of its own with a memory limit on it, which is a change of shape rather than a setting. — *Rejected:* capping `range` itself, which would forbid a loop of two million iterations that the step budget pays for happily; charging a list of tuples by its length alone, which reads simpler and lets a product of nineteen pairs through — half a million tuples, ten million values and a hundred and sixty megabytes, against a ceiling that saw five hundred thousand; a memory limit in Go, which has none per goroutine — `debug.SetMemoryLimit` makes the collector work harder and refuses nothing. — *Where it lives:* `internal/infra/starlark/vocabulary.go`, SPEC 6.5 and 6.6.

**R46. A recursive solver is `error`, not `bad_source`.** The dialect keeps the recursion check on; the check belongs to the interpreter and fires when the recursive call happens, not when the file is parsed, so the status is the one for a program that failed while running. — *Why:* the alternative is to promise the parser catches something the parser does not look at. A pass over the syntax tree before the run could name the direct case, but a name in Starlark can be reassigned — the dialect allows it — so the pass would be an approximation that still does not replace the check underneath it. The model reads the same sentence either way, because every status reaches it as one refusal; the status is read by the aggregate log, and there "it failed while running" is exactly what happened. — *Rejected:* the pre-pass above; matching on the text of the library's error, which is the library's to reword. — *Where it lives:* `internal/infra/starlark/dialect.go`, SPEC 6.3 and 6.4.

**R48. A helper refuses what the solver got wrong rather than quietly mending it.** A string handed to a helper expecting a sequence is refused, and so is a date that never happened — the thirtieth of February, a thirteenth month, a year outside the four digits. Each refusal says what to write instead. — *Why:* both mendings are available and both are worse. This language does not iterate a string, so a helper that accepted one would teach a second rule that holds nowhere else in the dialect, and the model would learn the wrong one; the porting table of SPEC 6.8 carries the one line it costs instead. A month of thirteen is January of the year after, and answering about it would give a solver a confident wrong number — the one failure this whole check exists to catch. — *Rejected:* taking a string as its characters, the way Python does, for the reason above; normalising a date the way the standard library does by default, which turns a solver's bug into a plausible answer. — *Where it lives:* `internal/infra/starlark/calendar.go`, `internal/infra/starlark/vocabulary.go`, SPEC 6.5 and 6.8.

**R53. The step ceiling is 25,000,000, and the memory it lets through is the price.** *(Its measure of what the helpers let one run hold is overtaken by the load of T52a.3, R124: products of pairs hold 824 MiB at this ceiling, more than twice the 381 of the two products of nineteen measured here; SPEC remark 63. Settled by R125: the helpers pay for bytes, so at this ceiling what they keep is at most 381 MiB whatever they build, and the costliest reference solver is 2,413,617 steps under that price.)* `MATHTRAIL_SOLVER_STEPS` defaults to twenty-five million, up from ten; the time limit stays at two seconds. — *Why:* SPEC 6.6 asks for a ceiling an order of magnitude above the costliest reference solver, and the bench over all 450 measured that one at 2,081,362 steps — ten million was under five times it. Three of the prototype's checks, transcribed as they stood, did not fit ten million at all, and a model's first draft looks more like a transcription than like the rewrites SPEC 6.8 describes. Time is not what limits it: at the measured rate twenty-five million steps are some four hundred milliseconds, against two seconds a run. — *Consequence:* the step budget is also the only thing that bounds memory (R45), so the bound moves with it. Measured on one run: what the helpers build rises from 190 MiB to 381 MiB — the product of nineteen pairs that R45 was written to refuse now passes, twice — and a loop of appends from 129 MiB to 326 MiB, on an instance of 512 MiB that runs up to four solvers at once. The same measurements found that the operator gap R45 accepts is wider than it says: `xs = xs + xs` in a loop reaches 480 MiB in under three hundred steps, at any ceiling. Nothing runs a submitted solver yet, so neither is reachable today. The review of T39a.2 found it wider again: the methods of the values themselves take a lazy range of any length in one step — `x.extend(range(3000000))` is nineteen steps, `x += range(3000000)` eighteen and `set().union(range(3000000))` twenty at the default budget, three times the million the helpers refuse, and a billion is as cheap to ask for — so no cap on the helpers can close the gap, and only a limit on the memory of the process can. Time has the same gap inside a single call: the clock is seen only between instructions, the step budget counts elements rather than their digits, and a list of a million copies of one number with a hundred thousand digits, which appending pays for honestly, keeps `sorted` or `set` busy for minutes; a process of its own would bound its time as well as its memory. — *What would change it:* the tools that first run a submitted solver (T41–T45) are where memory has to be settled, because that is when the gap becomes reachable from outside. Two shapes are on the table: a separate allowance for what the helpers build, kept at ten million values while the steps stay at twenty-five, which restores R45's bound for the helpers and nothing else; or the process of its own with a memory limit that R45 names, which closes the operators too. — *Rejected:* keeping ten million and relaxing the rule of SPEC 6.6, which would leave a model's ordinary first draft refused for being ordinary; making the two costliest reference solvers cheaper, which would hide the distribution the ceiling is calibrated on. — *Where it lives:* `internal/config/config.go`, `internal/telemetry/solver.go` (the buckets reach the new ceiling), SPEC 6.5, 6.6 and remark 15.

**R54. The near-duplicate check is pg_trgm reproduced, and the fingerprint is fixed for good.** `internal/domain/checks` cuts a question the way Postgres's `pg_trgm` does — lowercased, split on anything that is neither a letter, a digit nor a mark, each word padded with two spaces in front and one behind, the set of its trigrams — and compares two sets by their Jaccard index. A question whose task is in a language written without spaces — or, with no language, one most of whose letters are in the scripts written so — is cut into padded character bigrams instead, and what it is compared with is cut the same way. The reference tasks are compared by their texts; the child's past tasks by a MinHash sketch of the same set: FNV-1a of each shingle, xor-ed with a hundred and ninety-two seeds that step by the golden ratio, spread by the SplitMix64 finaliser, the smallest kept per seed and its lowest four bits stored, two to a byte — 96 bytes in standard base64 — and the share of agreeing positions taken back by the one in sixteen that agree by chance. A sketch that does not decode to 96 bytes is skipped, and each of the two sources is reported once however many of its entries a question resembles. — *Why:* the threshold was chosen with Postgres's numbers, so the measure has to give those numbers, and it does: the fifteen exported pairs, the fifty most alike reference pairs and all sixteen buckets of the nearest-neighbour distribution match `trgm_similarity.json`. The last took one detail that is not about the measure — the export read single-precision values as their shortest decimals, and a similarity of exactly 0.65 or 0.70 falls into one bucket or the next depending on it. The sketch is fixed because the profile keeps it: two hundred of them per child, compared against for as long as the file lives, and a sketch made any other way agrees with them nowhere — the service would stop noticing a repeat of anything given before the change and say nothing. A test pins two sketches as literals for that reason, and it is not to be regenerated. The sketch was replaced once, before any profile kept one: it began as sixty-four one-byte positions, whose error of about 0.06 let the same task given new numbers in Russian — 0.74 alike against 0.7 — slip under the threshold about one time in five; a hundred and ninety-two four-bit positions err by about 0.03 in 128 characters of base64, which two hundred of them can take and leave the profile inside its 64 KB target (T39a). — *Consequence:* a change of how sketches are made needs a way to carry the old ones; a changed threshold needs nothing, because a sketch is compared, not judged. Counting the two kinds of script against each other is what makes Japanese one kind of text however its letters divide between three scripts, where a task comes with no language; where it comes with one, the language decides, for this check as for readability (T39a). — *Rejected:* comparing past tasks by their texts, which the profile does not keep (О-40); a sketch of eight bytes a position, sixteen times the profile for a bias that the correction for chance takes out anyway; two hundred and fifty-six four-bit positions, which pass the profile's 64 KB target near its caps for an error of 0.027 against 0.031; a sketch that remembers which construction made it, which costs a byte for a comparison that is already near zero across kinds. — *Where it lives:* `internal/domain/checks/shingles.go`, `fingerprint.go`, `duplicate.go`, SPEC 5.6.

**R55. A submission is read strictly, every stray named, and no refusal quotes an option.** `checks.Decode` reads the brief, the task and the self-check each on its own, as the JSON they arrived as. A part that is missing or is not an object is refused by name and not checked further; within a part, every member the format does not have is named at any depth, the first value of the wrong type is named with what it should hold, and what could be read is kept, so that `checks.Structure` finds the rest of what is wrong in the same attempt. Inside a list the same holds: every faulty entry is named by its place — `brief.constraints.2`, `self_check.issues.0` — not only the first one found; an entry keyed by an option's letter cannot be pointed at without the letter, so what is wrong with such entries is counted instead. Wherever a path would pass through an option's letter — `distractors.B.trap` — it is written with a star instead, and no message of either function names a letter or an option's text. The brief is read into `profile.Brief`, the type the request already records it as. — *Why:* SPEC 5.1 wants every fault reported at once, because a second attempt that fixes one thing and trips over the next costs a child another minute, and `DisallowUnknownFields` stops at the first stray and reports it only as the library's text — R46 already declined to branch on that. The letter rule is SPEC 5.1's: the result of `submit_task` draws a card in the widget, and a refusal that names the options explained reveals the one that is not; the tests hold every refusal to it rather than trusting the wording. — *Consequence:* only the first type error in a part is reported, because the JSON library stops reporting after it; the others surface as that field being empty on the next attempt, if at all. — *Rejected:* a JSON Schema validator, for R37's reasons; decoding into structs keyed by letter, which would put the letter in every path; reading past unknown members, which turns a misspelt field into one the model believes it filled in. — *Where it lives:* `internal/domain/checks/decode.go`, `structure.go`, SPEC 5.1 and 5.2.

**R56.
