The near-duplicate threshold for words is 0.7, and a new setting makes a new task.** `checks.Threshold` is 0.7, up from the prototype's 0.6, and text written without spaces is held to it too: SPEC 5.6 gave that text a stricter 0.7 while words stood at 0.6, and with words at 0.7 the two are one number. One constant rather than two equal ones, because a choice between equal numbers decides nothing and a test of it could not fail; the choice comes back with a number of its own for the characters. — *Why:* the prototype recorded no measurement behind its 0.6, and T32 made one — the question a new task is asked, put to the 450 reference tasks: at 0.6, 155 of them have a neighbour of their own level at or above it, nearly all of the same topic; at 0.7, 115. Against the pairs Postgres measured, 0.7 is the highest threshold that still catches the same task given new numbers in Russian, which measures 0.74 where English gives 0.85 — an inflected language changes more trigrams for the same edit. What it lets through is the same task in a new setting, 0.61 to 0.63: that is a repeat a child might notice, and the price of catching it was a third of good tasks refused as copies and an attempt spent each time. — *Consequence:* a model that dresses the last task in the next of the child's interests is not stopped by this check; the rule's own rotation of settings and topics is what varies the tasks, and the self-check and the instructions carry the rest. Two topics are not helped by any threshold — `logic.knights_liars` and `algorithms.weighing_pouring` open every question with the same paragraph of rules, and a set of trigrams cannot tell that paragraph from a problem. — *What would change it:* the acceptance runs (T62, T63) showing those two topics refused on repeat; the answer then is a measure that subtracts each topic's recurring trigrams, which took the 155 down to 61 in a trial but works only where the reference tasks share the chat's language. A corpus in Chinese or Japanese is what gives the character threshold a number of its own. — *Rejected:* keeping 0.6, for the refusals above; 0.8, which lets the same task with new numbers through in Russian; the subtracting measure now, which is a different measure from the one the threshold was measured with and helps only English. — *Where it lives:* `internal/domain/checks/duplicate.go`, SPEC 5.6 and remarks 9 and 25.

**R57. An explanation is pointed at by its trap, three words is the floor, and the reference tasks pass the check they teach.** `checks.Explanations` holds the four explanations to SPEC 5.3 and refuses with `distractor_explanations`. A refusal names the condition and the explanation's trap — "an explanation for trap \"double_count\" is 1 word long" — and never its option's letter. An explanation must be at least three words long, or six characters where the script is written without spaces. All 450 reference tasks pass, and a test in `content` holds them to it; fifteen reference explanations were rewritten to get there — five that repeated another explanation of their task word for word, ten that were the opening words of the solution. — *Why:* R10 and SPEC 5.3 said the refusal names the option, and SPEC 5.1 says no refusal ever quotes an answer letter; they are the same letters, because naming the options whose explanations failed names options that are wrong, and four of them name the right one. The trap is the model's own choice, already in the brief, and enough to find one text among four. The reference tasks are what the model imitates, and SPEC's six words refused 117 of the 450 — their shortest explanations say what went wrong in three, "Monday is today." — so a check that strict would teach the model to fail it; three refuses none of them and still refuses "Wrong." and "Try again.". Texts are compared as they read, because a copy with its full stop turned into an exclamation mark is still a copy, and a prefix is taken word for word, because "Four" does not begin "Fourteen ships". — *Consequence:* the length the product wants is asked for by the instructions (T36), about six words that name the child's mistake, and is not enforced; where two explanations share a trap, the refusal points at both and the model checks both. — *Rejected:* the option's letter, for the reason above; the condition alone with a count, which leaks nothing either but makes the model search; six words, for the 117 examples; leaving the reference tasks as they were, which would leave the examples contradicting the check. — *Where it lives:* `internal/domain/checks/explanations.go`, `script.go`, `content/examples/`, `content/checks_test.go`, SPEC 5.3.

**R58. Flesch–Kincaid counts as the prototype's library did, and estimates only the syllables.** `checks.Readability` measures the longest sentence of a question and, for English, its Flesch–Kincaid grade. The words and sentences of the formula are counted exactly as `textstat` 0.7.13 counted them — a test recovers the library's own syllable count from its grade as a whole number for all 450 reference questions — and the syllables are estimated by rule instead of looked up. The formula's words are the pieces between whitespace that hold a letter, a digit or an underscore: the library took the punctuation out before it split, which never joins two pieces or splits one, so the count is its count, and each word keeps its accents for its syllables. The rule reads an accent by what it says in English spelling — an accented vowel is still a vowel, a diaeresis says it apart from the vowel before it, an accented e is never silent — and reads letters decomposed, with `golang.org/x/text/unicode/norm` from a module the binary already carried, so that an accent written after its letter as a mark of its own is the same accent. A word, for the sentence limit and for the explanation check alike, is what whitespace separates, unless it is punctuation alone. — *Why:* the margin of +3 was calibrated on the library's numbers, so the numbers have to stay close to them, and everything except the syllables can be made identical. The syllables cannot without the CMU dictionary — a hundred thousand words, several megabytes in the binary — and the hyphenation patterns the library fell back on; the rule gets within the tolerance SPEC 5.5 now states (0.205 on average, the verdict agreeing in 97.7 % of 900 cases), and most of what it misses is the dictionary's rather than English: Russian names the dictionary did not have counted as one syllable, "drawer" as one, "hour" as two. Read byte by byte, an accented vowel was a consonant: "café", "José" and "naïve" came out as one syllable, and so did "résumé", which kept down the grade of a question with such words. Whitespace tokens are the prototype's words, which is what the sentence limits were measured with; a token of punctuation alone is not one, because French sets its question marks apart with a space and every French question would otherwise be a word longer. — *Consequence:* a first-grader's English task passes a little less often than the prototype's did — 79.5 % of the reference tasks against 83 % — and the pass rates of the other grades move by three points at most. An ellipsis standing alone in a sum is no word here and was one for the prototype, which makes four reference tasks a word shorter. — *Rejected:* embedding the dictionary, for its size and for a fallback it would still need; counting words as runs of letters and digits, which splits "o'clock" and "3:00" and differs from the prototype on 105 of the questions; counting every whitespace token, which counts the French "?"; keeping the apostrophe of a contraction as the library did, which it needed only to look a word up in its dictionary and which changes no count here; taking an acute accent after a vowel for a split, which French does ("Chloé") and Irish does not ("Seán"); a table of accented letters, which does not see an accent written as a mark of its own. — *Where it lives:* `internal/domain/checks/readability.go`, `fleschkincaid.go`, `script.go`, SPEC 5.5.

**R59. A drawing is matched against the wording by its Latin capitals, and a capital that could be a word is taken for one.** `checks.DrawingMatch` refuses with `drawing_mismatch` when a label `drawing_structure` declares is not drawn — drawn meaning present whole, with no letter or digit joined to it, so that "A" is not found in "BAR" nor "1" in "12" — and when the wording names a label the structure does not declare. What the wording names is read from its Latin capitals standing apart from any word: a lone capital is a label except at the start of a sentence, of a quotation or after a colon, except the English "I", and except straight after a number, where it is a unit; capitals written together are a label each when most of their letters are declared labels, and a word in capitals otherwise. Numbers and quotations are not read. — *Why:* SPEC 5.4 took every lone Latin capital, digit and short quoted name for a label. Over the 450 reference questions, 351 hold a number, 110 a lone capital — nearly always the article "A" — and all 21 short quotations are speech, so nearly every task with a drawing would have been refused for labels it never had, each refusal an attempt of the child's. Under this rule one reference question of 450 names labels, the X, Y and Z of a locker code, which a drawing of it would label; and the case the check exists for — "triangle ABC" with only A and B drawn — is still caught. The author chose this reading over SPEC's. — *Consequence:* a label the wording names by a digit or a word is not checked against the structure, though the structure's own labels are still checked against the drawing; a lone capital that is really a word in the middle of a sentence — a Roman numeral, say — is taken for a label and refused if the structure does not declare it. — *Rejected:* SPEC's reading, for the numbers above; reading nothing from the wording, which misses the triangle; taking capitals written together for labels as soon as one of their letters is declared, which reads "NOT" as three labels the moment a drawing labels a point O. — *Where it lives:* `internal/domain/checks/drawingmatch.go`, SPEC 5.4.

**R60. A drawing is measured in cells with the ambiguous characters as one, against limits set in one place in the code.** `checks.DrawingFormat` counts a wide or fullwidth character — East Asian Width W or F, looked up with `golang.org/x/text/width` — as two cells and every other as one, including the East Asian ambiguous characters, which are most of box drawing, block elements, arrows and geometric shapes. The limits, 30 cells, 12 lines and 20 spaces in a row, are `checks.DefaultDrawingLimits`, and the check is handed them. Each rule a drawing breaks is one refusal, naming the first five lines and the first five characters by code point. — *Why:* a frame is made of ambiguous characters, and counting them as two would halve every frame's width for the sake of East Asian fonts that draw them wide; monospaced fonts elsewhere draw them narrow, and T58 checks which the widgets use. The limits are the product's numbers rather than a deployment's (SPEC 11.1): RUN.md asked for them "from the config, so that T58 changes them without code", and the author chose SPEC 11.1's reading — one named place, and a release to change it — over environment variables. One refusal per rule keeps the refusal of a badly broken drawing short. — *Rejected:* ambiguous characters as two cells; environment variables for the limits (SPEC 11.1, remark 23); a refusal for every faulty character, which a drawing of emoji would turn into hundreds. — *Where it lives:* `internal/domain/checks/drawing.go`, SPEC 5.4.

**R61. A task is reviewed in two halves, and every check that has its input runs.** `checks.Reviewer` has two methods. `Examine` reads the submission and runs its solver twice: the one slow part, and one that needs nothing from the profile. `Judge` runs every check of SPEC 5.1 in its order against the open request's brief and language, the child's grade and the profile's fingerprints, and is arithmetic. A check whose input is missing does not run and is listed as not checked, with what it needs; a check that has its input runs whatever failed before it. The first problem's code is the primary one. The outcome carries every problem, the checks that did not run, the types of the self-check's minor issues, and an event of codes and costs for the log line. — *Why:* 05-storage forbids anything slow between the profile's read and its write, and only the solver is slow, so it runs before the read. RUN.md had a fault of the structure stop every later check, as the prototype did; SPEC 5.1 reports every failed check at once, and the author chose that: a brief asking for another topic leaves the task whole, and a model told only about the topic would learn of a long sentence one attempt later, at a minute of a child's patience an attempt. — *Consequence:* the solver runs on a task a cheaper check has already refused, which costs at most its two runs; the line `task_submitted` is written where the attempt and the request are known, from `Outcome.Event()`. `Judge` judges only what it has the input for: given a submission `Examine` did not finish, or no brief of an open request, it returns an error rather than an outcome, like a failure of the sandbox, and only an outcome it returned can be accepted — the empty one a caller holds after skipping an error accepts nothing (T39a.1). — *Rejected:* the prototype's stop at the first fault of the structure; one call that reads the profile first, which would put the solver inside the window between the read and the write. — *Where it lives:* `internal/domain/checks/review.go`, `outcome.go`, `internal/app/container.go`, SPEC 5.1.

**R62. No refusal carries the model's words or the interpreter's.** A blocking issue of the self-check is pointed at by its place and its type. A solver that failed is told which way — it does not parse, has no solve to call, failed while it ran, returned something other than letters, or ran into a limit, which the sandbox's own sentence names. A solver that disagreed is told how, never with the letters. — *Why:* SPEC 5.8 handed the comment back, and the sandbox's message carries what the program or the interpreter said; either can name an option or a letter — "C and D are both right", `int("6 pairs")` — and a refusal reaches the widget (SPEC 5.1, R55). The model wrote the comment and the program and has both. The author chose this over handing them back with the letters cut out. — *Consequence:* a model whose program crashed is told that it crashed, not where; the instructions carry the dialect, so that it seldom has to guess. `solver.Result.Message` and `Agreement.Explain` stay what they are, diagnostics for whoever reads a run. — *Rejected:* handing the comment and the error back as they are; handing them back with letters and option texts cut out, which protects by cleaning a string rather than by never building it. — *Where it lives:* `internal/domain/checks/answer.go`, `selfcheck.go`, SPEC 5.7 and 5.8.

**R63. The package for the model is built by the content, as one JSON object.** *(The budget half is superseded by R65: the budget is 64 KB and nothing is dropped from a package. What stands is the rest — what a package holds, which reference tasks it shows and why their solvers stay out. Since R79 the reference tasks and the limits are those of the brief's level rather than of the child's grade, and the grade travels only as the child's age. Since R205 a package also names the idea of the topic the task is built on, a number made from the tasks of the topic the child has left behind.)* `content.Package` takes the brief, the corridor, the child's grade, interests and notes, the language and the answer count, and returns the JSON the model receives: the brief, the corridor, the topic, every trap, the prohibitions, the child, three reference tasks, the limits, the guide and the version of the instructions. The reference tasks are the topic's own at the child's level — the requested difficulty first, then the nearest, the easier of two equally near, and the level below where the topic has none — and take turns by the answer count. Their solvers stay out, and so does anything about past tasks. The limits come from the checks themselves, `checks.ReadabilityLimitsFor` and `checks.DefaultDrawingLimits`, so that what the model is told is what it is held to. Over the budget, the third reference task is left out, and a package still over it is sent as it is. — *Why:* everything in the package but the request's own values is the content's — catalogs, reference tasks, the guide, its version — and the content is what 01-context gives the job; the direction of the dependency is outward-in, content to domain, which R42 leaves open. JSON, because the budget is a number of bytes and the tests read exactly what the model reads; the parent's notes travel as a JSON string, which nothing inside can close. The solvers stay out by the author's choice over RUN.md's first wording (SPEC 1.5, R08): a reference task's own solver is a whole answer to copy, and the templates of T36a are the generalised form. Past tasks stay out because their text is not kept (О-40) and a fingerprint tells a model nothing. A package still over the budget once its third reference task is gone is sent rather than stripped further, by the author's choice: what carries it that far is the parent's own text at the profile's limits, another reference task dropped would buy a few hundred tokens of a rare profile with the quality of the task, and no drop could promise the budget anyway — in the characters JSON has to escape, a child at every limit is over it with no reference task at all. — *Consequence:* the guide is the largest part, about 7 KB. A child at every limit the profile sets, written in Latin letters, gets packages of 14.0 KB on average and none over the budget; the same limits in characters of four bytes reach 18.2 KB. The templates and frames of T36a and T36b add to all of it, and more packages will then lose their third example. — *Rejected:* building the package in a domain package behind interfaces over nearly all of the content; the JSON schemas instead of the guide's prose and worked example; the reference tasks' solvers; dropping reference tasks until a package fits, which trades the task's quality for a few hundred tokens and still cannot promise the budget. — *Where it lives:* `content/package.go`, `content/instructions/task_writing.md`, `internal/domain/checks/readability.go`, SPEC 4.1.

**R64. A solver template is a reference task's solver, generalised, and the bench proves it on that task.** R08 put the templates in `content/solvers/`; this is their form. Every topic that has reference tasks has one or two, in `content/solvers/<topic>/<name>.star`, and a package shows them in the order of their names as `solver_templates`. A template opens with the kind of question it fits and the idea of its search, names the task it came from on a line of its own — `# From reference task ord-34-d2-2.` — and keeps that task's numbers and names as capitals at its top, which is where a model puts its own; every word the solver matches against an option, a weekday or "It is impossible to tell", is one of those capitals too. The loader refuses a template it cannot place under a topic, one not named in lowercase words, an empty one, one that names no task or two of them, a task nobody has or a task of another topic, and a third template for one topic. The bench runs each template on the task it names, with the service's limits and both runs, and requires that task's answer; a test requires a template of every topic that has reference tasks. The templates count in the version of the instructions. — *Why:* keeping the source task's numbers is what makes a template provable at all: a sample with blanks in it cannot run, and one that merely runs could still teach a wrong search, so this way a template is a correct program to start from, which is the point of R08 — a failed solver costs the child a whole attempt. The words matched against an option are capitals because the task is written in the chat's language and `match` compares text as it stands, and the bench, running English reference tasks, could never catch a solver left matching "Tuesday" against "вторник". R63 kept the reference tasks' solvers out as whole answers to copy; a template is one such solver, but its numbers are marked as the ones to replace, the guide asks for the task first and for a search of the model's own whenever the task needs one, and a copied task is still met by the near-duplicate check at the child's level. The version counts the templates because they shape what the model writes as much as the guide does, and a rise in solver errors after a template changed should point at that change. — *Consequence:* the seven topics of `5-6` have no templates until their reference tasks exist (T37); the test turns red the day those arrive. The near-duplicate check compares a task with the reference tasks of its own level, while a template carries the numbers of a task of grades 1–4 to every level, so a grade 5–6 task that copied one is compared with nothing it came from (SPEC remark 32). — *Rejected:* blanks such as `{{N}}`, which cannot run and so cannot be proved; the source task's id as the file's name, which says nothing about what the template is for; a list kept by hand of the topics without a template, which goes stale the day a topic gains reference tasks — the rule is read off the content instead; templates for the seven new topics written from nothing, which О-43 rules out. — *Where it lives:* `content/template.go`, `content/solvers/`, `content/solver_test.go`, `content/package.go`, `content/instructions/task_writing.md`, `.gitattributes`, SPEC 4.1, 6.7 and 6.8.

**R65. The package budget is 64 KB, and nothing is dropped to meet it.** `content.PackageBudget` is 64 KB, up from 16, and `Package` sends every part whole. A test holds every package a profile allows to it — every topic, grade, difficulty and turn of the reference tasks, for a child at every limit the profile sets, written in Latin, Cyrillic, Chinese, characters of four bytes and the characters JSON has to escape. — *Why:* the author's decision: the context models read only gets cheaper, and the solver templates did not fit the old budget. A child at every limit in Latin letters was already at 16,371 of 16,384 bytes; with the third reference task dropped and the guide's paragraph on templates added, a template of pouring had about a hundred bytes where it needs nine hundred, and one of knights and liars about four hundred. Every choice left under 16 KB traded the quality of the task for a few hundred tokens of a rare profile, which is the trade R63 already refused for the reference tasks. At 64 KB the heaviest package a profile allows is 24.0 KB, so a drop could never fire, and a mechanism that cannot fire is code no test can hold honestly. — *Consequence:* R63's drop of the third reference task and SPEC 4.1's order of drops are withdrawn, and so is the drop of the drawing frames beyond the first that T36b would have added. An ordinary profile's package is 13.3 KB on average across the catalog and 17.1 KB at most. The model's own reason for a choice travels in the brief's `rationale` and has no limit until T44 gives it one (SPEC remark 29), so the test's promise covers everything the profile allows and nothing the model sends. — *Rejected:* dropping the first template as well, which kept the old promise by leaving the children with the fullest profiles without a template on the two topics that need one most; 17 KB, which fitted one template and would have been outgrown by the drawing frames. — *Where it lives:* `content/package.go`, `content/package_test.go`, SPEC 4.1.

**R66. A drawing frame is a generic drawing that passes the checks as it stands and once filled, and its filled form is a test fixture.** *(Its "no reference task has a drawing" no longer holds: since T37.1 the reference tasks of `geometry.grid` carry drawings of their own, shown with them and held to the same checks (R67). Everything else stands, the frames' twins among it. Since T37.4 there are ten: bars split into equal parts, for `ratio.sharing`, `fractions.parts` and `percent.basic`, and places in a ring, for `number.divisibility` (R68); since T37.5, eleven, with two overlapping groups for `logic.sets`, while `games.strategy` no longer carries the row, since its piles and counts stay words (R69).)* R09 put the frames in `content/drawings/`; this is their form. There are eight, one JSON file each — a number line, a row of objects with the gaps between them, a 3 by 3 and a 4 by 4 grid, a timetable, a balance, containers for pouring and a clock face — and each names the topics whose packages carry it and says what it is for and how it is filled. The drawing is a list of its lines, so the picture stands in the file as it is drawn. A number goes where the frame has a run of `#`, one character to each and right-aligned, a minus sign included; the labels are Latin capitals, set apart from every `#` and from each other by something that is neither a letter nor a digit; the structure holds no values; and there are no words, no units and none of the characters some platforms draw as wide colour emoji. A package carries the frames of its topic as `drawing_frames`, in the order of their names. Each frame has a twin in `content/testdata/drawings/`: the same frame filled with what the question of a reference task of one of its topics gives, the unknown marked `?`. The tests hold every frame and every filled twin to both drawing checks at the default limits, and to labels that agree with the structure both ways — every declared label drawn, everything drawn in letters declared. The frames count in the version of the instructions; their twins do not. The guide tells the model to start from a frame, to show what the question gives and nothing it asks, and to write no words or units in a drawing. — *Why:* no reference task has a drawing, so a frame is the only drawing the model is ever shown, and it has to be one that passes: a frame that failed a check would teach the model to fail it, and one that failed only once filled — a label run into its number — would fail every task drawn from it, which is why the filled twin is tested and not only the frame. The labels have to agree both ways because the check a submitted drawing passes looks only one way, from the structure to the picture, and a word left in a frame would be carried into a task in another language, where the allowed characters could not even write its translation. The child sees a drawing before answering, which the rules of the drawing had not said: a drawing that shows the answer spends the task. The filled twins are not shown to the model, by the author's choice, and a fixture is kept with the tests rather than in the binary, so that editing one neither changes the version nor needs the loader to know the reference tasks. — *Consequence:* the fixtures are the only record of how each frame is meant to be filled, and a frame without one fails its test. A model's own drawing may still use a character that shows as an emoji and pass the format check (SPEC remark 33). Of the seven topics of `5-6`, `geometry.grid` gets the grids and `games.strategy` the row, and the other five get none until their reference tasks show what they draw; every frame is shown at every level its topics are taught at. — *Rejected:* showing the filled twin beside the frame, which would be a reference task's numbers in every package and a second drawing to copy; the twin inside the frame's own file, which would put test data into the binary and a fixture edit into the version; words in place of `#`, which the model would carry into a task in another language; a fixture from the square flowerbed of `gaps-12-d4-4`, whose picture would show its answer. — *Where it lives:* `content/frame.go`, `content/drawings/`, `content/testdata/drawings/`, `content/frame_test.go`, `content/package.go`, `content/instructions/task_writing.md`, `.gitattributes`, SPEC 4.1 and 4.4.

**R67. A reference task of grades 5–6 passes what a task the model writes must pass, wherever that applies to it.** *(From T37.2 on, the wrong options are also labelled with the rule in mind. A child new to a topic is given the two traps most frequent among its reference tasks, ties going to the catalog's order, in which the traps added for `5-6` come last; so the two most frequent are made the mistakes most typical of the topic, and a test pins each topic's pair, since one relabelled option can tip a count. `logic.sets` gives `double_count` and `answered_other_question`, `games.strategy` `ignored_condition` and `first_move_assumed`, `fractions.parts` `part_whole_swap` and `answered_other_question`, `percent.basic` `percent_wrong_base` and `part_whole_swap`, `ratio.sharing` `ratio_total_confusion` and `wrong_operation`, and `number.divisibility` `remainder_vs_quotient` and `off_by_one`. `geometry.grid`'s `double_count` and `missed_case` are the typical mistakes of counting cells, and it stays as it is. For the fraction tasks, T37.3 widened `part_whole_swap` in the catalog to a fraction of one taken as if it were a fraction of the other — a third of what was left taken as a third of the pack — which its old wording, answering with the part for the whole, did not cover; a percentage taken of the wrong quantity stays `percent_wrong_base`. From T38.1 `logic.ordering` gives `ignored_condition` and `reversed_relation`, as at `3-4`, and `logic.knights_liars` `trusted_statement` and `ignored_condition`, the pair of `3-4` in the other order. From T38.2 `counting.gaps` gives `off_by_one` and `wrong_operation`, as at `3-4`, and `combinatorics.enumeration` `ignored_condition` and `missed_case` where `3-4` has `wrong_operation` first: most of its tasks of `5-6` restrict what may be counted, often twice over, and dropping a restriction is the mistake they invite most. From T38.3 `time.clocks` gives `ignored_condition` and `stopped_early` where `3-4` has `wrong_operation` and `off_by_one`: its tasks of `5-6`, about time zones, midnight and clocks that run fast or slow, take several steps, and a forgotten difference, day or length, or an answer given at a step on the way, such as the time at home, are the mistakes they invite most; ahead taken for behind comes next, one behind. From T39.1 `arithmetic.tricks` gives `stopped_early` and `wrong_operation`, as at `3-4`, level at eight each, with the catalog's order putting `stopped_early` first; `time.calendar` gives `ignored_condition` and `answered_other_question` where `3-4` has `off_by_one` and `ignored_condition`: its tasks of `5-6` ask ages in the past and the future, and an age of the wrong person or of the wrong year is the mistake they invite next after a forgotten leap day. From T39.2 `parity.alternation` gives `wrong_parity` and `ignored_condition`, as at `3-4`, and `pigeonhole.basic` `best_case_not_worst` and `off_by_one` where `3-4` has `best_case_not_worst` and `stopped_early`: each of its tasks of `5-6` offers the lucky draw, and the worst case counted without the one more that settles it is the mistake they invite next. From T39.3 `algorithms.weighing_pouring` gives `missed_case` and `ignored_condition` where `3-4` has `ignored_condition` and `off_by_one`: its tasks of `5-6` ask for the fewest steps or weighings, so a longer way than the shortest is the mistake they invite most, and a rule of the task left out, such as a pan that holds two marbles or water that may not be poured away, the next. T39.3 also widened `missed_case` in the catalog to a shorter way than the one found, which the tasks of `3-4` already used it for, since a model reading only "missed an option while enumerating" would write tasks of a different mistake under the pair. T39.1 leaves the catalog's descriptions of topics as they are, the same at every level: the focus SPEC 1.2 gives a topic at `5-6`, such as digit puzzles for `arithmetic.tricks` or time zones for `time.clocks`, reaches the model through the reference tasks of that level, which is where its package takes its examples from, while a wider description would reach children of `1-2` as well. T38.1 also settles the question left below: the knights tasks open with the paragraph of rules of `3-4` and stay in the near-duplicate test. That paragraph alone brings two questions to 0.56, their closest pair measures 0.60 once each question ends in words of its own, and no exception is needed; an exception would let two reference tasks come closer to each other than a model's task may come to either.)* Every reference task already has its answer proved on the bench and its explanations held to the check of the wrong options (R57). The tasks of `5-6`, written for this service, are held to more:
- each has a hint;
- its question reads within the limits of grade 5, the strictest of the level;
- no two of them are near-duplicates by the threshold a new task is refused at;
- a drawing passes both drawing checks against its own question, with its labels agreeing with its structure both ways;
- each topic that has them has exactly three at each of difficulties 2, 3 and 4.

The tests say so. The prototype's corpus is compared with Postgres's numbers on its own 450 questions only, chosen by level before any pair is measured. The batch of the seven new topics is written in four parts, each planned first as a table of ideas the author approves, and `geometry.grid`, which could have failed its entry exam, went first and passed it. — *Why:* a reference task is what the model imitates, so one that failed a check would teach the model to fail it. That is why R57 held all 450 to the explanations. The ported tasks were never written to the other checks, and a fifth of them would fail readability — but the new ones can be written to pass from the start, and cost nothing more for it. Near-duplicates among them matter for a reason of their own: a new task is refused when it comes too close to any reference task of the child's level, so two reference tasks too close to each other would leave a model that followed either one refused as a copy of the other. The first draft of the grid tasks showed that — two questions phrased alike measured 0.915 — and varying the wording brought the closest pair to 0.41. The comparison with Postgres is restricted because its golden numbers describe the 450 questions it measured: a nearest neighbour among the new tasks, or a new pair among the fifty most alike, would be a disagreement with a corpus Postgres never saw. The questions are chosen before the pairs are made, so that the pairs between grades 1–2 and 3–4 it did measure are all still there. — *Consequence:* the drawings of the grid tasks are the first real drawings among the reference tasks, and the model sees them with the tasks — unlike the frames' filled twins, which stay test fixtures (R66). The rule suggests a new child the two most frequent traps of a topic's reference tasks, and for `geometry.grid` those are `double_count` and `missed_case`, with the topic's own `area_perimeter_swap` fourth; the model may swap it in. The test of the cells names the topics still waiting for their tasks of `5-6` rather than failing on them, until T39 completes the level. `logic.knights_liars` at `5-6` will open its questions with the same paragraph of rules as at `3-4`, and T38 decides whether it is excepted from the near-duplicate test on R56's reasoning. — *Rejected:* holding the new tasks to the explanations and the bench alone, as the ported ones are, which would let the model imitate a task too long for its reader or too close to another; a list of the new tasks' ids to restrict the Postgres comparison, where the level says the same and needs no upkeep; writing all 63 tasks in one review. — *Where it lives:* the tasks of `5-6` in `content/examples/` (ids with `-56-`), their solvers `content/examples/solvers/*-56-*.star` and the templates of their topics in `content/solvers/`, `content/catalogs/traps.json`, `content/content_test.go`, `content/checks_test.go`, `content/rule_test.go`, `content/frame_test.go`, `internal/domain/checks/corpus_test.go`, RUN.md (T37.1–T37.4, T38.1–T38.3, T39.1), SPEC 1.3, 1.5 and 1.6.

**R68. A task carries a text drawing wherever it has a picture in it.** *(Its "draw whenever the task has a picture in it" is narrowed by R69 since T37.5: a task draws when a child solving it would, and not when the picture would give the answer away, take the key step or only repeat the words.)* The first rule of the topic catalog, "words only" — a task statable in words, with no picture and no spatial imagination — is gone, and a topic enters the catalog on the one rule that a program can check its tasks. The model is told to draw whenever the task has a picture in it — a grid, a number line, places in a ring, the bars of a ratio — and never to decorate. The reference tasks draw where their task has a picture, and the frames grow to ten: bars split into equal parts, for `ratio.sharing`, `fractions.parts` and `percent.basic`, and places in a ring, for `number.divisibility`. — *Why:* the author's decision. The rule dates from before it was settled what a picture in a task would be. Drawings have since become text, drawn by the model and checked by the service for their format and for agreeing with the wording (О-11, О-11а, SPEC 5.4, R66), so a picture no longer takes a task outside what a chat can show or the service can check. A set of reference tasks without drawings teaches a model not to draw, even where a child would reach for a picture: bars for a ratio, a ring for going round. — *Consequence:* the reference tasks of `5-6` draw from T37.4 on, and T37.5 adds drawings to the tasks of T37.1–T37.4 that a child would draw (R69). The cells of a grid keep the names SPEC 1.6 gives them, now as a rule for labelling rather than a way of doing without the drawing. The prototype's D05 exclusions are a decision of their own, and they stand. — *Rejected:* keeping pictures to the grid topic alone. — *Where it lives:* SPEC 1.2, 1.6 and 4.4, PRODUCT (the focus of the topics), `content/instructions/task_writing.md`, `content/drawings/`, `content/testdata/drawings/`, RUN.md (T37.5).

**R69. A task draws when a child solving it would draw.** *(Since T39.3 there are 153 reference tasks of `5-6`, and 24 of them draw; RUN.md T37–T39 says why the new ones that do not draw do not. For the reference tasks T38.1 adds the author's rule that one which fits no frame of its topic, or not the limits of a drawing, does not draw, and its words carry all it needs: a row of seven places is wider than 30 cells, and a row cut short with `...` hides the middle an ordering task is about. A model's own drawing stays allowed, as the guide says.)* Three kinds of task have a picture in them: where things stand — cells of a grid, a row, a ring, a number line, rows of seats; parts of a whole — bars of equal parts for a ratio, a fraction or a percentage; and groups that overlap — two boxes sharing a region. A task does not draw when it is about numbers alone — a remainder, digits, a price after a change, a pile in a game, which a picture would only write out again — nor when the picture would give the answer away or take the task's key step, as a grid of cells does when the area is asked, or bars cut into the common parts two fractions need; nor when it would only repeat the words. Bars show the parts the question names: when a number the question gives belongs to a part the child has to work out first — the rest of a whole, what is left after a step — tying the two together is the key step, and the task does not draw. The question carries every fact the task needs, the drawing shows what the question gives and adds nothing, and the labels it draws are named in the question: "the football club (F)". Of the 63 reference tasks of `5-6`, 20 draw by this rule from T37.5 on; RUN.md T37.5 says why the others do not. — *Why:* the author left the rule to be decided here. A child's own sketch is the measure because that is what a picture in a task is for: it shows the arrangement a child would otherwise have to draw before thinking. The facts stay in the words because the solver and the self-check work from the words and the drawing check reads only labels, so a fact that lived only in the picture could be drawn one way and solved another, and no check would see it; and a child reading the task aloud in text mode, or through a screen reader, still has all of it. — *Consequence:* a game's pile, an abstract number and a price change stay words. No reference task of `percent.basic` draws: each is about numbers alone, or its given number belongs to a part the child works out first, as the three guests who chose strawberry belong to the tenth the others leave; the bar frame still serves the topic. A quantity shared by two ratios, as the tulips of the flower stall are, is drawn the same length in both pairs, each pair with parts of its own width: with one width for all the parts, the picture would show one quantity at two sizes. The frame for two groups writes each group's size beside its label, outside its box, and the number of everyone on the border: a size written inside the part of a box that only its group has reads as the count of that part, and adding the parts then counts the overlap twice, the very mistake the topic tests. Three overlapping groups are not drawn: three boxes do not fit a phone card legibly, and counts given for pairs include those in all three, which a picture by regions would misstate. The bead bags of `ratio-56-d4-3` are not drawn either: drawn to scale the cells give the answer by counting, and drawn out of scale the picture needs a key that says which shading is red, which a drawing has no words for. — *Rejected:* drawing in every task that could have a picture, which turns the picture into decoration or into the solution; leaving the call to each task's author, which is how the reference tasks came to draw almost nothing. — *Where it lives:* SPEC 4.4, `content/instructions/task_writing.md`, `content/examples/` (the tasks that draw), `content/drawings/venn.json`, RUN.md (T37.5, T38.1–T38.3, T39.1–T39.3).

**R138. A drawing's limits are the widget card's measured ones: 30 cells, 12 lines, the WGL4 part of the drawing blocks, and every label named in the question.** T58 measured the widget's own card in Chromium — the engine of Android's WebView and of Chrome — at the widths of the hosts' cards, in three monospaced fonts: Liberation Mono, whose characters are WGL4's as those of Droid Sans Mono and Consolas are; DejaVu Sans Mono, whose metrics Menlo and SF Mono share; and Noto Sans Mono. The width stays 30 cells: that is what the drawing holds without scrolling sideways on a card 320 px wide, a phone 360 px wide once a host's margins are off, while Claude's phone card of 353 px holds 34, a card of 390 px 39 and the web's 79; a wider drawing scrolls inside its frame. The height stays 12 lines: with twelve, the question, the drawing and the five options take 664 px of a phone card 390 px wide, about the chat such a screen shows. The characters narrow from four whole blocks, 368 characters, to 65, `checks.DrawingCharacters`: the light and double lines of box drawing, the block elements ▀▄█▌▐░▒▓, the shapes ■□▬▲►▼◄◊○●◘◙◦ and the arrows ←↑→↓ — the part of those blocks in WGL4 that measured one cell wide in all three fonts, less ↔ ↕ ▪ ▫, which are also emoji. The rest — heavy, rounded and diagonal lines, double arrows, most shapes — is missing from some of the fonts and is drawn from another, out of line with the ASCII around it: ▲△▲△ ran 36 px long of a 29-cell ruler, ⇐⇒ 104 px. The package hands the model the list as `limits.drawing.characters`, and the guide sends it there. The clock frame's diagonal hands, drawn with `╱` and `╲`, are now drawn with `/` and `\`. And a label the structure declares must be named in the question, read softly: as a word of its own, with no Latin letter or digit joined to it — in a script written without spaces too, 点A在3, and in A-D, A's and the Turkish A'dan — unless it stands straight after a number, as a unit does; among capitals written together; in the name of a cell; or inside a range, two declared labels of its kind with a dash, dots or at most three words between them that neither list nor reckon — rows A to D, A부터 D까지, cells A1 to D4, but not points A and D. The refusal counts the unnamed labels and quotes none. This reading parts from the one that looks for labels the structure lacks on purpose: that one takes a capital for a label only where it surely is one, this one takes a label for named wherever it may be, since each refuses when it errs the other way. A frame, which has no question, is not held to it, and the frames of the clock, the balance and pouring no longer insist on their letters: name them, or leave them out and let the numbers the question gives be the labels. — *Why:* О-11а asked for the size to be found by trying real widgets. The author chose a calibration Claude runs itself, with the devices' confirmation left to T62 and T63. ↔ ↕ ▪ ▫ go because the measurement had neither WebKit nor a phone's own font, and a phone whose font lacks one draws it from another, perhaps as a colour emoji two cells wide. The reverse check follows a live run of `just play`, twelve tasks in the ten topics that have frames: the model drew four, as R69 asks, and one of them — sweets shared between Anya and Boris, as bars labelled A and B — left the child to guess which letter was whose. Its reading was tried on a range as each of the 22 languages of the cards writes one, and on a label opening a sentence in each of their Latin scripts: a list of the words that span a range knew those of ten languages and refused a range as the other twelve write it; a hyphen read as joining a word refused rows A-D in all of them; and the English article, read in every Latin script, refused the label A opening a sentence in the ten other languages written in one — A y B, the Turkish A kutusunda. So the reading lists what breaks a range rather than what makes one, and reads no article. The 24 reference drawings name every label they draw; eight of the eleven filled frames did not, so they were filled anew: four for other reference tasks, whose questions name the labels, and balance, pouring, clock-face and number-line, whose topics have no such task or whose question's A is its article, with the numbers their questions give as labels and the letters they have no use for left out. — *Rejected:* 34 cells, which fits Claude's phone card but scrolls on the most common Android phones; heights of 16 or 20 lines, which push the options off the screen; keeping the blocks whole, whose characters run out of line on Android and Windows; the 81 characters that measured one cell in all three fonts, of which twelve are outside WGL4 — Liberation Mono carries them, Droid Sans Mono and Consolas need not; keeping ↔ ↕ ▪ ▫ on Chromium's word alone; a strict reverse check that reads only words, which would refuse the cells and ranges every grid task names; any label between two named ones counted as named, which let points A and D pass for B and C; a list of the words that span a range, and the English article and I in this reading, which refused ordinary tasks in most languages of the cards. — *Consequence:* SPEC remark 33 closes with none of the characters some platforms draw as colour emoji left in the set. The reverse reading lets through what a word or two of relation between two ends hides — A beats D names B and C — and refuses a range spelled out at more length, from A all the way to D; the acceptance runs in the chat languages show how often. The measurements are Chromium's with fonts that stand in for the phones' own; T62 and T63 confirm them on Claude's and ChatGPT's cards with the calibration set of `just drawings`. — *Where it lives:* `internal/domain/checks/drawing.go` and `drawingmatch.go`; `content/package.go`, `content/instructions/task_writing.md`, the frames `clock-face`, `balance` and `pouring` in `content/drawings/`, `content/testdata/drawings/`; `cmd/drawings/`; SPEC 4.1 and 5.4 and remark 33; `docs/live/06-drawings.md`; RUN.md (T58).

**R71. A run that stops at a `%` format Starlark cannot fill is told so.** When a run fails, the sandbox takes the position the interpreter stopped at from the top of its call stack and looks at the source there. If that is a `%` whose left side is a string written out, or a name the program binds once, at its top level, to a string written out, and Starlark cannot fill that string — a stray `%`, a width or a precision, a number of values it does not ask for, a key with no dictionary to find it in — the run is `bad_format`, and the model is told, in a sentence of the service's own, how `%` is written in this dialect. Any other failure stays `error`. The guide lists `%` among the differences from Python that bite. — *Why:* models write Python, whose `%` has widths and precisions, and a task about percentages puts a `%` into every message. The interpreter's own sentence, "unknown conversion", never reaches the model (R62), so such a run was told only that the solver failed while it ran, which sent the model looking through its logic for a mistake in a string. A position is exact where the library's sentence is not: it is the operator the run could not carry out, and reading it ties the verdict to the source rather than to wording the library may change, just as the limits are read from the budgets. — *Consequence:* a stray `%` that a run never reaches — in a `fail()` for a case that does not happen — costs nothing, and the program still proves its answer. A string put together while the program runs is not read, and a run that stops at one is `error`. The programs the content ships are held to the same check throughout, reached or not, because models copy them; their names are bound the way a run binds them, so a local that shadows such a name, or a function of the program's own named after a built-in, is not taken for it. — *Rejected:* escaping `%` in the model's program, which cannot tell a percent sign from a conversion and would change a program the model wrote; checking before the run, which would refuse a program that proves its answer over a string it never reaches, at the cost of an attempt of the child's (R59); reading the interpreter's message. — *Where it lives:* `internal/infra/starlark/format.go` and `classify.go`, `internal/domain/solver/solver.go`, `internal/domain/checks/answer.go`, `content/instructions/task_writing.md`, `content/format_test.go`, SPEC 6.3, 6.4 and 6.7.

**R78. A tag that names neither a language nor a script leaves a task to its letters.** Whether a language tag names anything is read from what it says before anything is inferred: `und` alone, the language not determined, and a tag of private words alone name nothing, and a task tagged so is counted in the unit its letters call for, as a task with no tag is. `und-Hans` names its script and is read by it, and `und-CN` its place, from which the script is inferred as before. A tag whose script cannot be told at all — `mul`, several languages, or a language the library has no script for — is left to the letters too, rather than read a word at a time, and so is a tag whose script is one of the codes that stand in for none, such as `Zyyy` for several in common use. — *Why:* asked for the language of `und`, the tag library answers English as its likeliest guess, so a Chinese task tagged `und` was counted a word at a time, and the test for `und` in the code never fired. — *Where it lives:* `internal/domain/checks/script.go`, `internal/domain/checks/readability_test.go`.

**R210. The texts a child reads are held to the letters of the lesson's language.** `submit_task` refuses with `wrong_language` a task whose question, hint or solution, or whose four explanations behind the wrong options taken together, are mostly in other letters than those the lesson's language is written in. A text is counted as a child reads it — a word at a time, or a character at a time in a script written without spaces — and is refused when less than half of what is counted is in the lesson's letters; half passes, and a text with nothing counted says nothing. Numbers, signs, the letters every script shares, words of Latin capitals alone, short words in small Latin letters — three at most — and single Greek letters are left out of the count, so that labels such as AB, an unknown x, a product ab, the orders abc and acb, a unit such as cm and π cost a task nothing. The options are not held to it. The letters are those of the script the tag names, or of the one its language is written in as a rule — what the tag library reports with exact or high confidence — and Han for Chinese at any confidence, since its two likely scripts are one set of characters; a language written in several scripts whose tag names none — Serbian, Uzbek, Azerbaijani and Mongolian, and Kazakh, Malay, Punjabi and Bosnian, of which the library reports one script as sure — a script outside those of the cards' languages, and a tag that names none hold a task to nothing. The check runs second, after the structure. The words of `get_package`, the guide and the server's instructions say that every text the child reads is written in the lesson's language, though the package is in English (SPEC 5.11). — *Why:* in a live run reported on 2026-10-05, Haiku 4.5 wrote the question and the options of a task for a lesson in Russian in English, and the service showed them. The model had the language as a bare tag in a few sentences of a package that is otherwise English: the reference tasks, the solver templates, the guide and its worked example. R144 decided which language a lesson is in, and nothing held a task to it. Counting letters is arithmetic, not a judgement of meaning, so it stays within О-46; a refusal costs one attempt and is mended by writing the task again. Units rather than letters, because a Chinese question naming Tom and Mary has more Latin letters than characters. — *Rejected:* a library that detects languages: it guesses on texts of ten to thirty words with names and numbers in them, a wrong guess refuses a good task — the objection of О-46 and О-36 — and its models weigh megabytes. Judging the options, which may be labels or names in any language. The instructions alone: the guide already named the language, and the model wrote in English. A stricter share, and a count of letters rather than units, either of which refuses a Chinese question with Latin names. Holding a language written in several scripts to its likely one, which refuses Serbian written in Latin and Kazakh written in Latin. Counting every Latin word of two letters or more, which refuses a Russian solution that lists the orders abc, acb and bac, or a hint in Hindi that measures in cm. — *Consequence:* two languages written in the same letters are not told apart — English in a lesson in German, Russian in one in Ukrainian — and there the words to the model are all there is. An English text of short words alone, such as "is it so", has nothing counted and passes. The service has ten checks: the page "Research" counts `checks.Codes()`, while the paper says nine until it is measured again. — *Where it lives:* `internal/domain/checks/language.go`, `script.go`, `review.go`, `checks.go`; `internal/transport/mcp/getpackage.go`; `content/instructions/task_writing.md`, `mcp_instructions.md`; SPEC 4.1.1, 5.1, 5.9, 5.11, 7.3, 7.5; `docs/architecture/03-flows.md`.

## Widgets and showing progress

**R11. There is no "advice for the adult" block in v1.** The task card stays a child's card: no hidden block of leading questions, no widget-only tool for it, no questions in the sealed block. — *Why:* the author's decision (О-45) — the adult guides the child themselves and gets leading questions from the model in the chat. — *Consequence:* task T55a is deleted and the wording of PRODUCT section 1 is rewritten; if the idea comes back, it goes through an open question in 12.2 again. — *Where it lives:* PRODUCT 1, 2, 4.2.

**R12. A rank above the rating number.** *(Since T81 a rank is told by its number, with no name, and the overall course marks the grades each run of ranks roughly matches: R213.)* *(Since T74.1 the rating's number is drawn nowhere on the progress — the model says it, and the result of an answer shows it: R162.)* *(Since T62.6 a topic has a rank of its own again, beside the overall one, and the rating's number is shown at the overall rank alone: R147.)* *(Its five steps are superseded by R79: eleven of the same width cover the whole ladder of grades 1–6, and none is shown during the trial series. R95 keeps one rank, above the overall rating, and none above a topic's. The rest stands.)* Five steps, with boundaries computed from the rating corridor and names taken from the locale dictionaries. The rank is not stored in the profile: it is a matter of presentation, not data. — *Why:* "1500" means nothing to a seven-year-old, and the scale itself does not change (PRODUCT 4.5). — *Where it lives:* T57; the keys in T54, the translations in T59 (О-48).

**R13. The rhythm of practice is not shown.** *(Since T74.4 the review of the progress advises a rhythm — two or three short tasks a day — for a topic that fell, and nowhere else: R176.)* No counter of tasks solved today in the profile, no counting them from the tail of the history, no streaks, no penalties for skipping and no reminders. — *Why:* the author's decision (О-49) — progress stays about knowledge, not about volume; pushing a 7–10-year-old towards regularity is out of place (PRODUCT 10). — *Where it lives:* T09 and T57 stay unchanged.

**R70. The task card shows its options without their letters.** *Withdrawn by R90: the letters are back on the buttons.* The five answer buttons carry the options' texts and nothing in front of them. The letters `A`–`E` stay the task's keys — `options`, `correct_answer`, the explanations, what the solver returns and what `submit_answer` records — and the widget never shows them to the child. The texts the child reads name an option by its value, never by its letter. Text mode keeps the letters, because there the child types one to answer (О-42). — *Why:* the author's decision. A drawing labels what it shows with Latin capitals — the apples A, the rows A to C of a grid, the boxes A and B — and a child who sees "A 18" in the picture above a button marked A can take one for the other. Taking the letters off the buttons ends the clash in one place, in a widget not yet written. — *Consequence:* a hint, a solution or an explanation that said "option C" would point at nothing on the card, so the guide tells the model to name an option by its value. In text mode the option letters and the labels still share an alphabet. — *Rejected:* keeping the drawings' labels out of `A`–`E`, which would rename the cells of the grid (SPEC 1.6), the labels of every frame and the rule the model is given for labelling. — *Where it lives:* PRODUCT 3 and 4.2, SPEC 8.2, `docs/architecture/01-context.md` and `03-flows.md`, `content/instructions/task_writing.md`, RUN.md (T55).

**R89. The design in `draft/ui/mathtrail-site/` is the reference for the site and the widget.** *(Replaced by R156: the export was removed on 2026-10-03, once the widget matched it, and is in the history up to `8ff72f8`; the site's reference is the author's new draft, `draft/ui/site/`, and the card's is its own code.)* *(The site's colours are its own since R142; for the site the draft still set what its pages held and how they were arranged.)* The author's approved static export of the home page, in `en` and `ru`, with the widget's whole card built in: `assets/tokens.css` holds the colours of the light and the dark theme and the sizes — widths of 360 and 640 px, a tap target of 44 px, an option at least 48 px high — `assets/mathtrail.css` and `mathtrail.js` the widget's components under the `mt-` prefix, and `assets/site.css` and `site.js` the page and its demo. The widget and the site are built from it, not near it: the components are ported to Preact one by one, with their names, their classes and their states, and the tokens are one file the widget, the site and the consent screen share. The draft's own React 18, loaded from `assets/vendor/`, is how the export runs without a build; it ships nowhere. What the draft shows but a host cannot do is named in SPEC 8.12, and the product follows the host there, not the picture. — *Why:* the author's decision: the design was drawn and approved as a whole, and a widget written against a description of it would drift from it screen by screen. — *Consequence:* R70 is withdrawn (R90); the widget reads the progress itself and sends the rest to the chat (R91); the waiting screen walks through steps it cannot observe (R92); "I don't know" is an answer (R93). Screens the draft does not draw — the first sign-in, the trial series, the rating before and after an answer, the daily limit, three failed attempts, the widget in a language written right to left — are drawn with its components and in its style, and the author sees them before they ship. `themes.png` beside the export shows one card in both themes, to make plain how they differ; the author prefers the export's cards to it, so it illustrates and does not decide — its options lack their letters and its header names the topic, and neither goes into the card. — *Where it lives:* `draft/ui/mathtrail-site/`, SPEC 8.2, 8.3 and 8.12, RUN.md (T43, T45, T48, T54–T57a, T59, T66, T66a).

**R90. The answer buttons carry their letters.** Each of the five buttons shows the option's letter, `A`–`E`, beside its text, as the design draws it; this withdraws R70. — *Why:* the author's decision, taken with the design: the letter is what the child says aloud to the adult beside them, and what they type in text mode, so one card and one chat use one name for an option. — *Consequence:* the clash R70 ended is back — a drawing that labels its points `A`–`E` above buttons marked with the same letters. The guide still tells the model to name an option by its value rather than its letter, and that stays true; only its stated reason, a card without letters, no longer holds, and it is reworded when the widget's screens are written. — *Where it lives:* PRODUCT 3 and 4.2, SPEC 8.2, `content/instructions/task_writing.md`, RUN.md (T55).

**R91. The widget reads the progress itself, and asks for everything else through the chat.** *(Since R156 the site's demo shows a question and the model's reply as messages of the chat beneath the card, labelled as an illustration, rather than inside it.)* *(Since T62.7 "Edit" at the profile's head opens a form in the card, which `edit_profile` saves, and a profile is made in the chat: R148.)* *(Since T62.4 the task card has no question field: R145.)* The line at the top of the card opens the progress in the same card: the widget calls `read_progress` with `callServerTool` — the tool of R97, which returns what `get_progress` does and draws no card — and the progress carries the profile's fields its screen shows, so one call that only reads draws it all. What changes something stays with the model: the field "Ask a question about the task" and "Edit profile" send a message to the chat with `sendMessage`, and the model's answer arrives in the chat under the card, as any answer does; the card keeps only a note that the question was sent. — *Why:* the author's decision. Reading the progress spends no turn of the conversation and needs no model; a question needs the model, and the model's words never reach a widget, so a card that showed an answer inside itself would be showing something it cannot have. The site's demo, which has no model, shows an answer inside the card, and says it is an illustration. — *Rejected:* opening the progress by a message the model answers with a new card, which spends a turn and leaves two cards where the design has one. — *Where it lives:* SPEC 7.1, 7.3, 8.2, 8.3, 8.11 and 8.12, `docs/architecture/01-context.md` and `03-flows.md`, RUN.md (T43, T55, T57, T66a).

**R92. The waiting screen walks through the steps a task usually takes, and claims none of them.** *(Since T62.8 the steps follow how the service says the task stands, as the card `next_task` draws asks it: R152.)* *(Since T62.5 the steps follow the call as the host tells of it, not a clock: R146.)* "Preparing the next task…" lists the steps — topic and difficulty picked, the task being written, every answer checked, readability for the grade, ready — and moves through them on a timer, with no numbers in them: no count of cases, since the card cannot know it. After 120 seconds the deadline message replaces the list, as before. — *Why:* the author's decision. The card cannot observe the work: the model writes the task and the checks run inside the model's calls, and the finished task arrives in a new card. A list that moves shows the child that something is happening; a number in it would be a claim nobody measured. — *Rejected:* steps reported by the service from the refusals of `submit_task`, which would be true but reach the card only through its own refusals, and would cost T44 and T56 a channel for it. — *Where it lives:* SPEC 8.2, RUN.md (T56).

**R93. "I don't know" is an answer, and a wrong one.** *(Since 2026-10-05 the card has no button for it: the child says it in the chat, where the model records it as `?`, and a card shows it only as an answer recorded before: R208.)* Pressed before answering, it shows the right option and the solution, so it is recorded like an answer: `submit_answer` with `answer: "?"`, which the server records as incorrect, with `confused` set and no `chosen` and no trap. The rating reads it as a wrong answer; the misconception map does not, since no trap was taken. After an answer the button is gone: the question field (R91) is how the child asks why. — *Why:* the author's decision. A card that has shown the answer cannot then take an answer, and a child who has seen the solution has not solved the task; counting it as wrong keeps the rating an honest estimate. It does not stop a child leafing through hard tasks: "Another task" before an answer leaves the rating alone, and what shows it is the record of R98. — *Consequence:* О-33 narrows — the hint and the pace still stay out of the formula, "I don't know" does not; `confused` keeps its name in the profile and now means this press. — *Rejected:* a skip that leaves the rating alone, which would need a third outcome in the profile and the formula; keeping "I don't understand" as a request for a simpler telling, which the design does not have. — *Where it lives:* SPEC 2.2, 7.2, 8.2, 8.3 and 8.11, `docs/architecture/01-context.md`, `03-flows.md` and `04-profile.md`, `content/instructions/mcp_instructions.md` (rewritten in T45), `docs/usage-evidence-for-grants.md`, PRODUCT О-33, RUN.md (T45, T55, T68.1).

**R94. The design tokens live in `internal/widget/tokens.css`.** The draft's `tokens.css` is ported once, beside the widget's page, and all three of its readers take it from there: the service embeds it with `go:embed` for the consent screen, the widget imports it when Vite builds `web/`, and the site's build copies it as a file of its own. The image's widget stage copies that one file with `web/`. — *Why:* the author's decision. `go:embed` reaches only files under the embedding package's directory, and the image sees neither `site/` nor anything outside `web/` in its widget stage, so `site/assets/tokens.css` could not serve the service or the widget; `internal/widget/` is already in both builds and already holds what the widget is made into. — *Rejected:* a copy per reader, which drifts on the third edit; `site/assets/tokens.css`, which neither the service nor the image's widget stage reaches. — *Where it lives:* SPEC 8.12, RUN.md (T48, T55, T66).

**R95. One rank, above the overall rating.** *(Superseded by R147: every topic has a rank of its own beside the overall one.)* *(Its pips are eleven, as the author chose on seeing both: R135.)* The progress screen shows the overall rating — the chess number of θ (SPEC 2.4) — with its rank above it, as the design draws it, and each topic's rating below as a plain number with no rank of its own. The rank counts eleven steps (R79), so the design's "Rank 3 of 5" reads "3 of 11" with the rank's name; whether its pips become eleven or go, the author sees when the screen is ported. This narrows R12, which put a rank above every rating. — *Why:* the author's decision, taken with the design: one rank is something a child remembers and moves up; a rank on every topic crowds a row at 320 px and says little a number beside it does not. — *Rejected:* a rank on each topic's row; topics alone, with no overall number. — *Where it lives:* SPEC 7.1 and 8.2, RUN.md (T57).

**R96. The task card is told whose it is.** Every result of `submit_task`, accepted or refused, carries `child` — the pseudonym and the grade — beside the task, never inside its text: the task card's top line and badge read them, and so does the waiting screen a refusal draws. `submit_task` has already read the profile, so this costs no call. — *Why:* the author's decision: the design shows the pseudonym on every card, and the payload is the only thing a card has when it opens. — *Rejected:* the card reading the profile as it opens, a Drive read and a wait on every task; a top line with no name, which departs from the design. — *Consequence:* the pseudonym now travels in a widget payload, as it already did in the progress and the profile; the rules that keep it out of the task's text and out of logs and spans stand. — *Where it lives:* SPEC 7.3 and 8.2, RUN.md (T44, T55).

**R97. The widget reads the progress through a tool of its own, `read_progress`.** *(Since T62.7 a second tool of the card alone, `edit_profile`, saves the profile's form: R148.)* It returns what `get_progress` returns, carries `ui.visibility: ["app"]` and no `ui.resourceUri`: the model does not see it, and no host has a card to draw for it. `get_progress` stays the model's, and draws the progress card as before. — *Why:* the author's decision. The protocol does not say whether a host draws a card for a call the widget makes to a tool that draws one; a tool that draws none leaves no second card to find out about, and costs one small entry in the list and one shared handler. Whether each host hides an app-only tool from the model and still lets the widget call it is not settled by this; the live runs of T62 and T63 check both. — *Rejected:* calling `get_progress` from the widget and settling the question in the live run of T62, which would leave the design's one card at the mercy of each host. — *Where it lives:* SPEC 7.1, 7.2, 7.3, 8.2 and 8.3, remark 16, `docs/architecture/01-context.md`, `03-flows.md` and `05-storage.md`, RUN.md (T43, T57, T62).

**R98. A skipped task is recorded, and the rating ignores it.** *(Since T62.6 how many were skipped is one number the service counts, in all, and the words for the model say it and name the same latest entries as the card: R147. Since R205 a topic's count of skips, added to its answers, also picks the idea of the topic the next task is built on.)* When `next_task` is asked for a new task while the current one has no answer, the current one is recorded as skipped — whether or not a new task ever arrives: an entry in the history window with `skipped` set and no `correct`, a count per topic, and a `task_skipped` event in the log. Nothing that reads answers reads it — not the rating, the trial series, the misconception map, the streaks or the rule — and the progress screen shows it: "Skipped" in the recent answers, and how many were skipped. Skips share the window with answers: when an entry has to go, the oldest goes, unless it is one of the five latest answers, and then the oldest skip goes instead. — *Why:* the author's decision. "Another task" before an answer is how a child walks past a task they do not like, and punishing that would punish a stray tap; but leafing through hard tasks until an easy one comes shows only in what the parent can see, and a skip that left no trace would hide it. — *Rejected:* counting a skip as a wrong answer, as "I don't know" is (R93); leaving it free and unseen, as before. — *Where it lives:* SPEC 2.2, 3.2, 7.3 and 8.2, `docs/architecture/03-flows.md` and `04-profile.md`, RUN.md (T44, T57).

**R99. The tools of the profile: what they say, and when they write.** *(Since T62.7 neither tool draws a card, every problem of a refusal carries a code beside its rule, and the card's form changes the details through `edit_profile`: R148.)* `get_profile` and `save_profile` answer with the child's details — the parent's notes among them — how far the trial series has got, and a short recommendation, `{topic, grade_level, difficulty, goal}`, taken from the rule's brief; the plot, the traps and the rule's reasoning reach the model with `next_task` alone. `get_progress` and `read_progress` answer the same progress (R97), with the details the progress screen shows and without the notes; the domain package `progress` works it out from the profile and the catalog each time, and "mastered" there is the rule's own `tutor.Mastered`, held at the level the topic's tasks now come from. `save_profile` makes an edit: a field left out stays, a list given replaces the one kept, an empty `notes` or `ui_language` clears it; text is cleaned — control characters dropped, and those that show nothing or reorder the text; any space read as a plain one — before its cap is counted (R16), and in the words the model reads whatever the parent wrote — the pseudonym, the interests, the notes — stands in quotes, a string of JSON apart from every sentence of the service's; the language is kept as a canonical BCP 47 tag of at most 35 characters that names its language outright; skills are ids of the catalog, and a profile may leave out all 25 of them; lists are written as `[]`, never null. Details that break a rule are refused as an answer, `status: rejected` with `code: invalid_profile`, one problem per field naming the rule and never the value — a count of how far off it is, and the place of a skill the catalog does not have in the list as it was sent; an edit that asks for what is already there writes nothing, and a call that names no field is answered with the profile as it is. A save that lost to a write made in between is a failure with a sentence of its own, and is not retried here. The store's other refusals — a damaged file, one from a newer build, access taken back — are told in the general sentence until T51 gives each its own, and the progress carries no misconception map until T57a builds it. Until profiles live in the parent's Drive (T50), the container keeps them in the memory of the process. — *Why:* the author's decisions on the notes — the model reads them back to the parent who wrote them, and no screen the child sees draws them — and on the cap, which was the size of the catalog when the catalog had fifteen skills; the rest keeps the tools cheap and honest: a write only when something changed, a refusal the model can act on field by field, and no word of a parent's text in anything that goes further than the answer. — *Rejected:* the whole brief as the recommendation, which a card has no use for; a refusal of a call that names no field, which costs the model an error to fix for nothing; the limits in the input schema, whose library quotes the value that broke them; a retry of a lost save here, which belongs to the store that loses it (T51). — *Where it lives:* SPEC 4.1, 5.9, 7.2, 7.3 and remarks 5, 28, 30, 36 and 42, `docs/architecture/04-profile.md`, RUN.md (T43).

**R100. The tools of a task: what they take, what they say, and when a request is over.** *(Since T62.8 the package reaches the model through `get_package`, and `next_task` draws the card the task comes to: R152.)* *(Since T62.3 a request's language is the profile's when the parent chose one, R144.)* `next_task` takes the language of the chat, which is required: a call without it is refused by the protocol library, and one whose language is empty or names none by the tool — there is no fallback to a host's locale or to the cards' `ui_language`. The language is read as `ui_language` is, a canonical BCP 47 tag that names its language. The model may set a topic, a level or a difficulty of its own with a reason of at most 300 characters; everything it passes is checked by the rule, and what breaks a rule is refused as an answer, `status: rejected` with `code: invalid_arguments`, one problem per argument naming the rule and never the value — a level named alone is held to the rule's topic, which the refusal names — and a reason with no choice chooses nothing and is not kept. The request opened is kept in the profile with a new id, and the package goes to the model in the words of the result alone, never in the payload a card is drawn from. While a request is younger than the window, `MATHTRAIL_REQUEST_WINDOW`, fifteen minutes by default and never under one, `next_task` hands it back as it was, with the package again, `already_open` and its age, applies none of its arguments and writes nothing; past the window the request is abandoned: the next `next_task` replaces it and a task handed in for it is stale. A task left on the card without an answer is recorded as skipped when the next one is asked for (R98). `submit_task` takes no language of its own — the task is written in the request's, and the checks read it from there — and takes the brief, the task and the self-check as whatever JSON arrives, read by the structure check in its own words. Its solver runs before the profile is read (R61); a task for no request that is open spends nothing and writes nothing, and its card shows the task the child already has when there is one, so that a task handed in twice cannot turn the card the child is working on into a wait. An accepted task is the one the request asked for — its topic, level, difficulty and language are the request's, not the model's to say again — and its answer, its traps and their explanations, its solution and its program are sealed together; the card is handed only what a child may see. `task_submitted` is written for every task that was judged; a stale one leaves none, only its `tool_call` and the `solver_run` lines of the runs it cost. The review is two spans under the call, `examine_task` with the solver's runs under it and `judge_task`, opened by the transport, so that the checks stay free of anything that watches them. — *Why:* the author's decision on the language: a task in a language guessed is a generation wasted, and a guess that is sometimes right hides the model that never passes it.
