Drive's refusals become six sentinels: no such file (404), the token not taken (401), a pause asked for (429, or 403 with `userRateLimitExceeded`, `rateLimitExceeded` or `dailyLimitExceeded`), a full Drive (403 with `storageQuotaExceeded`), Drive not answering (a 5xx, no answer, or an answer that breaks off or does not read as Drive's), and a file larger than the caller would read. A call its caller gave up on stays the caller's own and is none of them. A call about a file names one, or it is not sent — as it stands it would reach the address of the search — and an answer about a file says which file it is, or it is Drive not answering. Every answer is read to its end, so that its connection carries the next call. Any other refusal is told by its status and Drive's reason, a word of Drive's list. No error carries the address of a request, Drive's own sentence or the token: the address and the sentence name the file (SPEC 12.3). `internal/infra/drive/drivetest` stands in for Drive over `httptest`, as `googletest` does for the sign-in: a Drive for every token; the parent's own files never shown, which is the `drive.file` scope; the part of the search language the service uses, and a 400 that fails the test for anything else; exactly the fields asked for; uploads held to Drive's multipart form; the calls counted by kind, and the next call of a kind refused or left unanswered when a test asks. The store is `internal/store/drive`, package `drivestore`, as `internal/transport/mcp` is `mcpserver`, so that it does not share the client's name. — *Why:* five calls do not need Google's client library. `google.golang.org/api/drive/v3` brings the whole of it and its transports, the reason R107 turned down `idtoken`; its own instrumentation writes the address of every request into a span, and that address holds the file's ID; and a stand-in has to speak HTTP either way. Two packages, because CLAUDE.md's layout and R104 put Drive's API among the infrastructure, and RUN.md puts the store under `internal/store` (the author's decision, which settles note 1 of 01-context). The client changes when Drive's API does, and the store when what a profile needs of Drive does; T51 adds to both — revisions to the one, the recovery, the bin and a file set aside to the other. The client knows neither whose Drive it reaches nor the service's logs, like `cimd` and `googleauth`. What watches the calls is the store's, which knows the account. — *Rejected:* Google's client library, above; one package with the client unexported inside it, as RUN.md's wording had it, which would put Drive's request shapes and the profile's rules in one package that T51 then doubles; a mock of the client in place of the stand-in, which proves nothing about what the contract suite holds the two stores to (R43). — *Consequence:* no new dependency. The client's five methods are an interface's ceiling (CLAUDE.md), so the revisions T51 needs come as an interface of their own. The stand-in joins `storetest` and `googletest` as test code that `codecov.yml` leaves out of the coverage and `sonar-project.properties` counts among the tests. — *Where it lives:* `internal/infra/drive/`, `internal/infra/drive/drivetest/`, `internal/store/drive/`; CLAUDE.md (Layout); `codecov.yml`, `sonar-project.properties`; SPEC 9.2 and 11.2; `docs/architecture/01-context.md` note 1.

**R116. The profile in Drive is found by its marker, remembered by its file, and read again before it is written.** *(What its consequence leaves to T51 is R118 to R120; a remembered file is trusted for ten minutes since R120.)* The layout is 05-storage's: a folder `MathTrail` and in it a file `mathtrail-profile.json` of type `application/json`, marked with the service's own properties `mathtrail: folder` and `mathtrail: profile`, the file also with `schema`, a copy of the version of its shape. A profile is found by its marker alone, among the files not in the bin, and never by its name or its folder; of several, the one changed last is the profile. The file found is remembered while the instance runs, keyed by the account's identifier, for at most 4,096 accounts, and forgotten when Drive no longer has it, which sends the next read back to the search. A first profile is refused when a search finds one, readable or not, and goes into the folder its marker finds, or into a new one. The revision a read hands out is the file, the profile's own number and a SHA-256 digest of the bytes read. A save refuses a revision of another file, or a profile that is not one past it, without asking Drive anything. It then downloads the file again, refuses when the bytes are not the ones read — and writes nothing — and uploads the new bytes with the `schema` property, which leaves the marker as Drive keeps it. An account with no token reaches nothing and asks nothing. A token Drive does not take is `ErrAccessRevoked`; a file past 1 MB, four times the cap of a profile, is `ErrCorrupted`; every other refusal reaches the tool as Drive's, which tells it in its general sentence until T51. Every call to Drive is a span, `drive <op>`, of the client kind, inside the span of the tool, carrying `mathtrail.drive.outcome`. It is marked failed only when Drive or the service failed, and not for a file that is not there, access taken back, a full Drive or a file too large, which are answers about the parent's Drive, nor for a call its caller gave up on. Every call also leaves a `drive_call` line: the call, its time, its retries — none yet — and its outcome, with the request, the user and the trace. A profile lives in Drive under the real sign-in, and in the memory of the process under the development sign-in, whose account carries no Google token. — *Why:* the read before the write is the author's decision. R80 refuses a save made from a state that has moved on, a change by hand or by another instance included; Drive has no conditional write, and the only way to see what is there now is to look. It costs one call per write: a whole task is nine calls on a warm instance where 05-storage counted six. A digest of the bytes rather than Drive's revision, because Drive's revision cannot be read in the call that reads the bytes, so a warm read would take two calls instead of one. Drive downloads a revision by its ID only once it is kept forever. It gives `sha256Checksum` "if available", and MD5 is a digest the linter refuses. Bytes that are the same are the same state, whatever happened in between. Remembering the file is what makes a warm read one call. Remembering nothing else keeps the service without state (PRODUCT 5), and a memory that is wrong costs one search. The sign-in decides the store, by the author's decision: without a Google token there is no Drive, and a switch of its own would be a second thing to keep in step with the first. So the switch T52a asks for is the development sign-in, which a deployment already refuses. `current_task.answered` needs no new version of the file (SPEC remark 44): T50 is the first build that reads files in Drive, and it knows the field, so no build that predates the field ever meets a file that holds it. — *Rejected:* no check before the write, which would keep six calls and write over a parent's own edit; comparing `headRevisionId`, a second call on every warm read; downloading a revision by its ID, above; the checksums Drive reports, above; a switch of its own for the store, above; remembering "no profile here" as well, which would make a second file the day another chat of the parent's made the first. — *Consequence:* the table of 05-storage says what each tool costs now, and a test in `transport/mcp` holds every tool to it. The window left is the upload itself. Two first profiles made at once make two files, of which the newest is read, and two saves from one revision that meet on one instance can both land, as R80 lets them — nothing here holds one account's writes to one at a time yet. Nothing is retried yet after a conflict or a pause. These, the bin, the recovery, a sentence of its own for each refusal and the resource's 401 are T51's. — *Where it lives:* `internal/store/drive/`; `internal/app/container.go`; `internal/config` (`MATHTRAIL_DRIVE_TIMEOUT`); `internal/transport/mcp/drive_test.go`; `justfile` (`run`, `run-signin`); `docs/architecture/05-storage.md`, `04-profile.md`; SPEC 9.4, 12.2, 12.5 and remarks 44, 55 and 59.

**R118. A write that loses is made again from what won, the writes of an account take turns on an instance, and every refusal of Drive's is told in words of its own.** *(R214: `ErrRestored` is gone, and a read that finds a damaged file changes nothing, so the tools that only read have no mending to make again.)* Every tool that reads and writes the profile — `save_profile`, `next_task`, `submit_task` after its solver has run, `submit_answer` — runs its read and its write again from a fresh read when the store says the profile moved on in between: the write refused as a conflict, or the file deleted for good before it, found as no profile. Three times at most, 50 to 150 ms apart at random, and the third is told as it is. The tools that only read do the same, for a read that found a damaged file changing while it was put back. Nothing a run computed is sent again. Each run decides from what it read, so what another tab did in between is found done — the request already open, the answer already recorded, the task already accepted — and the rest is done on top of it. Within an instance the writes of one account take turns: the read again and the upload of a save, the search and the creation of a first profile, a new start, and the write of a file put back. The writes of different accounts never wait for each other, and a write whose caller gives up while it waits is never made. A call that Drive asks to pause — `429`, or `403` with a reason of rate — is made again after about half a second and then a second and a half, each spread by a quarter; so is one Drive failed on its side, a `5xx`, unless it is a creation or an upload, which may have landed. A call that ran out of its time, or that its caller gave up on, is never made again, and the `drive_call` line counts the tries. A `403` with `insufficientPermissions` is access taken back; one with `appNotAuthorizedToFile` or `insufficientFilePermissions` is a file the service may no longer reach, forgotten and searched for; `download_restricted_for_revision` is a revision not kept forever (R119). The account carries the end of the Google token inside the access token, and no call to Drive is started that could outlive it: the operation is refused as `store.ErrAccessExpired`, and its line is a warning with the outcome `expired`. The store's refusals are the four of R80 and six more — `ErrAccessExpired`, `ErrInBin`, `ErrBehind`, `ErrRestored`, `ErrStorageFull` and `ErrUnavailable`, the last for Drive out of reach past its tries — and each is told to the model in a sentence of its own, as is `profile.ErrNewer` (SPEC 7.4). Access taken back and a Google token that ends are answered as a failure whose `_meta["mcp/www_authenticate"]` carries the challenge the resource answers a request without a token with, and `error="invalid_token"`. Every host but ChatGPT, told by the name its client gives, is also answered `401` with that challenge in `WWW-Authenticate`: the protocol's library sends the status of a call with its result, so the call that failed decides it. The development sign-in gives no challenge, and its failures carry the sentence alone. — *Why:* the deadline and the answer by host are the author's decisions, the second taken on the evidence below. R80 refuses a write made from a state that moved on, and 05-storage asks for the change to be made again rather than lost. Two tabs served by one instance are the common case of the race, and taking turns closes it there. Drive's own guide asks for backoff on `429` and `5xx`, and a chat host waits seconds, not the half-minute Drive suggests. R117 left the deadline to T51: without it, a call past the Google token's end is refused with a `401` that reads as access taken back. OpenAI's support wrote on 2026-03-23 that a bare `401` during a tool call should not be relied upon to start a sign-in, and that the trigger is an error result carrying `_meta["mcp/www_authenticate"]`. Claude, in the live run of T04 (`docs/live/02-spike-oauth.md`), answered a `401` in the middle of a call with a Connect button in the tool card, and kept the conversation; it showed the `_meta` as an ordinary error. Each host is answered the way it was seen, or is documented, to start a sign-in. — *Rejected:* making the whole call again in the frame of every tool, which would run the solver of `submit_task` again; sending the same bytes again after a conflict, which would lay a stale state over the one that won; one turn for every account, which would make families wait for each other; making a failed upload again; one answer for every host: a `401` alone leaves ChatGPT with words and no sign-in, and a `200` alone leaves Claude with an ordinary error; telling hosts apart by anything more than the name their client gives, which is all a call carries. — *Consequence:* what is left of the race is two instances whose uploads overlap (SPEC 7.6). A task costs nine calls as before, and each conflict a read and a write more. A client whose name is mistaken gets the other answer, and its host relays the sentence, which asks the adult to connect MathTrail again. What each host does is for T53 and T63 to see. — *Where it lives:* `internal/transport/mcp/afresh.go`, `failure.go`, `boundary.go`, `challenge.go`, `signin.go`; `internal/store/drive/turns.go`, `calls.go`, `lines.go`; `internal/infra/drive/refusal.go`; `internal/store/store.go`; `internal/transport/oauth/access.go`; SPEC 7.4, 7.6, 9.2, 12.2 and remarks 48 and 55; `docs/architecture/02-auth.md`, `05-storage.md`.

**R119. A state of each day is kept forever, the earliest past a hundred are deleted, and a damaged file is put back from its history by a call that does nothing else.** *(R126: the day's first write reads the file once more after making room, since a write may land while the room is made.)* *(R214: a read no longer mends — a damaged file is put back only by `save_profile` with `restore`, when the adult agrees —, no write deletes a revision kept forever, and the history is kept up to Drive's two hundred.)* The first write of a day, by UTC and by the `updated_at` the file holds, uploads with `keepRevisionForever`; the rule of every fiftieth write is dropped. A write dated before the file's own day — an instance whose clock runs behind — is no new day. The day a profile is made keeps nothing forever: its first state is the new profile, and the next day's first write keeps where that day ended. Before it uploads, the day's first write lists the file's history, and at a hundred revisions kept forever it deletes the earliest, so that its own is the hundredth; a deletion that fails is left to the next day. An upload asked to keep its revision that Drive refuses for a reason the store has no word for — the most it keeps, among them — is made again without: the write matters more than the revision kept. A file that is no profile at all — not JSON, cut short, of no profile's shape, or too large for any — is put back from the four latest revisions before it and the latest one kept forever, the latest first, in the order the revisions were made, since Drive promises none. Each is kept forever before it is read, since Drive gives no other, room made for them beforehand, and the first that reads is written over the damage as a new revision — unless the file changed while its history was read, in which case what it holds now counts. The read that did it answers `ErrRestored` and nothing else, and the model is told that the file was put back to its latest earlier version that can be read, and that what came after it is lost. No date is named: the file's version history in Drive shows it. A file the instance remembers that does not read is searched for again before anything is mended (R120). A recovery that could not look — a history Drive would not list, Drive out of reach, a Drive too full to keep a revision in, or a revision refused for a reason the store has no word for — says so, and nobody is offered a new start for it; a file no state of which reads is `ErrCorrupted`, and left as it is. A file that is a profile but breaks a rule of this build is `ErrCorrupted` at once, and never rolled back: a newer build may have written it without raising the version it needed, or the parent edited it by hand, and putting an earlier state over either would lose it. — *Why:* Drive's guide to revisions says that "once a blob file revision is set to 'Keep Forever', it can only be downloaded or deleted" — setting it back is refused with `illegalKeepForeverModification` — and that "you can only download blob file content revisions marked as 'Keep Forever'", read on 2026-09-28. So R17's release of the oldest pins cannot be done, and a revision kept forever is a place spent until it is deleted, of two hundred. Deleting the earliest past a hundred is the author's decision. The day's trigger cannot miss a day: a write lost to another leaves `updated_at` as it was, so the next write still finds the day to keep. Telling the parent through a failure of the call reaches them from every tool without a field in every answer, and the call that found the damage does nothing on a state just put back. — *Rejected:* letting go of a pin, which Drive refuses; never deleting and stopping at a ceiling, which ends the history after about a hundred and fifty days of use; keeping nothing on a schedule, which loses everything to damage followed by a month away, when Drive has purged the rest; notices in the result of `Load` (R80), a change at every call for a message given once; putting a file back on every read without writing it, which would repeat both the work and the message. — *Consequence:* the earliest revisions kept forever are deleted whoever kept them, the parent by hand included. `get_profile` is annotated read-only and may still put a damaged file back: a repair of a state the parent had, not a change the call asks for. A hundred days of use are there to recover from, at one call more on the first write of each day. — *Where it lives:* `internal/store/drive/history.go`, `write.go`, `read.go`; `internal/infra/drive/revisions.go`, `drivetest`; `docs/architecture/05-storage.md`; R17.

**R120. The bin is the parent's, a new start sets aside, a remembered file is trusted for ten minutes, an early read is held to the number written, and the profile's tool says where the file is.** *(Since T62.6 the two tools of the progress say where the file is too: R147.)* *(R128: a new start sets aside a newer build's file too.)* *(R214: `Restore` joins `StartOver` in `store.Recovery`, apart from the four operations of `store.Profiles`.)* A search that finds no profile looks in the bin — the search of a read, and the one a first profile is made after — and a profile there is `ErrInBin`, told as "restore it from the bin in Drive, or start a new profile"; nothing in the store restores it. `StartOver` is the fifth operation of R80, and `save_profile` calls it with `start_over` and the details of a first profile, only when the profile cannot be read or is in the bin. It makes the new profile before it sets anything aside, so that one failing halfway never leaves the account with no profile. It downloads the profile's file once more, and one that reads — by this build or a newer one — is `ErrConflict` and never replaced: the call then says the profile can be read, and writes nothing. Otherwise it is renamed "mathtrail-profile set aside <day>.json" and marked `mathtrail: set-aside`, holding what it held and left where it lies — and so is every other file with the marker that cannot be read, and every one in the bin; a file beside it that reads is a profile too, and stays as it is. The new profile is then made as a first one; with nothing to set aside — the bin emptied — it is a first profile. A file the instance remembers is trusted for ten minutes from the search that found it, and then searched for again: a file the parent put in the bin is seen by every instance within that long. The answer to an upload says whether the file is in the bin and what it is marked as, and a write that landed in a file in the bin or set aside counts for nothing: the file is forgotten, and the write refused as a conflict. The number of the profile the instance last wrote is remembered with the file, and nothing else of the profile. A read that comes back with an earlier one is read again after 250 ms; one still behind is `ErrBehind` — "make the same call again in a moment" — and the number is let go of, so that a file put back on purpose is read as it is next time. `get_profile` carries `location`: the folder, the file, the link — the file is the export — and the other files that carry a profile, for the adult to delete; the other tools say nothing of them. The first sign-in's words cover a profile deleted for good and another Google account, which the service cannot tell from a first sign-in. — *Why:* the bin, the argument of `save_profile`, the ten minutes, the number without the profile and the location in `get_profile` are the author's decisions. Drive answers a file in the bin by its ID as any other, and only a search leaves the bin out (05-storage, note 9). Two files are reported and never merged (note 5). A file set aside and remembered elsewhere is damage to the instance that reads it, and the search again before mending is what keeps it from being read as the profile. — *Rejected:* restoring from the bin as a sixth operation, past the ceiling of five methods and on the service's say rather than the parent's; a copy of the profile kept to answer an early read with, which is keeping profile data; trusting a remembered file without end, which never sees the bin; reading a file's metadata before every read, a call more on each; two files told by every tool; "the profile is gone" as an answer of its own, which the service cannot tell from a first sign-in. — *Consequence:* for up to ten minutes an instance that remembers a file put in the bin reads it, and writes into it once. A first sign-in costs two searches more, of the bin, and `get_profile` two calls more: the search and the folder's name. — *Where it lives:* `internal/store/drive/read.go`, `startover.go`, `remembered.go`, `write.go`; `internal/store/memory`, `internal/store/storetest`; `internal/transport/mcp/profile.go`, `payload.go`; SPEC 7.2 and 7.3; `docs/architecture/05-storage.md`.

**R214. A read never mends and a write never deletes: a damaged file is put back only when the adult agrees, by `save_profile` with `restore` alone, and the history is kept up to Drive's own limit.** *(Decided by the author in the planning of T71.2, 2026-10-05, as part of the honest scheme of the hints, R215; carried out in T71.2.)* It revises R119, the part of R126 that read the file again after making room before the day's first write, and the retry R118 gave a read that found a damaged file changing while it was put back. A file that is no profile at all — not JSON, cut short, of no profile's shape, or too large for any — is told by every tool, the ones that only read among them, and nothing is done: no history is listed, no revision kept and nothing written. The store answers it as `store.ErrDamaged`, a kind of `ErrCorrupted`, and the failure `damaged` tells the model that the file is damaged and nothing was done; to tell the adult; that, if they agree, `save_profile` with `restore` set to true and nothing else puts the file back to its latest earlier version that can be read, and that anything saved after that version is lost; that `start_over` with a pseudonym and a grade starts a new profile instead; and that either way the damaged file is kept. `restore` comes alone: beside any other argument the call is refused with `restore_alone` and does nothing. It calls `Restore`, which with `StartOver` makes `store.Recovery`, apart from the four operations of `store.Profiles` — `Load`, `Create`, `Save` and `Export` —, and `store.Storage` is the two together, so that no interface holds more than four methods. `Restore` reads the file again, and one that reads is never put back over: the call says so, hands back the profile and writes nothing. Otherwise it recovers as R119 did — the four latest revisions before the damage and the latest kept forever, each kept forever before it is read — and writes the first that reads over the damage, unless the file changed in between, when the call is made again from a fresh read, as any write that loses is (R118); a file no state of which reads is `ErrCorrupted`, told as before. `ErrRestored` is gone. A file just put back may read for a while as the damage it was put back from, since Drive does not promise that a read right after a write sees it: the instance that put it back reads it once more after a moment when a read finds the damage again, and one that still does is told as behind, once, as R120 holds any early read — never as damage the adult would be asked about again. The day's first write asks Drive to keep its revision forever and does nothing else for it: it lists no history and deletes nothing. Drive keeps at most two hundred revisions of a file forever, and the store counts to the same two hundred; past them Drive refuses the keep, and the upload is made again without, as R119 already had it — now on any refusal but one an upload without keeping would meet as well, since Drive does not document how it refuses a revision past its limit. Only a recovery makes room: before it keeps the revisions it reads, it deletes as many of the earliest kept as those of them not kept yet need. — *Why:* the author chose the honest scheme over marking the reads as writers. A host asks a person before a call, or lets it through, by what the tool says it does (R215). A read that mends writes the file, and a day's first write that deletes the earliest kept revision destroys a state the parent had: neither fits a tool marked as one that reads, or as one that only adds. Putting a file back also loses what was saved after the version it returns to, which is the adult's to agree to, not a tool's. — *Rejected:* mending on a read and marking every reading tool a writer, which makes every look at the progress a write a review reads as a mismatch, and one Claude may ask about; mending in the first write that finds the damage, a write the adult did not ask for; a tool of its own for the repair, one more to list, approve and review, where `save_profile` already replaces the whole profile with `start_over`; keeping a hundred days by deleting the earliest at the day's first write, which every lesson would then do. — *Consequence:* a damaged file stops the lessons until the adult answers in the chat; a card's own calls show that they failed, and the model hears of the damage at its next call. After about two hundred days with writes no further day is kept forever until a recovery makes room; the history Drive keeps on its own stays as before. The first write of a day costs one call less; past the two hundred, it uploads twice, the first asking to keep its revision and refused. How Drive refuses the two hundred and first is not documented, and a live account is the first place the refusal is seen (note 12 of 05-storage). A recovery at Drive's limit deletes up to five of the earliest kept revisions. — *Where it lives:* `internal/store/store.go`, `parse.go`; `internal/store/drive/read.go`, `history.go`, `write.go`, `drivestore.go`; `internal/store/memory`, `internal/store/storetest`; `internal/transport/mcp/failure.go`, `profile.go`, `afresh.go`, `restore_test.go`; SPEC 7.1, 7.2, 7.4 and 7.6; `docs/architecture/05-storage.md`; `docs/parents.md`.

**R121. Each limit is counted where the refusal can be told, and a call held back by a pace is told the way a failure is.** *(Since T62.8 the account's pace starts at sixty a minute, its burst twenty, and nine of the ten tools declare the shape of their payload, `get_package` answering in words alone: R152.)* *(Amended by R126: the token endpoint is held to its address's pace alone, since renewals there keep the lessons going; and by R127: each account's renewals at Google are held to a pace of their own.)* *(R219: the pace of an address stays at twenty, counted against the hosts' ranges, and the token of ChatGPT's directory is one of its documents.)* `internal/ratelimit` keeps a pace for each key in the instance's memory. The allowance is held as time: a request costs one interval, a minute over the number, and every moment that passes gives a moment back, up to a third of a minute's requests — ten of thirty, the burst of SPEC 10.2, six of twenty, sixty-six of two hundred. A limiter keeps at most 4,096 keys and forgets the one used longest ago; a key forgotten starts from a full allowance, so forgetting only ever lets a request in. A request is held to the paces in turn, the key's own first, and one that a later pace turns away is given back to the ones before it, so that it costs no allowance (`ratelimit.Admit`). Where each pace is counted:
- **An address**, `MATHTRAIL_RATE_IP_PER_MIN`: the sign-in's six endpoints and its two documents, before any of them runs. The address is the last entry of `X-Forwarded-For`, or the connection's own when the header is missing or its last entry names no address; an IPv6 address counts by its /64, since one household holds a whole network of addresses. The refusal is `429` with `Retry-After` — one request of the pace, in whole seconds — and `Cache-Control: no-store`. A program is answered in the router's own shape, `TOO_MANY_REQUESTS`: OAuth has no error for a pace, and the refusal comes from in front of the endpoint, not from it. A parent's browser — the request a host sends it with, the consent screen's answer, Google's — is shown the sign-in's own page, "too many sign-ins at once", in the parent's language.
- **An account**, `MATHTRAIL_RATE_USER_PER_MIN`: every message a signed-in account sends the MCP endpoint, counted in the boundary, where the account and the method are both known. Tool calls alone would leave out the heaviest request an account can repeat: reading the widget's page, about 250 KB.
  - A tool call past it is held back before any tool runs. It is answered with one sentence — wait a moment, then make the same call again — marked as an error, with no payload (the author's choice). Six of the seven tools declare the shape of their payload, a host may reject a payload that breaks it, and none of that shape can be made without the tool. Its `tool_call` line is a refusal of status `limited`, not a failure.
  - Any other message is refused with JSON-RPC's `-32000` and "too many requests": no model reads it, and the host asks again.
  - A notification is not counted. It asks for no answer, so a refusal would only lose it without a word, and it asks for no work.
- **The instance**, `MATHTRAIL_RATE_INSTANCE_PER_MIN`, at each of its two doors apart: the sign-in, which anybody reaches, and the lessons, which only a signed-in account does. Apart, so that a flood at the sign-in never holds back a child in the middle of a lesson. Each is counted after the key's own, so that a runaway is stopped by its own ceiling before it spends everybody's. It counts only requests that start work. The two documents, a request with no token, a path nobody declared, another host and the probe are refused or answered for nothing, and counting them would let a flood of them close the door on everybody while saving nothing.
- **The day**, `MATHTRAIL_DAILY_TASKS` and `MATHTRAIL_DAILY_FAILED`: `next_task` reads the day's counters. Counters of another day count as none. The check comes after a request already open is handed back and before the rule chooses. Past either ceiling the call is refused `limited`, `limit_reached`, in the words of SPEC 10.3 — no more new tasks today, more tomorrow, and what to do meanwhile, true after a day of failed requests too — and nothing is written; which ceiling it was is in the line alone.

`limit_hit` names the ceiling — `user_rate`, `ip_rate`, `instance_rate`, `daily_tasks` or `daily_failed` — and the day's counter as `count`, and never an address. It is a warning whichever ceiling it names: what reaches one is worth a look, a runaway or a topic the models cannot write on. A pace writes it once for a flood — refusals no further apart than the time its allowance takes to fill back whole —, so that a flood of refused requests leaves no flood of lines, however many of its requests the pace lets through; the day writes it for every call it refuses. Every number is an environment variable of at least 1, and the service writes the ones in force as it starts. — *Why:* PRODUCT 6 asks for a message the model relays, and a limit can say something only where it is counted: the account and the tool are known inside the MCP endpoint, the address in front of the sign-in, the day in the profile. — *Rejected:* a `429` at the door for every limit, which leaves the model to guess; a payload with a status and empty fields for every tool, which puts a limit into every tool and hands a card of nulls; a variable for each burst, which SPEC 11.2 does not have and a third of a minute makes unnecessary; `golang.org/x/time/rate`, which the protocol library links in but which gives a request back only by cancelling a reservation at the very moment it was made. — *Known imprecision:* the token, registration and revocation endpoints and the two documents are asked by the hosts' own servers. Every family of a host shares their few addresses. At twenty a minute and a fifteen-minute access token, one address carries about three hundred families' renewals, or five sign-ins, a minute. That is enough for v1; T53 sees the addresses, and T52b and T64 set the number. — *Where it lives:* `internal/ratelimit` and `ratelimittest`, `internal/transport/http/middleware/limit.go` and `address.go`, `internal/transport/oauth/pages.go`, `internal/transport/mcp/limits.go` and `nexttask.go`, `internal/config`, `internal/app/container.go`; SPEC 7.4, 9.2, 10, 12.2; [03-flows](architecture/03-flows.md), [02-auth](architecture/02-auth.md).

**R122. A run waits three seconds for a slot of the sandbox, and a task that finds none in that time is told it was not checked.** `starlark.Limits` gains `Wait`, read from `MATHTRAIL_SOLVER_WAIT`: 3 s by default, and a positive duration. A run takes a free slot at once; otherwise it waits for the first to come free, and past the wait it does not start and ends with `solver.ErrBusy`. The domain declares that error beside `Runner`, since whether a run could start at all is part of the contract of running one. The two runs of a task wait apart. `submit_task` tells the model in a sentence of its own — MathTrail is checking too many tasks right now, so this task was not checked and no attempt was spent; hand the same task in again in a moment — as a failure of the kind `busy`. No attempt is spent: the sandbox runs before the profile is read, and a failure there writes nothing. — *Why:* the author's decision, taken while planning T52a, on a wait nothing bounded before. The request's cancellation is not passed to a tool (R83), so a hand-in queued behind runs that spend their whole clock waited as long as the queue took: after the host had given up on it, past the HTTP server's write deadline, and on a deployment past the platform's minute. The guard of T52a asks that the calls a sandbox cannot take are told so rather than left hanging. Three seconds brings the slowest `submit_task` to the minute: two runs of three seconds' wait and two of running, and five calls to Drive at their ten seconds each, make sixty. It passes the minute only by the milliseconds of the checks, or further when a conflict sends it back to Drive as R117 allows, and only when every wait, every run and every call to Drive takes all of its time at once; a longer wait would pass it in that worst case by whole seconds. — *Consequence:* a burst of runs the size of the reference solvers', milliseconds each, passes without a refusal; only a queue behind runs of the whole clock meets one. The number is a guess until T52b measures a loaded instance — it did, and it stays (R125): with eighty hand-ins in flight past the instance's pace every call was answered within 6.2 seconds. When the second run of a task finds no slot, the `solver_run` line of the first is lost with the hand-in, since `Examine` returns nothing on an error; the span of that run and its steps are still recorded. That is accepted rather than widening the contract of `Examine` for one line. The margin of R117 no longer has an unbounded wait inside it. — *Rejected:* refusing at once when every slot is taken, which turns away the fifth of five tasks arriving in one millisecond although a slot frees a few milliseconds later; a wait equal to the solver's clock, which ties together two numbers T52b may need apart; a deadline on the whole examination, which would stop a run already under way and blame its solver for the queue; holding one slot across both runs, which puts the two runs of a verdict into the sandbox's contract. — *Where it lives:* `internal/domain/solver/verdict.go`, `internal/infra/starlark/starlark.go`, `internal/config`, `internal/app/container.go`, `internal/transport/mcp/failure.go`, `internal/transport/mcp/concurrency_test.go`, `internal/infra/starlark/starlarktest`; SPEC 6.6, 7.4, 10.3, 11.2, 12.2; [03-flows](architecture/03-flows.md).

**R125. What the sandbox's built-ins build is paid for by its bytes, an instance runs one solver for each vCPU, and it has 1 GiB, nine tenths of it told to the runtime.** Everything predeclared pays the step budget before it works: a step for every element it walks, as before, and for what it builds and hands back a step for every sixteen bytes, estimated from the sizes it was handed, with a quarter on top for the allocator's rounding. The estimates are:
- a list, 32 bytes and 16 for each value;
- a list of tuples, 32, and for each tuple 40 plus 16 for each value;
- a set or a dictionary, 512, and 256 for each entry;
- a whole number made past 32 bits as a range or a numbering is walked, 48;
- each sequence `product` and `zip` begin to walk, 64.

`internal/infra/starlark/price.go` holds them (SPEC 6.5). The cap of a million elements, and of a tuple of a million values, is unchanged, and `product` now reads its pools where they are instead of copying them out once for every repeat.

`MATHTRAIL_SOLVER_CONCURRENCY` defaults to 1, and a deployment sets it from its `cpu`, a whole number of vCPUs. The instance's `memory` is `1Gi`, with at least 1 GiB for every vCPU at the default step ceiling and more in proportion to a higher one, and a deployment sets `GOMEMLIMIT` to nine tenths of it. `settings` may set neither. The steps stay at twenty-five million, the clock at two seconds, the wait at three, and the requests of an instance at eighty. The line that says the sandbox is built now also says what the runtime got: its processors and its soft limit.

The load of T52a runs every Monday against the image of what is on `main`, by hand, and on a pull request that changes it (`.github/workflows/load.yml`, `just ci-load`). Each run is held to the memory measured for it, with room to spare, and the reports are the run's summary.

— *Why:* the author's decisions, taken while planning T52b on what T52a measured.
- **The budget of steps limited processor time, not memory.** A value cost one step whatever it took, so pairs held 824 MiB in one run, and a single call could build some 400 MB before anything looked. Priced by bytes, the steps bound what the built-ins of a run keep: 381 MiB at the ceiling. The four numbers of an instance can then be set against each other: one run's worth, the collector's as much again, and the service beside them come to a GiB for each solver running at once.
- **One slot for each vCPU, because the clock is wall time.** Runs sharing a processor each spend it on the others' work, and the verdict of a task — accepted, or rejected as too slow at the cost of an attempt — would turn on what happened to run beside it.
- **Memory costs nothing inside the free tier up to 2 GiB for each vCPU,** since vCPU-seconds run out first.
- **`GOMEMLIMIT` keeps garbage within the instance.** `product` keeps little and lets go of a great deal: four of it at once on an instance of 512 MiB peak at 469 MiB with the soft limit, and end the instance with `GOMEMLIMIT=off`.

Measured on the development machine in a container of one vCPU and 1 GiB (`docs/load.md`):
- every scenario is clean: `tuples` peaks at up to 782 MiB, `pairs` at up to 705, and `sets` between 411 and 580 as the collector chose;
- with eighty hand-ins in flight past the instance's pace, every call was answered within 6.2 seconds, 85 % of them as busy;
- at 512 MiB every scenario survives too, but at its soft limit, 50 MiB short of the kill, with the heaviest runs a fifth to a third slower;
- with four slots at 1 GiB, `tuples` and `pairs` end the instance, and a solver that needs 0.62 seconds alone is stopped by the clock in 38 runs of 44.

— *Consequence:*
- A set costs some twenty steps an element, and a run may hold one of a million. The costliest reference solver rises from 2,081,362 steps to 2,413,617, still under a tenth of the ceiling.
- A hand-in that finds the one slot taken for its wait is told the sandbox is busy. It spends no attempt, where four slots would have blamed its solver.
- One account handing in solvers that spend their whole clock, for requests nobody opened, keeps an instance's single slot busy; a freed slot goes to whoever waited longest. Such a hand-in spends no attempt, so the account pays nothing for it. That stays open (`docs/load.md`).
- What the language builds by itself stays unpriced: literals, operators, the methods of values. `[{} for i in range(n)]` keeps 512 bytes for every nine steps and ended the instance of 1 GiB in the load. `[0] * n` and `xs = xs + xs` are as R45 and R53 say.
- Merging this changes the deployed service: a revision of 1 GiB, with the two variables.

— *What would change it:* that gap seen in the aggregate log. The fix is a solver in a process of its own with a memory limit on it, as R45 names.

— *Rejected:*
- **Four slots with the clock as insurance.** Verdicts depend on the load, 4 × 381 MiB needs 2 GiB, and a clock long enough to be insurance for four breaks the minute of R122.
- **The old price with 2 GiB.** Pairs still hold 824 MiB a run, and sets and empty tables stay unpriced.
- **A watch on the heap in the process that cancels a run past a line.** It cannot stop an operator inside one instruction, and cannot tell whose run grew.
- **The process of its own now.** A change of shape, and the author's decision.
- **Pricing what a call lets go of as well.** It would charge every small `max` for its iterator.
- **A second allowance in bytes beside the steps, R53's option.** It is a second number to calibrate where one price bounds both.
- **Slots from `GOMAXPROCS`.** The runtime never sets it below two on a machine of two processors or more.
- **Reading `memory.max` inside the service.** A deployment's environment already says it.

— *Where it lives:* `internal/infra/starlark/price.go`, `vocabulary.go`, `combinatorics.go`, `starlark.go` and their tests; `internal/config/config.go`; `internal/app/container.go`; `infra/terraform/variables.tf`, `service.tf`; `tools/load/`; `justfile` (`ci-load`); `.github/workflows/load.yml`; SPEC 6.5, 6.6, 10.5, 11.1, 11.2 and remarks 15 and 63; `docs/load.md`; `docs/self-hosting.md`.

**R128. A newer build's file is waited for while a rollout could explain it, and a new start is taken over it at any time.** The author's decision (2026-09-29), after the review of R126. A file whose `schema_version` is past this build's is refused as `newer` — make the same call again in a few minutes — only while the `updated_at` it carries is within 30 minutes of now, on either side. Written longer ago or later than that, or giving no moment this build reads, it is refused as `unsupported`: saved by a version this one cannot read, for too long to be an update in progress, with the ways out of a damaged file — restore a version from Drive's history, or start over. `save_profile` with `start_over` is taken over a newer file whether or not a rollout could explain it: the file is set aside, whole, as a damaged one is, and the new profile takes its place; a file this build reads is still never replaced, and a newer build's file beside the profile stays a profile, as one this build reads does. An instance that remembers a newer file searches again before it tells it, as it does for a damaged one, since another may have set it aside. — *Why:* R80 and R120 weighed a rollout, which is over in minutes. A file edited by hand to a newer version, or left by a newer version that was then withdrawn, was answered "try again in a few minutes" for good, and refused the new start that was the only way on. The moment is the file's own, so no instance keeps anything to judge it by. The adult asking for a new start consents to leaving the file behind, and setting it aside keeps it for the version that can read it. — *Rejected:* keeping the refusal of a new start for a newer file, which is the deadlock; judging by Drive's modification time, which a store in memory does not have and which a hand edit moves as well; counting how long an instance has seen the file, which needs a state the service does not keep. — *Consequence:* the window holds because the service is rolled out whole, with no traffic split between two revisions; a split kept for longer than the window would have the older revision tell a live newer file unsupported. During a real rollout, an adult who asks for a new start from an old instance sets the new file aside; it stays in Drive under its set-aside name. `store.ErrUnsupported` wraps `profile.ErrNewer`, which carries the moment the file gives. — *Where it lives:* `internal/domain/profile/json.go` (`NewerError`); `internal/store/parse.go` (`ParseAt`, `RolloutWindow`), `store.go`; `internal/store/drive/read.go`, `startover.go`; `internal/store/memory/memory.go`; `internal/store/storetest/storetest.go`; `internal/transport/mcp/failure.go`, `profile.go`; SPEC 7.4, 12.2; `docs/architecture/04-profile.md`.

**R219. The pace of an address stays at twenty a minute, counted against the hosts' published ranges, and its first refusal after a directory lists MathTrail is the sign to raise it.** *(Decided in the planning of T71.2, 2026-10-05.)* The hosts call the sign-in's documents and the token endpoint from their own servers: Claude from `160.79.104.0/21` (CL-36 in `docs/catalogs.md`), ChatGPT from the 278 prefixes it publishes (GPT-44). A family in a lesson renews its tokens about every ten minutes, a tenth of a request a minute, and a sign-in costs a host three requests, four with a registration; so one address carries some two hundred families in a lesson at once, or six sign-ins a minute, and that many times more as a host spreads its calls over more addresses, which neither says it does. The account's own pace of renewals at Google, twelve a minute (R127), already bounds what one family can cost. The first `limit_hit` naming `ip_rate` after a directory lists MathTrail is the sign: `MATHTRAIL_RATE_IP_PER_MIN` is raised through `settings`, with no new build. — *Why:* the author's choice among the three ways of §8.1 of `docs/catalogs.md`: the number has never been reached, and the line that would show it being reached is already written. — *Rejected:* a pace of its own for the hosts' ranges, a list of addresses to keep in step with two hosts; a higher pace for every address now, which loosens it for whoever is not a host as well. — *Consequence:* until the number is raised, a renewal refused sends a family to sign in again. — *Where it lives:* SPEC 10.2 and remark 60; §8.1 of `docs/catalogs.md`.

## Counting how the service is used

**R184. A child is counted in the log under a name of the month; the lines it is on are kept two months, and the counts years.** The lines a child is counted from — a task handed out, an answer, a topic mastered — carry `learner`: the first sixteen base64url characters of HMAC-SHA256 of the profile's `student_id`, under a key that is HMAC-SHA256 of the month, written `YYYY-MM` in UTC, under a secret of its own, `MATHTRAIL_LEARNER_KEY`. The secret is never rotated with the sealing keys, and only ever at the turn of a month; off a deployment a process given none makes one that lasts its own life. The task handed out carries beside it the chat host, in the words of the tool call's `client`, the primary subtag of the task's language, the grade, the month the profile was made, the profile's country and region and the country of the sign-in (R185). The answer carries the grade, the month and `topics_mastered`, how many topics count as mastered once it is in, as the progress counts them. `topic_mastered` is written when an answer earns a topic its mastery (SPEC 2.5) and brings the topic among those the progress calls mastered. The raw lines of the three events are to be kept 62 days apart from the rest of the log, and the counts taken from them, with no identifier, for years (T67.2). The report holds every field of those lines to the form it may hold. — *Why:* grant applications ask how many children use the service, how much, and what they learn. Distinct `user`s count a child twice in the month of a key rotation, since `user` changes at the first sign-in after one, and every parent's changes every quarter. A name that holds all month counts a day and a month exactly, and a name that changes with the month keeps no thread from one month to the next, so what is kept for years carries no identifier at all. The host is read from the name the client gives itself in each call, the same word the tool call's line has, rather than from the OAuth client, which no tool call knows: it is right for every family from the first day and needs nothing new in the tokens, and a client that sends no name is `unknown` — the author's choice (2026-10-04); ChatGPT's name is for its acceptance run to check. `topic_mastered` follows the progress rather than the answer's own flag, because the flag is raised too by a mastery declared below the level a topic's tasks come from now and by a second mastery above a level already shown, neither of which the parent ever sees; a topic that counts again because the child's level in it fell back is in `topics_mastered` and is no topic won. — *Consequence:* `user` stays on the same lines — the author's decision (2026-10-04) — and while the raw lines of two months are both kept it joins a child's two names, as it joins any two lines of one account; past those lines nothing joins them, and the privacy policy says so. A week that spans the turn of a month counts a child twice. A profile made anew is a new child. A learner key rotated in the middle of a month would count every child of that month twice. — *Rejected:* keeping `user` with the events for years: a pseudonymous identifier for years, and a row a few coarse fields could tie to a family. Flags in the profile for a child's first day, week and month: the day of the limits moves with a failed generation, two acceptances at once count twice, and it counts less than a name does. Distinct `user`s in a short window: a child counted twice in a rotation's month. A salt that changes every day: days alone. HyperLogLog sketches: they still need an identifier, and they give away who is in a set. The OAuth client as the host, sealed into the tokens: every family signed in before the release would be `unknown` for up to 90 days. The research behind this is [usage-evidence-for-grants.md](usage-evidence-for-grants.md) §4. — *Where it lives:* `internal/learner/`; `internal/config/config.go`; `internal/app/container.go`; `internal/transport/mcp/activity.go`, `submittask.go`, `submitanswer.go`, `labels.go`, `boundary.go`; `internal/domain/tutor/tutor.go` (`MasteredTopics`); `internal/report/events.go`, `audit.go`; `infra/terraform/secrets.tf`, `service.tf`, `variables.tf`, `outputs.tf`; `justfile` (`ci-tf-apply`); SPEC 11.2, 12.1–12.5.

**R185. Where a family is comes from two places, and as codes alone: what the parent says in the profile, and the country of the browser at the sign-in.** *(R220: the parent may have the country of the sign-in left out.)* The profile gains two optional fields: `student.country`, an ISO 3166-1 alpha-2 code, and `student.region`, a state of the United States by its ISO 3166-2 code. The list is `internal/domain/country/places.json` — the 249 codes ISO assigns and Kosovo's XK, the fifty states and the District of Columbia — which `save_profile` and `edit_profile` hold them to and the form of the profile offers, read by the widget from the same file: each country by the card's own name for it, each state by its name in English. The model sets them only when the adult says them, and never asks; the form says why they are there. At the callback, the one request of a sign-in the parent's own browser makes, the address the platform saw is looked up in DB-IP's IP to Country Lite (CC BY 4.0), read from a file at start. The code alone is sealed into the code and the tokens beside the user, travels with the account to every tool and is written as `signin_country`; the address is neither written nor kept, an address no country holds is looked up nowhere, and a code off the list is no country. The file is a month of DB-IP's, kept unchanged as an image of its own in GHCR (`just countries-publish`) and copied into the runtime image, which names it in `MATHTRAIL_COUNTRY_DB`. A deployment without it refuses to start, a file that does not open stops any start, and a machine given none knows no country. — *Why:* grant makers in the United States ask how many children are there, and a tool call comes from the host's servers, so the sign-in is the one place the family's own network shows; what a parent says is the surest source, and as complete as parents make it. DB-IP keeps a month to download for three months only, so an image pinned to its address would stop building three months on; a copy of our own keeps every month an image was built with, pinned by tag and digest like any image — the author's choice (2026-10-04). The fields are optional, and an optional field is no new version of the file (04-profile), so there is no migration. The states are named in English: no platform names subdivisions, and 51 names in 21 languages is more to keep than a parent in the United States needs — the author's choice (2026-10-04). The country travels in `store.Account`, which every tool is already handed and which the line about a task writes `user` from, rather than in a struct of its own through the token reader and the context: one field no store reads, against two hops more for one field of one line. The key and the database are required on a deployment alone, as the Google client is — the author's choice (2026-10-04). — *Consequence:* a family signed in before the release has no country of its sign-in until its next fresh sign-in, at most 90 days on. An older build drops `country` and `region` when it writes, so one given while two revisions are live may have to be given again. The runtime image is 8 MB larger, and so is its memory. A new month is published and pinned by hand, and until the first is, the image does not build. DB-IP's attribution is in `THIRD_PARTY_LICENSES` and the privacy policy, and goes on the public dashboard (T67.2). — *Rejected:* the host's own hint of where a user is: ChatGPT alone sends one, with no promise, and finer than needed. A city or coordinates. Downloading the month from DB-IP in the Dockerfile: dead in three months. A copy as a release of this repository: the releases are the service's own, numbered and signed. — *Where it lives:* `internal/domain/country/`; `internal/domain/profile/student.go`, `edit.go`, `profile.schema.json`; `internal/infra/geoip/`; `internal/transport/http/middleware/address.go` (`Sender`); `internal/transport/oauth/callback.go`, `code.go`, `grants.go`, `token.go`, `access.go`, `server.go`; `internal/store/store.go`; `internal/transport/mcp/profile.go`, `editprofile.go`, `payload.go`, `activity.go`; `web/src/widget/ProfileForm.tsx`, `ProfileScreen.tsx`, `places.ts`, `names.ts`, `editing.ts`; `web/locales/`; `Dockerfile`; `justfile` (`countries-publish`, `_license-list`); 02-auth, 04-profile, SPEC 7.1–7.2, 11.2–11.3, 12.2–12.3.

**R192. The counts are kept for years in BigQuery, made again every night from 62 days of raw lines, and shown in two sets of views: exact for the owner, and for anyone with no group of fewer than ten children.** *(Since R221 two more tables of a month keep the answers weighed against the chance promised, and three public views show them by range, with no fold.)* A deployment that sets `analytics` — off by default, on in this repository's own — copies the lines of `task_accepted`, `answer_recorded` and `topic_mastered` into a log bucket of their own, `activity`, kept 62 days, with Log Analytics on and linked to BigQuery as `activity_logs`. One scheduled query, every night at 01:30 UTC, counts every day from the day after the first line that names a child into seven tables of the dataset `impact` — every period again while the bucket, which holds 60 of its 62 days whole, still holds every line since the counting began, and after that each period while it holds every month a name in it was given for from the first of that month — deleting their rows of those periods and inserting them anew in one transaction. Every table's schema is written out, and its columns are words of a closed list a test holds them to. A day keeps totals alone: the children of the day, of the seven days to it and of the month so far, and the tasks, the answers and the topics won. The children told apart by host, language, grade, country, state, country of the sign-in and cohort are kept by calendar week and by month, each child under the value of its latest task of the period. A month keeps the tasks and active days of each child in ranges, the answers by the months since a profile was made, each topic, and the traps by topic and grade, by topic over every grade and by grade over every topic. A child the load tool — a host word of its own, `load`, since this decision — or MCP Inspector handed a task to that month is counted nowhere. Views in `impact_private` read the tables exactly. Views in `impact_public` show only months counted every day and over; fold a dimension's groups of fewer than ten into `(others)`, topping it up from the smallest, and hide a month of fewer than ten; show a topic or a trap only where ten children stand behind it; round every count to five and show none of fewer than ten; and show a share or a mean only on ten children. The new children of a month come from the folded cohort view. `just impact` prints the main numbers, public by default; `just report` adds the same lines up a day at a time beside them; the SQL is tested against a BigQuery emulator in a container (`just analytics-test`, a CI job of its own). — *Why:* the numbers grant makers ask for span months, while the log keeps a month and names a child by a name of the month; only counts outlive the lines, so nothing that could name a child is kept past them. Counting the whole window again every night needs no date of the run — which the emulator hands every statement of a script, and every statement but the first refuses — and makes a night that never ran good on the next; once the bucket lets lines go, a period is counted again only while it holds every month a name in it was given for from its first day, so that leaving out the load tool's children reads the month from its start and no period is counted from part of its days; after that it keeps the rows it was last counted to, rather than lose its week and its month to a window that has moved past their start. Until then nothing stops a night from counting everything again, so the first weeks of the counting, counted while under way or before a child of theirs was left out, come right. The counting's first day is read from the table of days once a night has counted one, so that a stretch with no line at all, longer than the bucket keeps lines, leaves no day uncounted. A child under its latest value makes a dimension's values add up to its whole, which the `(others)` rule needs: summed over its categories, a child in two would count twice, and ten in `(others)` might be fewer children. Breakdowns by week, not by day — the author's choice (2026-10-04): a day's breakdown by country or grade is often one child, and its rows together would draw that child's day for years. Shares on ten children, not on ten answers — the author's choice (2026-10-04): a hundred answers of one child are one child. Closed months alone in public — the author's choice (2026-10-04): a number that moves every day gives a child away by the difference. The load tool and Inspector left out — the author's choice (2026-10-04): their profiles are no children, and a load run hands out tasks by the dozen. A switch for copies, off by default — the author's choice (2026-10-04): a copy keeps counts only once its own privacy policy says so. — *Consequence:* the privacy policy's "no row for any one family" became what holds: a row counts a group, which may hold one child, no row names one, and no group of fewer than ten is shown publicly (SPEC 12.4). The identity Terraform applies as needs two roles more, `roles/logging.configWriter` and `roles/bigquery.admin`, so an existing deployment runs the bootstrap again before its first apply with analytics on, and that apply may have to be run twice while the counter's rights spread. Analytics on a log bucket cannot be switched off, a bucket deleted cannot be made again under its name for seven days, and the tables are protected from deletion, so removing a deployment keeps them until the protection is lifted. Where the linked dataset is kept is not documented; a check warns on every plan if it is not the region. A missing day is made good by a later night while the bucket still holds it, from what it holds; a period is counted again while the bucket holds the months its names were given for from their first day, a month or so after a month ends. A day whose seven days reach back into the month before, and a week that runs on into the month after, are counted for the last time on the night the bucket still holds the earlier month from its first day: when the two months come to 61 or 62 days, that is a day or two before the later one ends, and a child the load tool or MCP Inspector reaches on those last days stays counted there. Keeping the lines longer would close it; 62 days is what the privacy policy says. The emulator is not BigQuery: the logic is tested there, and the first week of real lines is laid beside `just report`. — *Rejected:* a destination table per partition with `WRITE_TRUNCATE`, as Google's examples write it: it names no columns, and a table partitioned by a column needs the column named beside it; `MERGE … ON FALSE`: the emulator takes a table as the source of a merge and nothing else; a scheduled query for each table: seven configurations and seven backfills for one night; the date of the run as a parameter, above; the fold as a BigQuery function over an array of structs: the emulator never tried one; breakdowns by day, above; the linked dataset's location read from a data source: unknown at plan time whenever the link changes, and a dataset's location forces its replacement; streaming the lines into BigQuery tables, Cloud Monitoring for these numbers, third-party analytics and the public report embedded in the site, as [usage-evidence-for-grants.md](usage-evidence-for-grants.md) §6 sets out. — *Where it lives:* `infra/analytics/` (`events.json`, `tables/`, `nightly.sql`, `views/`, `impact/`, the tests); `infra/terraform/analytics.tf`, `infra/terraform/analytics/`, `variables.tf`, `prod.auto.tfvars`; `infra/bootstrap.sh`; `internal/transport/mcp/labels.go`; `internal/report/children.go`, `analytics_internal_test.go`; `justfile` (`analytics-test`, `impact`); `.golangci.yml`; `.github/workflows/ci.yml`; SPEC 12.2, 12.4; [self-hosting](self-hosting.md).

**R220. The parent can have the country they sign in from left out of what is counted.** *(Refines R185; decided by the author in the planning of T71.2, 2026-10-05, a tick in the profile; carried out in T71.2.)* The profile gains an optional `student.signin_country_off`. Set, the line of a task handed out carries no `signin_country`, and the nightly counts read the field left out as `unknown`; absent, the country is counted as before. `save_profile` and `edit_profile` take it, the model setting it only when the adult asks; the form of the profile has a tick for it under the country — "Don't count the country I sign in from" —, in all 22 of the widget's languages, English and Russian written for the task and the other twenty machine translations under R140's rules; the profile's words for the model say when it is set; and the privacy policy, in English and Russian, says how to set it. The sign-in still looks the country up and seals it into the tokens, since it cannot read the profile: nothing reads it there but the line, which leaves it out. — *Why:* ChatGPT's guidelines turn away metadata such as IP addresses "unless explicitly disclosed, narrowly scoped, subject to meaningful user control" (GPT-23 in `docs/catalogs.md`): the privacy policy discloses it and the code of a country alone is kept, and a tick the parent sets is the control. — *Rejected:* not looking the country up once the tick is set, which would have the sign-in read the parent's Drive; the disclosure alone, which leaves the parent no control. — *Consequence:* the counts by the country of the sign-in count the families who ask as `unknown`. An older build reads past the field and drops it when it writes, so a tick given while two revisions are live may have to be given again (04-profile). — *Where it lives:* `internal/domain/profile/student.go`, `edit.go`, `profile.schema.json`; `internal/transport/mcp/profile.go`, `editprofile.go`, `payload.go`, `activity.go`; `web/src/widget/ProfileForm.tsx`, `editing.ts`, `payload.ts`; `web/locales/`; `site/content/en/privacy.md`, `site/content/ru/privacy.md`; `docs/parents.md`; SPEC 7.2, 12.2 and remark 38; `docs/architecture/04-profile.md`.

**R221. The live numbers of the page "Research" are counted for years by month and range, every instructions version together, and shown where ten children and thirty answers stand behind a range, with no fold.** *(T72.15.1, decided by the author on 2026-10-05 in the planning of T72.15, which it splits in two, T72.15.1, the counts, and T72.15.2, the page; it revises R204's live block.)* *(T72.15.2, 2026-10-05: the page reads the latest month counted whole from a snapshot committed each month, `site/research/live.json`, which `just site-live` takes with `infra/analytics/site/live.sql`. The bench's `page-file -live` and the site's reader refuse a cell under the rule, a count not rounded to it, a cell above its month, and a state its numbers do not give, and the reader a rule weaker than the views', which a test holds to their SQL as another holds the bench's; each range is named by what it spans, since the views name it in words and digits the page's words cannot hold; the block's styles sit in the shared stylesheet, by the author's choice. `docs/research-page.md` §11 says so.)* The nightly query of R192 reads four more fields of `answer_recorded`: the chance in hundredths, rounded rather than cut, who chose the task, the trial and the range of the child's answers. It weighs the answers the report weighs (R153): to tasks the rule chose, after the trial series and without the hint, with a range and a chance. A line the report could not read weighs nothing here either, and a chance that is not a number or lies outside nothing to one, which the report would count in its first range or its last, weighs nothing and stops nothing, where a cast that failed would stop the whole night; the service writes neither. Two more tables of a month keep them. `chance_monthly` counts them by the report's six ranges of chance and over every range, a child once in each row. `kept_up_monthly` counts them by the range of the child's answers, with the sums the mean and its standard error by child are read from: each child's margin, its right answers times a hundred less its chances in hundredths, squared and weighed by its answers, so that no child is named and every column is an integer. Three public views show them, `chances`, `chances_total` and `kept_up`: closed months alone, a range or a month only where ten children and thirty answers or more stand behind it, counts to the nearest five, and shares, means and the error to three places. Two private views give the owner the exact numbers.

— *Why:*
- The instructions version changes with nearly every release while the student model does not (R212), so cells split by version would nearly all fall under ten children.
- A child's answers in a month fall in many ranges of chance and of answers, so `(others)` would count a child more than once (`views/public/fold.sql`). R192 shows topics the same way, a topic of fewer children left out.
- What is left out can be worked out roughly by subtraction: a hidden range's answers and right answers, from the month's whole less the ranges shown, to within the rounding to five, some seven answers, as a hidden topic's answers can be from the months view. Its children cannot. The author accepted that on 2026-10-05, as R192 does for topics.
- Integer sums keep a night counted again the same to the last row, and the error reads from them exactly. The sum under the root is held at zero or more, since rounding may take an error of nothing a hair below it.
- The emulator the SQL is tested on keeps in memory what it reads, so each test of the night drops its datasets once it is over, and the tests of the views read one space made once.

— *Rejected:* cells by instructions version; the `(others)` fold of R192 for these ranges; a month shown only when every range clears both thresholds, which would keep the block empty while the rare ranges, a chance under 0.50 or answers past the 200th, stay small; FLOAT64 sums; reading BigQuery from the site's build through Workload Identity, as R204 already rejected for a monthly snapshot.

— *Consequence:*
- The privacy policy names the new kind of total, and with it the three fields of an answer's line it has lacked since R153: the chance promised, who chose the task and the range of the child's answers. A deployment keeps these counts only once its policy says so (R192).
- The apply that makes the tables must land before the bucket lets go of 1 November 2026, near 30 December 2026, or November is never counted into them.
- A month in which a release changes the student model holds two models.
- The page shows the month of the snapshot last committed, and names it: a month nobody takes a snapshot of leaves the one before on the page.

— *Where it lives:* `infra/analytics/` (`nightly.sql`, `tables/chance_monthly.json`, `tables/kept_up_monthly.json`, `views/public/chances.sql`, `views/public/chances_total.sql`, `views/public/kept_up.sql`, `views/private/chances.sql`, `views/private/kept_up.sql`, `site/live.sql`, `emulator_test.go`, `nightly_test.go`, `views_test.go`, `snapshot_test.go`); `internal/report/analytics_internal_test.go`, `answers.go` (`ChanceRanges`); `tools/learners/pagelive.go`, `pagefile.go`, `testdata/live.json`; `site/research/testdata/research.json`; `web/src/site/research.ts`, `ResearchLive.tsx`, `ResearchNumbers.tsx`, `reader.tsx`, `style.css`; `site/content/en/research.yaml`, `site/content/ru/research.yaml`; `justfile` (`site-live`, `research-data`); SPEC 8.12.2, 12.4; `site/content/en/privacy.md`, `site/content/ru/privacy.md`; `docs/grants/data-inventory.md`; `docs/self-hosting.md`; `docs/research-page.md` §11; RUN.md (T72.15.1, T72.15.2).

## The cloud

**R26. Terraform owns every cloud resource, and the three things it must not hold stay out of it.** `infra/terraform/` describes the enabled APIs, the image repository with its cleanup policies, the two secrets, the runtime identity, the Cloud Run service, its public invoker binding, the domain mapping, the spend alert and the federated identity a deployment arrives as. What it does not describe: the **values** of the secrets, which are added with `gcloud` by hand; the **project, its billing and the Google OAuth client**, which exist before the first apply; and **domain ownership and DNS**, which live with the registrar. Because a revision cannot start before its secrets hold something, the first apply is two passes — the secrets, then everything else — and that order is the self-hosting page's spine. — *Why:* a state file holding the sealing key is a copy of the key in a bucket that anyone with state access can read, and the key is the one secret whose loss forges every token; a project or an OAuth client recreated by a tool takes the parents' `drive.file` grants with it, and those cannot be recovered. — *Rejected:* `google_secret_manager_secret_version` resources with the values in variables, which puts both secrets in the state; a bootstrap module creating the project and the billing link, which makes a `destroy` capable of deleting the one thing that must never be recreated. — *Where it lives:* `infra/terraform/`, [self-hosting](self-hosting.md), T20.

**R27. The custom domain is a Cloud Run domain mapping in `us-central1`, and the platform's own address is withdrawn.** The region is chosen by two constraints at once: only a handful of regions offer domain mappings at all, and the free allowance is a discount at first-tier prices. The mapping is in Terraform, and `default_uri_disabled` is a variable that is turned on as a step of its own once the domain answers — Cloud Run asks for a domain to be mapped before its own address is withdrawn, and until the certificate is issued that address is the only way to reach anything. — *Why:* PRODUCT 7 asks for our own domain and PRODUCT 6 for $0, and a domain mapping is the only thing that satisfies both — a global external Application Load Balancer bills a forwarding rule by the hour whether or not anybody visits. One entrance also means one issuer, which is what the sign-in metadata depends on. — *Consequence:* Google labels domain mappings a preview feature and says they are not production-ready, citing latency; that is accepted for v1 and recorded as О-52, to be judged on the numbers from the live runs rather than on the label. If the latency turns out to be real, the escape hatch is a load balancer and a monthly bill. — *Rejected:* a load balancer from the start, which breaks the cost requirement before a single child has used the service; serving on the `run.app` address, which PRODUCT 7 rules out. — *Where it lives:* `infra/terraform/service.tf`, [self-hosting](self-hosting.md), PRODUCT 12.2 (О-52).

**R28. gcloud is installed for x86_64 only.** *(Since R151 the image has a pinned Python, the editor's: gcloud on arm could use it through `CLOUDSDK_PYTHON`, and stays left out until someone builds there.)* Its x86_64 archive carries a Python interpreter of its own and runs straight out of the tarball; the arm archive expects to find a system Python, and this image has none. On arm the same commands are run from Google's own `google-cloud-cli` image. Terraform, being one static binary, is installed on both. — *Why:* the alternative is a distro Python from `apt`, which would be the only unpinned thing in an image where everything else is an exact version, and an exact `apt` pin breaks on the day Ubuntu supersedes the package. Running a tool from a pinned container image is what this repository already does for everything that is not Go. — *Where it lives:* `.devcontainer/Dockerfile`, [self-hosting](self-hosting.md).

**R29. The deployment owns which image is served; the configuration owns everything around it.** `google_cloud_run_v2_service` ignores changes to `template[0].containers[0].image`, so the `image` variable is only what a newly created service starts with — the placeholder that lets a fresh project apply cleanly — and after that a deployment rolls revisions with `gcloud run services update --image <digest>`. — *Why:* two owners and no rule between them is drift by construction: a deployment publishes a digest and the next `terraform apply` puts the placeholder back. Of the two ways to settle it, this one keeps the deployment identity as narrow as T20 made it; the other — a pipeline running `terraform apply -var image=…` — would hand a workflow the state bucket and the right to change every resource in the project in order to move one string. — *Consequence:* the digest that is live is not recorded in the state, so "what is deployed" is a question for Cloud Run (`gcloud run services describe`), and a rollback is a revision — `gcloud run services update-traffic --to-revisions=…`, or the deployment run again at an older commit — never a `terraform apply`. — *Rejected:* Terraform applying from a workflow; leaving the field unignored and hoping the two owners agree. — *Where it lives:* `infra/terraform/service.tf`, `.github/workflows/deploy.yml`, T21.

**R30. A deployment is configured by repository variables, one per Terraform output, and holds no secret at all.** *(The variables half is superseded by R33: the configuration moved into the repository, and the only repository setting left is one secret. What stands is the rest — the triggers, and the federated token as the only credential. Since 2026-10-03 a push to `main` is delivered once its release is out, not on the push itself: R154.)* Six variables carry the target — the identity provider, the deployment identity, the image repository, the service, its region and the public address — and each is a copy of one output of the configuration that created it. The only credential is the federated token the workflow mints for itself; there is no service account key anywhere, and nothing in the repository or in its settings is secret. A deployment starts on a push to `main`, which is what a merged pull request produces, or by hand, and then it deploys whatever branch it was started from. — *Why:* variables rather than secrets, because none of these values is one and pretending otherwise makes them harder to read when a deployment misbehaves; one variable per output, because then the question "where did this value come from" has one answer. Pushes to `main` are the trigger because the checks gate the pull request that produced them, and running the same checks again in the deployment would double every merge for a verdict already given. — *Consequence:* a commit pushed straight to `main`, past a pull request, is deployed without the checks having finished — so the branch is protected, and the previous revision is one command away. — *Where it lives:* `.github/workflows/deploy.yml`, [self-hosting](self-hosting.md), T21.

**R32.
