The cloud is delivered by the pipeline, and what the pipeline signs in through is not the pipeline's to create.** *(Since 2026-10-03 the apply on `main` runs in the delivery that follows a release, rather than on the push: R154.)* `terraform apply` runs on every push to `main` and on a manual run from any branch; a pull request that touches `infra/` gets a `plan` in its summary. What stays outside is `infra/bootstrap.sh`, run once by a person: the project and its billing, the bucket the state lives in, the federated pool with its GitHub provider, and the identity Terraform is applied as. The two secret values are seeded by the apply itself and only when they are empty — the sealing key is 32 bytes generated in the job, the Google client secret comes from the one repository secret. — *Why:* the author's decision: deployments are not done by hand. Everything Google exposes through an API therefore belongs to the pipeline, and what is left by hand is left for a reason that can be named — no API exists (the consent screen and the OAuth client), the record lives at a registrar (DNS), or it is the door the pipeline walks in by. A sealing key generated inside the job is also simply better than one pasted through a form: nobody has to be trusted with a value nobody ever sees. — *Consequence:* an apply on every merge means a change made by hand in the console is undone, silently, by the next merge; that is the intent, and it is worth knowing before somebody edits a service in the console. The identity that applies the configuration is broad within its one project — an enumerated set of roles, not Owner — and holds no key at all: only a federated token from this repository, under a condition naming the owner's numeric id. It is deliberately not the identity that rolls out images; the narrow one from R29 stays as it was. — *Rejected:* a service account key in a repository secret, which would let a workflow bootstrap itself at the price of the one thing this design does not have; Cloud DNS, so that the records could be Terraform's too, because a managed zone is billed monthly and $0 is a requirement; `terraform apply` from a laptop, which is what this replaced and which survives as the documented path for when the pipeline is unavailable. — *Where it lives:* `infra/bootstrap.sh`, `.github/workflows/deploy.yml`, `justfile`, [self-hosting](self-hosting.md), T22.

**R33. The deployment's configuration lives in the repository.** `infra/terraform/prod.auto.tfvars` (project, region, host, client id, repository and owner id, pool id), `infra/terraform/backend.hcl` (the state bucket) and `infra/ci.env` (the provider and the identity a workflow needs before it can sign in) are committed. There are no repository variables, and there are two repository secrets: the OAuth client's secret and the id of the billing account. The billing account is the one deployment fact deliberately kept out of the files — it is not a credential, and no API accepts it without IAM, but it names the account that pays and this repository is public, so it arrives from the environment as `TF_VAR_billing_account`, which Terraform reads by itself. — *Why:* changing where this service deploys is then a pull request with a diff and a reviewer, rather than a field in a settings page whose history nobody can read. None of the values is a secret — a client id travels in every authorization request, and the pool is guarded by its condition rather than by the obscurity of its name — and the REST API that would have written repository variables needs a personal access token with `repo` scope, a credential worth more than the values it would set. — *Consequence:* a public repository states which project, project number and host this deploys to; that is accepted. A fork edits three files and runs the bootstrap, and needs nothing else. — *Where it lives:* `infra/terraform/prod.auto.tfvars`, `infra/terraform/backend.hcl`, `infra/ci.env`, `.gitignore`, T22.

**R34. The liveness probe answers on `/health`, and never on `/healthz`.** The frontend that sits in front of a Cloud Run service answers the literal path `/healthz` itself, with its own HTML page, and the request never reaches the container. Measured on the first delivery, against a service that was in fact healthy: `/healthz` was intercepted on both the platform's own address and the mapped domain, while `/health`, `/healthzz`, `/healthz/`, `/livez` and `/readyz` all arrived and were answered by our router. The deployment check asks for `/health`. — *Why:* the failure it caused is the expensive kind — a green service, a red delivery, and a probe that cannot be reached from anywhere outside. It cost an afternoon to find because everything else was working, and the difference between the two paths is one letter. — *Consequence:* the path appears in the endpoint tables, in the deployment check and in the container's own comment, which says why it is not the conventional name; a copy of this service running anywhere else is unaffected, because `/health` is reserved nowhere. — *Rejected:* `/livez` or `/readyz`, which arrive today but belong to the same family of names the platform helps itself to; `/healthz/` with its trailing slash, which our router redirects and which therefore works by accident rather than by design. — *Where it lives:* `internal/transport/http/router.go`, `justfile`, SPEC 9.1, T22.

**R35. A finding from the static analysis is answered by changing the code; accepting one is the last resort and carries a written reason.** The first full sweep of the 27 findings on the new code is what set the rule, and what it changed is worth listing, because most of it was not a considered position but a line nobody had reread: every download now refuses a protocol downgrade, on itself and on redirects; the development image is installed from a registry at an exact version rather than by running an installer script off the network, and no longer defaults to root; the deployment identity holds a role of five permissions instead of a ready-made one of sixty, which had included deleting the service and opening a root shell inside a running container; the site's footer navigation is named for a screen reader; and two shell scripts name their parameters. One finding stayed and is marked in place: the `"http://"` in the site check is the string that check searches for, and removing it removes the check. A second pass merged the two remaining adjacent `RUN` instructions and stopped there, because both other findings turned out to cost more than they were worth, and the cost was measured rather than assumed. Installing the editor's Go tools the way the rule wants means a second Go module in the repository with a `go.sum` of its own to keep current, for versions that are already exact and already verified against the Go checksum database; it was written, read, and taken back out. Forbidding every npm install step means the Claude Code CLI loses the native binary that step puts in place and starts through a Node wrapper instead, every time it runs. Both are marked, and those are the reasons. — *Why:* a mark makes the number go down and leaves the reason where it was, and this sweep found that the reasons were mostly real. The one exception is what a mark is for: an analyser that cannot tell a protocol from a search term. — *Consequence:* the identity that applies the configuration gained the right to write roles, because the configuration now defines one; a deployment that never had to delete a service can no longer do so, and if some later step turns out to need a permission the five do not cover, it fails in the delivery and the list grows by one. — *Where it lives:* `.devcontainer/Dockerfile`, `infra/terraform/deploy.tf`, `infra/bootstrap.sh`, `web/src/site/Footer.tsx`, `sonar-project.properties` (the markers `web/tools/sitecheck/check.ts` searches a stylesheet for).

**R36. The environment asks nothing of the host beyond Docker itself, and that is a rule, not a convenience.** Whoever opens this repository installs Docker and VS Code and reopens in the container; no builder plugin, no daemon configuration, nothing edited as root. Two things follow. The image is written for any builder — no `RUN --mount`, no heredocs — so build caches are what the builder happens to offer rather than something the file depends on. And the network the container runs on is created before it starts, by `.devcontainer/host-network.sh` through `initializeCommand`, carrying the MTU of the host's own default route; `post-start` mirrors that value onto the docker running inside the container, which would otherwise hand its own networks 1500 again. — *Why:* a tunnel on the host — a VPN, in practice — makes the real limit smaller than the 1500 Docker assumes, and then a container's large downloads stop after a few kilobytes while small requests answer normally; nothing in the error names the network, and the hours go into the Dockerfile instead. The first fix written for it was a daemon setting applied by hand on one machine, which is exactly the kind of instruction this project does not get to give. Detecting the host's MTU and building the container's network with it costs twenty lines and holds for anyone, not just the machine it was debugged on. — *Consequence:* the container runs on a named network rather than the default bridge, and that network is created on every start — a host without `bash`, such as Windows outside WSL, cannot run the script and needs the network made by hand. A host whose tunnel appears or disappears keeps the MTU the network was built with: the script says so and names the one command that clears it, because a network in use cannot be changed from under a running container. Downloads inside the image go through one helper with `--retry-all-errors`, because the failure this was all written for — a transfer that dies partway — is not one a plain `--retry` repeats. — *Rejected:* cache mounts and the BuildKit-only syntax they need, which bought faster rebuilds at the price of a plugin the host had to have; telling each person to set `default-network-opts` in their daemon, which is the same fix written as a chore. — *Where it lives:* `.devcontainer/host-network.sh`, `.devcontainer/devcontainer.json`, `.devcontainer/post-start.sh`, `.devcontainer/Dockerfile`.

**R49. Traces and metrics go straight to the platform's standard endpoint, and a request pays for its own delivery.** *(Since T62.1 the measurements are read only when asked — by a request that finds them due, and once when the process stops — through a reader with no clock of its own: the reader's periodic clock and the request's fell due together after a quiet minute, and Cloud Monitoring refused the second point, 4.8 s after the first. A reading with nothing in it is not posted, and the stop's delivery, which is not held to the interval, may be refused when it follows a request's by seconds.)* *(Since T64.3 the share is read off the whole trace identifier, stirred, rather than through `TraceIDRatioBased`, which kept a run of requests whole (R173); the share of a tenth, the deadline of 200 ms and an alert on no metric were measured against the deployed service and stay (R174).)* `internal/telemetry` builds a tracer provider and a meter provider on every start, and attaches OTLP-over-HTTP exporters to them only where there is somewhere to send: `https://telemetry.googleapis.com`, signed with the credentials the platform issues, naming the project in a `gcp.project_id` resource attribute and in the `X-Goog-User-Project` header. The sampler keeps a tenth of the traces whatever a request arrives saying: a remote parent, marked kept or not, goes through `TraceIDRatioBased(0.1)`, and our own spans follow their parent — the frontend's decision travels in a header a client can set too, and a client asking for every trace would be choosing what the service spends. A request whose trace is kept delivers its spans before it finishes, with a deadline of 200 ms, and measurements leave at most once a minute, as deltas, with whichever request finds them due, kept trace or not, beside the spans rather than after them so that each has the whole deadline. Off a deployment the switch is off and the providers drop everything, so no credential is needed to run the tests or the server. — *Why:* four numbers decide the shape. Google's own Go exporters (`exporter/trace` v1.38.0, `exporter/metric` v0.62.0) are deprecated and will be archived after 1 January 2027, and they post to this same endpoint, so taking them buys nothing and costs a migration. A sidecar collector wants 0.25–0.5 vCPU and 256–512 MB on every instance against 180 000 vCPU-seconds of free allowance, which is the whole budget spent on carrying telemetry rather than on answering children. `cpu_idle = true` means the processor is taken away once a response has been returned, so a batch left in memory after that is a batch that may never leave — hence a delivery inside the request. And the free allowances are what the labels are for: the platform's own system metrics and automatic traces are not billed at all, Logging gives 50 GiB a month, Trace 2.5 million spans, and custom metrics 150 MiB — a label carrying a user or a task identifier passes that last one in a week, so every label is a closed dictionary and every histogram's buckets are written out. *(Corrected in T64.5: metrics sent as OTLP are billed as Prometheus samples, $0.06 a million, with no free allotment — the 150 MiB is the allotment of metrics billed by bytes, which these are not. A point of a counter is one sample, of a histogram two and one for each bucket that is not empty, and a delivery carries up to about twenty: at a few lessons a day that is under a cent a month, and with three instances answering the whole month under $0.20. The labels stay closed for the same reason: every series that changed is samples in every delivery. Whether to keep sending them is О-58.)* — *Consequence:* the resource is built with `contrib/detectors/gcp`, which adds two Apache-2.0 modules and one call to the platform's metadata service at startup, and is what makes the instance identifier in a span the same one the console shows. HTTP rather than gRPC as the transport, because the credentials then attach as an ordinary `http.Client` and the collector a test posts to is an ordinary `httptest` server; it saves nothing in the binary, because the exporter's own configuration package imports gRPC whichever transport is chosen. Exporting at all costs this build 186 packages and 19 MB — 24.8 MB to 43.5 MB, measured — and that is the price of OTLP in Go rather than of anything decided here. It is also a standing maintenance cost: the image scan reads the modules linked into the binary rather than the code that runs, so a gRPC advisory now fails the build of a service that serves no gRPC, and the answer each time is to raise the version. The delivery carries its own 200 ms rather than trusting whoever asked for it to remember one, so a caller that brought no deadline still gets its goroutine back, and it is detached from the request, because a caller who hung up would otherwise take the spans with them. A delivery of spans in the background is bounded by the batcher that makes it, five seconds with every retry inside them: the exporter's own timeout applies only to a client it builds for itself, and it is handed the signed one. Measurements have no delivery in the background: they leave with a request or at the stop. The collector's root is held to one rule, `collector.Root` in a package of its own that imports no exporter, so the configuration checks it with the same rule without carrying the SDK — http or https, a host and at most a path, with no user, query or fragment, and a refusal that never repeats what a secret could hide in — and the configuration adds only that it be https off this machine. The caller of a sampled request waits for the delivery too: a small answer leaves the process only when the handlers return, and the delivery is the last of them. That deadline costs what it saves: a delivery that misses it loses its batch, since the SDK discards a batch that failed to send rather than holding it for the next attempt, and the first delivery from a new instance — the one paying for the connection — is the likeliest to miss. Accepted, because the alternative is a child waiting on a collector; the number is revisited against the latencies of T64 rather than against a guess. and the one setting that belongs to the whole binary — the handler that reports a failed delivery — is installed at the composition root beside the web framework's mode, never by the constructor, because a constructor that reaches for a global changes what every other part of the same process reports. Neither a missing project nor missing credentials stops the service: both are reported once and leave export switched off, because an image can be rolled before the configuration that names the project reaches it, and a service that refused to start over telemetry would be an outage caused by observability. Enabling the telemetry endpoint is not enough on its own, which the first live run is what found: it accepts the data and decides nothing about keeping it, so a project without Cloud Trace has no bucket for a span to land in and one without Cloud Monitoring answers every measurement with a 403. The service reports either once and keeps serving, so nothing about it is loud — `apis.tf` enables all three. Enabling them is all it takes, and the first live run is also what showed how that looks while it is happening: the storage behind each is provisioned after the first data arrives rather than before, so for some twenty minutes a trace read answers `_Trace bucket not found` and no metric exists anywhere, while the service reports nothing because nothing of its own failed. Documented behaviour for Cloud Run — whose spans, unlike other senders', are not supposed to provision that storage at all — made this look like a missing manual step, and it is not one; `docs/self-hosting.md` says to wait rather than to click. Granting the runtime identity `roles/telemetry.writer` and `roles/serviceusage.serviceUsageConsumer` is the first binding this configuration makes on the project itself, so the identity Terraform is applied as gained `roles/resourcemanager.projectIamAdmin` in `infra/bootstrap.sh` — an existing project needs that grant re-run once before the first apply that carries this. — *Still open:* from 1 September 2026 Cloud Monitoring charges $0.35 a month for every metric named in an alerting policy. The spend alert of T20 is a Billing budget and is free of this; whether any of our own metrics is worth alerting on is a decision for T64. — *Rejected:* Google's own exporters, above; a sidecar collector, above; the Google Cloud Trace agent's `X-Cloud-Trace-Context` alone, which would tie the service to one platform's header instead of the standard one; relying on the periodic reader by itself, which under `cpu_idle` fires only when a request happens to wake the instance. — *Where it lives:* `internal/telemetry`, `internal/config`, `internal/app/container.go`, `infra/terraform/apis.tf`, `infra/terraform/service.tf`, `infra/bootstrap.sh`, SPEC 11.2 and 12.5.

**R173. The share of traces is read off the whole trace identifier, stirred.** *(Decided in T64.3, 2026-10-04.)* A trace is kept when its identifier — the right half put through the finalizer of MurmurHash3's 64-bit hash and folded into the left, the whole put through it again — falls within the share, where `TraceIDRatioBased` reads the right half alone. What a request arrives saying of its trace still counts for nothing, and our own spans follow their parent, as R49 has it. — *Why:* the live run against `mcp.mathtrail.app` found 0.007 of an hour's 828 traced requests kept, against a tenth configured. The platform's front end makes the identifier of a request that arrives without one, and fills its right half once for a run of requests and only the left half anew for each: the hour's 830 requests fell into 64 runs, thirteen of them 20 to 75 requests long, and read by the right half the runs were kept or dropped whole — 7 requests, in 4 runs. Read by the left half, the same requests keep 89, 0.107. The 41 requests the front end sampled itself shared one with the 6 we kept, so the share does decide rather than the front end, which R49 asked to be confirmed — but it decided a run at a time, and the share it kept was the runs' chance: the rest of that week, 941 requests of real lessons and sign-ins, came out at 0.116. — *Rejected:* the left half alone, which a client's own identifier may fill with a clock, as AWS X-Ray's does with its first 32 bits; a decision drawn at random for each request, which would keep part of a trace a client carries over several requests; OpenTelemetry's consistent probability sampler, which reads the right 56 bits, the same half. — *Consequence:* a property test holds the share kept to the share configured whichever half of the identifier changes, and fails on the standard sampler. Another process's standard sampler would decide a trace otherwise, and no other process of ours samples one. The deployed share is read off the report of the first deployment of this build. — *Where it lives:* `internal/telemetry/sampling.go` and `sampling_internal_test.go`; SPEC 12.5; `docs/live/09-load.md`.

**R50. A request cannot forge a log line, and the encoder is what stops it — not a sanitiser.** *(Superseded by R76 for the path and the verb, which no longer reach a log line: the alerts this entry answers went with them, and nothing here is a reason to dismiss a new one. What still stands is the encoder and its test, as the guard for whatever else a field carries.)* The code scan reports every place a request's own path, method or query reaches a log call (CWE-117, four alerts across `middleware/logger.go` and `middleware/recovery.go`). They are kept and answered rather than removed: a deployment writes JSON, a JSON encoder escapes newlines, quotes and control bytes, and `internal/logger` carries the test that holds it to that — a path of `/x\n{"severity":"ERROR",…}` comes back as one object whose `path` is that whole string and whose `severity` is still the one this service gave it. — *Why:* the attack the rule describes is a caller ending the line they are in and starting one of their own, and the encoder makes it impossible; the test says so in a way that a comment could not, because it goes red the day somebody swaps the encoder. The console format does not escape, and that is what it is for: it is read by a person at a terminal and never by a collector. A deployment refuses it, so that "never" is enforced rather than hoped for. — *Rejected:* stripping control characters out of the path before logging it, which would silence the alert and cost the one thing the line is read for — what was actually asked — while defending against nothing the encoder does not already; marking the alerts dismissed with no test behind the reason, which is the same claim without the evidence. — *Consequence:* the alerts stay open on the dashboard unless somebody dismisses them by hand, and the reason to give is this entry. — *Where it lives:* `internal/logger/logger_test.go`, `internal/transport/http/middleware/logger.go`, `internal/transport/http/middleware/recovery.go`.

**R73. A request's log line carries only the parameters defined to hold no data, and a panic is one field of it.** The request log writes the query parameters on a short list the sign-in and its protocols define — `code_challenge_method`, `error`, `grant_type`, `prompt`, `response_mode`, `response_type` and `scope` as the words their protocols define, with anything else in them written as `other` once, and `client_id`, `redirect_uri` and `resource` as a scheme and a host, which say which client or resource it is — and only counts the others, names included. A panic is caught by the recovery next to the handlers, below tracing, counting and the request log, so that a panicked request is answered 500, logged in its own request line, counted and delivered like any other; the line says what the request panicked with in the runtime's own words for a fault of the runtime, found inside whatever wrapped it, and by the value's type alone for anything else, beside the stack. Above everything stands a last resort for a panic of the middleware itself, which writes the one line the request log could not. A handler that panics with `http.ErrAbortHandler`, however wrapped, is passed on to the server as the abort it is: it is neither a panic nor an answer. The delivery of a sampled request's spans runs on the way out of an abort too, so it catches a panic of its own and writes it as a line of its own: a panic there would replace the abort with a 500. The errors a request collected are written by the same rule as a panic — a fault of the system in the system's own words, anything else by its type — because the text of a failed write names both ends of the connection; and for the same reason a span reaches the exporter without the text of its status, which the instrumentation fills with those errors. A connection that died under the service's own call is a fault like any other, since the framework reports a client that hung up as an error of the request rather than a panic. — *Why:* no personal data of a child, no task and no answer may reach a log, and a list of what to hide keeps out only what somebody thought of: a provider's `login_hint` carries a parent's email address and its `hd` the domain of a school, a name a caller chose can carry anything a value can, an address can hide an email address or a name in its path, its query or its user, and a panic value is whatever the code that panicked had in hand. A runtime fault is described by the runtime in types and numbers, the one kind of panic text that cannot carry data — and only a fault the runtime made, since any type can call itself a runtime error. One fault is one line, which is also what keeps a panic from being counted twice by whoever alerts on errors. — *Consequence:* a parameter this service starts to rely on is only counted until it is added to the list, and a panic of our own is found by its stack rather than its message. A value is chosen as freely as a name, so a listed parameter is held to its protocol's words — OAuth 2.0 with PKCE, resource indicators and registration, OpenID Connect, and the scopes this service and Google's sign-in speak, `mcp`, `openid` and `drive.file` among them — and an error of `alice@school.example` or a scope of a child's name is written as `other`. A scope the service starts to use is `other` in the log until it is added to the words. The recovery is this service's own rather than the framework's, because the framework's swallows an abort and lets the request be logged and counted as a 200. — *Rejected:* the list of secrets to mask, which is what this replaced; logging the panic's message after scrubbing it, which cleans a string rather than never writing it; a second line for the panic beside the request's. — *Where it lives:* `internal/transport/http/middleware/logger.go`, `query.go`, `recovery.go`, `tracing.go`, `internal/logger/panic.go` (where the rule for a panic moved in T41, so that the MCP endpoint writes a panic by it too), `internal/telemetry/redact.go`, `internal/transport/http/router.go` (T39a.3).

**R76. A request is named in a log line by the route it matched, never by the path it asked for.** The request's own line and the last resort's write `route` — the pattern the router declared, or `other` when nothing matched — and the method from the same closed list the metrics use, with `other` for a verb nobody defined. A probe is told by its route too, so the path a caller wrote is not read at all, and the traces blank the path and the verb the instrumentation records for itself. — *Why:* R73's own reason reaches the path: a name a caller chose can carry anything, and a path nothing matched is text a caller wrote, as free as the value of a query, which R73 already keeps out; a matched path carries identifiers the day a route has a parameter. The metrics and the traces already name a request this way (R49), so one request now reads the same in all three. It also removes what the code scan reports under CWE-117 rather than answering it: a fifth alert, raised when the path moved into `LastResort`, blocked the merge of the pull request that moved it. — *Consequence:* our log no longer says which path a 404 asked for. The platform's own request log still carries the whole address of every request, and that is where a path a host probed for — the sign-in's metadata documents, say — is found. A 405 is written as `other` too, since the framework matches no route for it. For the same reason a request for `/health` with a method nobody declared is no probe any more: it is logged, counted and traced like any other request that failed. — *Rejected:* keeping the path and dismissing each alert as R50 did, which answers the forged line and not the caller's text; stripping control characters out of the path, which R50 already rejected; holding the path's segments to a list of protocol words the way the query is held, which would keep the well-known paths readable at the cost of a second list to maintain, for what the platform's log already has. — *Where it lives:* `internal/transport/http/middleware/route.go`, `logger.go`, `recovery.go`, `probe.go`, `tracing.go`, `middleware_test.go`, `observability_test.go`, `internal/telemetry/redact.go`, SPEC 12.2.

**R141. The log is audited by a whole lesson through the service as it is built, and added up by a package that reads the service's own lines.** *(Since T64.2 the table of fields is the report's, names every event, and holds a deployment's log as well as the lesson; the test keeps the list of the events a lesson leaves — R169.)* The audit is a test rather than a document. `internal/app` runs one family's whole way through the container `NewContainer` builds: the host registering, the parent through the consent screen and a stand-in for Google, the child's profile in a stand-in for Drive, a race asked for, asked for again while its request is open, refused once and accepted, answered wrongly and told again, the progress, the day's limit, a renewal and the end of the access — with a stranger's token and a client on a private network on the way. Every line it leaves is held to two rules. It carries nothing sent or issued on the way — the pseudonym, the interests and the notes, the words of the task and of its program, a letter standing as a value of its own, the name the host gives itself, every token, code, cookie, state and verifier, Google's subject and the service's secrets, the id of the file in Drive, the addresses the requests came from — and no email address at all, each looked for in every value as it is written and as it reads with its percent-encoding undone. And it is a line of an event the lesson is known to leave, carrying only the fields decided for that event: a table in the test, SPEC 12.2 made executable, in which every event has to appear in the lesson, so that the table lists nothing the lesson does not reach. The lines together, written out as JSON, are then held to adding up in the report to what the lesson was. The container reaches Google's sign-in and Drive through one seam, the pair of addresses `NewContainer` sets to Google's own and the test replaces through the package's export file. `internal/report` adds lines up: the tasks asked for, handed in, refused and out of attempts, the checks that refused them, and the attempts and the seconds an accepted task took, by instructions version and chat host; the limits reached; and the tool calls by host, with the median and the 95th percentile of their time, every percentile by the nearest rank. A task is counted for the host of its call, read off the call's `tool_call` line: the one with the span the task's line names, since a span is the service's own, or, where the service names no span because it knows no project, the one of the same request, whose id a client may have chosen; a task whose call's line was not read is counted for no host, as `(call not read)`, never for the `unknown` the service writes for a client that gave no name. Cloud Logging keeps every number of a line as a double, so a whole number reads whether it is written 2 or 2.0, and a line of the service's that still does not read is counted and said, never left out as another's. `cmd/report` prints the report from standard input. `just report` feeds it the deployed service's lines — read whole into a file from Cloud Logging with `gcloud logging read`, in the project `just _project` finds in the Terraform configuration, and restored from their entries with `jq` — and a log of a local run is fed to it as it stands. — *Why:* the leak an audit exists for is the one nobody thought of, and a list of what to look for finds only what is on it; a field held to a table fails the test the day somebody adds it without deciding on it, before any value it carries can. The first run found what the tests of each package had missed: `task_skipped` wrote the topic of the task on the card as the file held it, so a parent who typed the child's name there would have had it logged. The topic is now held to the catalog, as `answer_recorded`'s already was. A lesson through the real composition sees the lines the parts leave together — the sign-in's, the lesson's and the Drive store's — which no package's own test does, and it is the one place the report can be held to the fields the service really writes rather than to a fixture written by hand; that second reader is why the adding up is a package of its own and not the command's. The numbers of О-16 need no backend: Cloud Logging keeps the lines, and one command reads them. A task's lines do not name the chat host; a field of their own for it is T67.1's `host`, taken from the sign-in rather than from the name a client gives itself. — *Rejected:* log-based metrics, or Log Analytics with BigQuery, for these numbers: a backend and a cost the MVP does not need, which T67.2 builds for the numbers that must outlive the raw lines. The adding up written in `jq` inside the recipe, which no test could hold. A Go client of the Logging API, which would bring Google's libraries into the module for a read `gcloud` already makes and signs. The audit as a document listing every line, which is out of date the day after. Renaming the lines of starting and stopping to one word each: nothing but their tests reads them, and SPEC 12.1 now names their shape instead. — *Consequence:* a new event or field on a line a lesson leaves fails the test until the table and SPEC 12.2 say it. An event the lesson does not reach — a conflict in Drive, a panic — is held by its own package's tests alone, and so is the column of seconds, since the lesson spends none. The report counts a request out of attempts by the product's own ceiling, so a log older than a change to that ceiling would be misread, and a request whose lines straddle a new release is counted under each version its lines carry. `ci-tf-apply` reads its project through `_project` too. The first read of the deployed log showed `caller` on every line, which SPEC 12.1 now names; holding the deployed lines' fields to the table, and `just report` itself against the deployed log, are the author's to run. — *Where it lives:* `internal/app/log_test.go`, `family_test.go`, `racetask_test.go`, `export_test.go`, `container.go`; `internal/transport/mcp/nexttask.go`, `submittask.go`, `labels.go`; `internal/report`, `cmd/report`; `justfile` (`report`, `_project`); SPEC 12.1–12.4 and 12.6 (T60).

**R169. The report holds every line of a deployment's log to the rules of the log, and adds up a tool's time without Drive, the traces and the busiest minute; what the platform counted is read beside it.** *(T64.2, 2026-10-04.)* The table of the fields each event may carry moved out of the lesson's test into `internal/report` and names every event of SPEC 12.2 — the process starting and stopping, the telemetry, the panics, and Drive's conflicts, stale reads and recoveries beside the lesson's. The lesson's test keeps only the list of the events a lesson leaves and holds their lines to the report's table, and the report holds every line it reads to the same rules: an event the service is decided to write, the fields decided for it, and nothing shaped like an email address in any value or name. A line that breaks one is named by its event, its field and the rule, never by what it held: an event the table does not name is not named, since its words may be anything, and a field named otherwise than the service names its fields is withheld; a line break in a cell is written as a space. `just report` therefore reads every line of the window rather than the five events it used to — what the service wrote on its standard output and error, and none of the platform's own records — and puts the trace back beside the span — a trace the entry says nothing of read as dropped, since Cloud Logging leaves a false out — and the instance beside the line from the entry's labels, streaming the entries out of their file one at a time. A tool's time without Drive is its time less that of the calls to Drive of its request, tied by the request's id and the trace together, since either alone can be another request's as well, and never less than nothing. The traces are counted from each request's own line and the failed deliveries from theirs, joined by the request's id — an id requests of both kinds came with tells nothing — and a failure whose words say the deadline was exceeded is counted as late; the share the telemetry was built to keep is the newest build's in the window. The busiest minute is a minute by the clock, the one a call or a request arrived in: its line is written as it ends, less how long it took. `just usage` reads Cloud Monitoring's `container/cpu/allocation_time`, `container/memory/allocation_time`, `request_count`, `container/billable_instance_time` and, of the instances that served, `container/instance_count` on average over each minute, for the service, a minute at a time, with `curl` and the token `gcloud` prints, and sets the window against the free tier of a month. — *Why:* T64 checks the real log for personal data, and a check by eye finds what the eye knows to look for; the rule the lesson's test enforces is already decided, and made whole it holds both. The five events the recipe read had left out the answers the report has weighed since T72.1, and the lines the new numbers need. The time without Drive is the number PRODUCT 6 sets its second against; the traces are how SPEC 12.5's share and its 200 ms are revisited against real load; the busiest minute is what the paces of SPEC 10.2 are counted over. The free tier is counted in vCPU-seconds and GiB-seconds, which the two allocation metrics give as they are, where the instance time would have to be multiplied by a size the deployment can change. — *Consequence:* an event added without its fields in the table fails the lesson's test when the lesson reaches it, and shows in the report of a deployed log when it does not. Reading every line makes a long window slower to read. The instance is the reader's: no event is decided to carry it, so the lesson's test still catches the service writing one, and the report passes it as the field its reader added. An account's pace counts every message it sends, but a line names the account on a tool call alone, so the busiest minute of an account is of its tool calls, with the requests the endpoint was sent for each beside it. Running both recipes against the deployment is the author's: it reads production. — *Rejected:* a second table for the deployed log, which would drift from the lesson's; log-based metrics or a dashboard for these numbers, a backend and a cost (R141); a Go client of the Monitoring API, which would bring Google's libraries into the module for a read `curl` and `gcloud`'s token make. — *Where it lives:* `internal/report` (`events.go`, `audit.go`, `traffic.go`, `report.go`, `tally.go`, `write.go`), `internal/app/log_test.go`, `justfile` (`report`, `usage`), SPEC 12.4 and 12.5.

**R174. The paces, the fuse, the share of traces and the deadline of a delivery stay as they were, measured, and no metric is alerted on.** *(Decided in T64.3, 2026-10-04.)* *(T64.5, 2026-10-04: Cloud Monitoring's refusals were not two instances writing one series. Google files an OTLP measurement under the `instance` of `prometheus_target`, taken from `faas.instance` when the resource names no `service.instance.id`, and each refusal names its resource with it. All eight came from builds before T62.1 reached the service, the last on 2026-10-02 at 02:10 UTC: the library's own clock delivering 1 to 5.5 seconds after a request's, `http_request` and `target_info` among the series. Since T62.1 none has come, and no stop's delivery has failed either. The resource stays as it is.)* The load ran `paces` and `ceiling` against `mcp.mathtrail.app` with two accounts a parent signed in, the service's concurrency lowered from 80 to 2 for the run so that the platform started three instances, and set the run beside a week of the service's log. The numbers of SPEC 10.2 stay: an account's 60 messages a minute with a burst of 20, an address's 20, an instance's 200 at each of its doors, and five failed generations a day. The share of traces stays a tenth, read as R173 reads it, and a delivery's deadline stays 200 ms. No alerting policy names a metric: the Billing budget of `infra/terraform/budget.tf`, a dollar a month with its three thresholds, stays the one alert. — *Why:* the busiest minute of real lessons held 15 tool calls of one account — the card of a task asking how it stands every four seconds — and 19 messages to the MCP endpoint on one instance: the account's pace is four times the one and the instance's ten times the other. The load's greedy account, sending six calls a second, was let through 178 in a minute, three instances' allowance, as a pace counted in an instance's memory is (SPEC 10.1), and every call refused was refused by its pace; the accounts beside it walked a lesson and read their profiles, and none of their calls was refused. The ceiling refused the sixth request of the failing account and nothing else. One instance took 247 messages in a minute, most of them refused by the account's pace before the instance's counted them, and the tools' own time, without Drive, stayed within 13 ms at the 95th percentile, within 33 ms in a week of real lessons — two accounts cannot reach an instance's 200, and the fuse decides nothing on a day of lessons. The run cost 169 vCPU-seconds and 323 GiB-seconds, under a tenth of a per cent of a month's free tier. A tenth of the requests, with at most about ten spans each, stays within Trace's 2.5 million spans a month for as long as the requests stay within Cloud Run's two million; a fifth would not. In the week, 14 deliveries of spans ran out of the deadline beside 115 kept traces: 6 in the first five seconds of an instance, the connection R49 expected to miss it, and 8 on instances from 18 seconds to 25 minutes old. The deadline is what a child's wait is bounded by; the log has the time of every request whatever becomes of its trace, and a sample that loses a trace in eight is still a sample. A metric named in an alerting policy costs $0.35 a month (R49), a third of the budget for one alert; the budget alerts on the spend for nothing, and `just report` and `just usage` tell the rest whenever they are asked, which at a few lessons a day is often enough. — *Rejected:* a longer deadline: the log does not say how long a delivery that succeeded took, so a longer one would buy an unknown share of the lost spans with a longer wait for a child; a fifth of the traces, above; an alert on refusals, failures or latency, above. — *Consequence:* SPEC 10.2 and 12.5 say the numbers were measured. Three things the same run found stay open (`docs/live/09-load.md`): Cloud Monitoring refused 8 deliveries of measurements in the week as written sooner than its sampling period after another point of the same series — 5.5 s apart in the one read, where an instance delivers at most once a minute, so most likely two instances writing one series; whether another address is served while the greedy one is held back, which a phone on a mobile network was to show and did not; and the deployed share of traces under R173, which the first deployment of that build shows. — *Where it lives:* SPEC 10.2 and 12.5; `docs/live/09-load.md`; RUN.md (T64.3).

**R74. The way out is one budget, in fixed shares.** `MATHTRAIL_SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT`, nine seconds by default, is the whole time from a stop being asked for to the process ending: the server drains the requests in flight for three quarters of it, and the container is closed in the quarter left — a start that fails halfway is closed in the same quarter. — *Why:* the platform stops the process ten seconds after asking it to. Before this the drain and the close took the whole budget each, twenty seconds between them, and the part cut off was the telemetry's last delivery. Fixed shares keep each clock where it is used: the server measures its drain from the moment it sees the stop, the close is measured from the moment the server has returned, and the two add up to the budget with nothing to keep in step. The default stops a second short of the platform's ten, for the moment the signal takes to arrive and the lines written after the close. — *Consequence:* a close that needed more than its quarter does not get the time a quick drain left unused. The close is mostly the telemetry's final delivery, which takes well under a second against a collector that answers. A way out shorter than a second is refused at start, since a quarter of it would be no time to deliver anything in. A drain cut short by a request still running is a warning, not a failure: the stop was asked for and happened, and the process ends as it would have — an exit code of failure would dress every scale-down with a slow request up as a crash. — *Rejected:* one deadline taken when the stop arrives and shared by both, the drain taking up to its share and the close whatever is left. The process has to learn the moment of the stop outside the server, which costs either a wait on the group's context ahead of the group itself — a hang the day a member returns without that context ending — or a second clock kept in step with the server's; and it pulls the close out of a deferred call, where a panic or an early return would skip it. — *Where it lives:* `internal/config/config.go` (`DrainTimeout`, `CloseTimeout`), `internal/app/server.go`, `internal/app/container.go`, `cmd/server/main.go`, SPEC section 11 (T39a.6).

**R77. A stop that arrives while the service is starting is a stop, not a failed start.** When building the container fails because the process was told to stop — its context has ended, and the error is that ending passed back — `run` writes `shutdown` with `started: false` as information and the process exits with 0. Any other failure of the build is still a failed start, written once as an error, even when a stop arrived beside it. — *Why:* the platform sends its stop to an instance that is still starting when it replaces a revision or scales down, and with export switched on the trace exporter refuses to start on an ended context. That was an error line and an exit code of 1 for a stop that had been asked for, which anyone alerting on errors would read as a crash. Without export the same stop already ended cleanly, at the listener. — *Rejected:* treating every cancellation as a stop, which would also hide a context this process was never asked to end. — *Where it lives:* `cmd/server/main.go`, `cmd/server/main_test.go`, SPEC 12.2.

**R75. How a text reads on a card lives with the solver's options.** `solver.Key` and `solver.Blank`, with the folding behind them, decide when two texts are one answer and when a text shows nothing. They sit in `internal/domain/solver` beside the options' labels, although `checks`, `profile` and `content` also ask `Blank` about texts that are not options — a constraint, an explanation, the options of a stored task. — *Why:* it is one rule. The solver's `match` and the structure check have to agree on which options are one, a stored task has to be judged blank by the measure that let it in, and a reference task has to load by the measure a submitted one is held to. Whatever package held the rule, all four would import it: a package of its own would move the import rather than remove it, and would put the options' labels and the comparison of their texts in two places that only ever change together. — *Consequence:* a change to how a card reads changes which stored profiles validate and which reference tasks load. That is intended: they are the same texts. — *Rejected:* a small package for the text of a card, which is the same coupling under another name (T39a). — *Where it lives:* `internal/domain/solver/options.go`, SPEC 5.2.

**R51. Eight Scorecard findings stay open, and none of them is a thing to fix.** They arrive as code-scanning alerts beside CodeQL's and are answered here once rather than re-argued at every scan. **Two are false for this configuration.** `Dangerous-Workflow` (critical) reports the release workflow checking out `workflow_run.head_sha`; Scorecard does not read the `if:` that guards the only job in that file, which requires the triggering run to be a push whose head repository is this one, so a fork's pull request can never reach it — the same reasoning R40 records. `Vulnerabilities` reports GO-2026-5932, the advisory that `golang.org/x/crypto/openpgp` is unmaintained and unsafe by design; the module is required for `chacha20poly1305` in `seal` and that one package is imported nowhere, which `govulncheck` — which follows reachability rather than a dependency list — confirms by finding nothing. The advisory has no fixed version, so no upgrade clears it. **Three need a second person.** `Code-Review` counts approved changesets and finds none, `Branch-Protection` wants required approvers, codeowners review, stale-review dismissal and last-push approval, and every one of those asks somebody other than the author to approve. A solo project cannot satisfy them, and inventing a second account to do so would be worse than the score. **Two heal by themselves.** `Maintained` warns that the repository is younger than ninety days, and `SAST` counts thirteen of twenty-six commits scanned because the CodeQL workflow was added halfway through the history; both improve with nothing but time, as does `CI-Tests` at twenty-five of twenty-six. **One is already decided.** `Pinned-Dependencies` wants the npm install pinned by hash; R35 explains why that install step exists at all, and npm verifies the tarball against the registry's own integrity hash, which is the verification available to a global install. — *Why:* a check that will be red for the life of a one-person project is not a defect, and treating it as one costs either a fabricated reviewer or an afternoon per scan. Naming them once means the next reader can tell the eight that are answered from the one that would be new. — *Consequence:* the Scorecard score stays where it is, deliberately; a finding that is not on this list is worth reading. — *Where it lives:* `.github/workflows/release.yml`, `.github/workflows/codeql.yml`, `.devcontainer/Dockerfile`, R35, R40.

## The public site

**R23. The site is built by a small Go program, and all of it is temporary.** *(The renderer was replaced in T66, R143, and goldmark left with it; `golang.org/x/net` stays a direct dependency after T66c too, because the sign-in's redirect check uses its `idna`.)* `cmd/sitegen` builds `site/` into `site/dist/` with `html/template` and goldmark; `cmd/sitecheck` then judges the finished directory. Both are replaced by the widget's own Preact and Vite toolchain once that exists — the renderer first, the checker one task later, for the reason in R25. — *Why:* the privacy policy has to be live before the first deployment, because Google will not release the consent screen without it, and `web/` does not exist yet at that point. Reaching for npm here would either block the policy behind the widget build or introduce a second, throwaway JavaScript toolchain. Go is already pinned, already in the image, and already the language the tests are written in. — *Rejected:* hand-written HTML per page and per locale, which duplicates the masthead, the footer and the language switcher into every file and drifts by the third locale; a static site generator, which is a new toolchain and a new license to track for six pages. — *Consequence:* goldmark and a direct `golang.org/x/net` sit in `go.mod` for programs the service does not ship, and both leave with the programs that needed them. — *Where it lives:* `internal/site/`, `cmd/sitegen`, `cmd/sitecheck`, T19, T66, T66c.

**R24. The address scheme is decided once and never moved.** *(Since T66c the site's checker holds the list of published addresses, R25.)* *(Since R158 a topic's page has an address a level deeper, `/<locale>/topics/<slug>/`, and anchors linked from outside their page are held like addresses.)* *(`sitecheck` catches a published page only while something on the site still links to it; since R143 a test of the build holds the list of published addresses, the apex among them.)* *(Since R206 the apex sends the reader on to the English front page at once, and the x-default of every page, a front page's included, is the reference locale's version.)* Every page lives at `/<locale>/` or `/<locale>/<page>/`; the apex is a page in its own right — the product's name, its one line and the languages it exists in — rather than a redirect. hreflang x-default points at the apex for a front page and at the reference locale for anything else. — *Why:* the addresses of the privacy policy and the terms are handed to Google for the consent screen and to the directories for the listing, and neither forgives a link that moved. The apex is what a product is listed under, and a redirect is a poor thing to list; Google also expects the policy to sit on the domain that hosts the home page, which the scheme satisfies by construction. — *Consequence:* T66 may replace the renderer but not the addresses, and `sitecheck` fails the build if a page that was published disappears. The authorized domain registered in T22 is the top private domain, which covers both the site on the apex and the service on its subdomain. — *Where it lives:* `web/src/site/addresses.ts`, `web/tools/sitecheck/`, T19, T22, T66, T66c.

**R25. The checker outlives the renderer by exactly one task, and then the site owns no Go at all.** *(Done in T66c: the checker is `web/tools/sitecheck/`, `parse5` reads its pages, and it holds a sixth rule beside the five — every address the site has published is still there, a list the build's test held until then; `internal/site/` and `cmd/sitecheck` are gone, and `golang.org/x/net/html` with them. The page's title is the document's first, as a browser reads it, so a title inside an SVG no longer stands for the page's.)* *(Since T66 the build's own tests run in `just ci-web` beside `just ci-site`, R143.)* T66 replaces the renderer and keeps `cmd/sitecheck` untouched; T66c ports the five rules to TypeScript under `web/tools/sitecheck/` and deletes `internal/site/` and `cmd/sitecheck`. The ported checker takes a directory and nothing else, and a lint rule forbids it to import from `web/src/**`. — *Why:* the author's decision that no Go about the site survives the project. Doing it in two steps is not caution: while the renderer moves, the old checker is the only judge that has no stake in the new builder, so "the Vite output still passes the unchanged checker" is real evidence rather than a build agreeing with itself. The same reasoning is what the lint rule protects afterwards — a checker that imports the builder's `hreflang` tests that a function equals itself, and in one language that import is one keystroke away. — *Rejected:* keeping the Go checker for good, which leaves a second toolchain in the repository for one program; off-the-shelf npm checkers, which cover dead links and markup validity but not the three rules that matter most to a site in twenty-two languages — no third-party loads, a complete hreflang matrix, and no locale quietly missing a page; moving both at once, which would leave a disagreement with no arbiter. — *Consequence:* after T66 the site is no longer covered by `just ci-test`, and `just ci-site` — already on every pull request — becomes its only gate. The design tokens stay a plain CSS file rather than a bundle artefact, because the consent screen and the sign-in error pages are rendered by the service itself and read the same file (SPEC 8.9). — *Where it lives:* `web/tools/sitecheck/`, T48, T66, T66c.

**R142. The site has a look of its own: white, light only, in the manner of Google's product pages.** *(Its fonts are revised by R157: the site sets its text in Onest, from its own domain. Its reference is the author's new draft since R156, and the card on the site keeps its own look, as this entry has it.)* *(Since R167 the footer is white under a hairline, in grey type, as the draft draws it, rather than a grey band, and the header stays on top of the page as it scrolls; the palette is the draft's.)* *(Since R191 the header's mark is MathTrail's own logo, in the same file at the same address.)* *(Since R206 the apex, which only sends its reader on and loads no stylesheet, carries neither the theme nor the tokens.)* A white page, near-black headings, grey text, and blue for whatever can be followed or pressed, with buttons shaped as pills and a quiet grey footer. The palette is named in the site's own stylesheet, `web/src/site/style.css`, under `--s-*`, and the site has no dark theme: every page carries `data-theme="light"` and `color-scheme: light`. The shared tokens still give the site its font stacks, its spaces, its radii and its widths, and every page loads them first. The draft stays the reference for what the site's pages hold and how they are arranged — the header with the mark and the languages, the footer with the documents and the note on translations — and no license line, which the README carries, the home page's sections in T66a — and for the whole of the widget's card; the card and the sign-in pages keep the draft's palette. — *Why:* the author's decision of 2026-10-01, with the product page of Google Drive as the example: the site is where a parent first meets the product, and a plain white page reads as familiar. The fonts stay the system's: the example's typeface is Google's own, under a license the project cannot ship, and the site loads nothing from another origin. — *Rejected:* the new palette in the shared tokens, which would change the card in Claude and ChatGPT and the consent screen with it, and send every state of the card back to the author; a dark theme, which the example does not have. — *Consequence:* the site and the consent screen differ in colour, which R109 had ruled out; a white consent screen would be a small task of its own. The header's mark is the site's own icon rather than the draft's, which came from another app (T61a). The demo card on the home page (T66a) keeps the card's own look inside the white page. — *Where it lives:* `web/src/site/style.css`, `web/src/site/Layout.tsx`; PRODUCT 4.2; SPEC 8.12; RUN.md (T66, T66a).

**R143. The site is drawn by the widget's toolchain, every page in memory, before a stylesheet is built.** *(Since T66c the list of addresses the site has published is held by the site's checker rather than the build's test, R25.)* *(Since R159 every page but the privacy policy and the terms is a component whose words are YAML, a file per page and language; since R160 a page on the build's list may run a script, and a page's weight counts what its stylesheets load.)* `web/scripts/prerender-site.ts`, run by Node as it stands, reads the texts in `site/content/<locale>/*.md`, loads `web/src/site/render.tsx` through a Vite server that serves nothing and watches nothing (`ssrLoadModule`), and draws every page with `preact-render-to-string`. The front matter is read as the Go renderer read it, the Markdown by `marked` as CommonMark alone, and the head, the addresses, the sitemap, `robots.txt` and `CNAME` come out as they did, byte for byte where they are files of their own. A text holding markup is refused, and so is one with a link or an image whose address may run something — a `javascript:`, `vbscript:`, `file:` or `data:` address, or one written with a character reference, which `marked` leaves in an address as it is and a browser decodes — where goldmark had blanked such an address out. `vite build` with `web/vite.config.site.ts` builds one stylesheet, `assets/style.css`, from an entry that is a stylesheet alone, under a fixed name, into a directory of its own, and it is read back; `internal/widget/tokens.css` and `site/assets/favicon.svg` are read as they are. Only when every file is made, and no two are bound for one path, is the output replaced, so a build that fails at any step leaves the last one where it was. It replaces only a directory that does not exist yet, is empty, or holds a build of the site, which always carries `.nojekyll`: a directory named by mistake, the repository among them, is refused rather than deleted. The base must be an origin written exactly as a browser writes one, since every address is built from it as it is given. The site's words are dictionaries of its own in `web/src/site/locales/`, held to the English ones — keys, slots, plural forms, empty texts, marks a reader cannot see — by one comparison, `web/src/i18n/dictionaries.ts`, which the tests of both dictionaries and the build itself run; a document's label in the footer is its own title. No page runs a script. A test of the build holds the list of addresses the site has published, so that a page dropped together with every link to it, or the apex, fails, which `sitecheck` alone would not notice. — *Why:* the components are TSX, the dictionaries are found by a glob and the JSON is imported, so the module has to be read by Vite, as the tests and the widget's build read it; a server loading one module is one process, with no bundle written to disk and read back. Making everything first keeps a broken text or a failed build from emptying the last good build; the Go renderer also made everything before it wrote. A build that runs with `NODE_ENV` at `production` throws on no missing word, so the build checks its dictionaries' keys itself, as the Go renderer did. The stylesheet keeps the address it was published under. CommonMark alone, because the texts were written for it, and GitHub's additions would change what a line that happens to look like one of them says. — *Rejected:* `vite build --ssr` and importing the bundle back, a second build and a file on disk for the same result; markdown-it, which brings six packages, one of them under the Python license; a hashed name for the stylesheet, which needs a manifest and changes a published address with every build; the site's words in the widget's dictionaries, which every one of the twenty-two languages would then have to carry; a published-address rule in `sitecheck` now, which would change the checker this move needs unchanged (R25) — T66c's checker takes the rule over. — *Consequence:* the two new packages are development dependencies. `preact-render-to-string` is listed in `THIRD_PARTY_LICENSES` all the same: Preact names it as an optional peer, so the lockfile marks it optional for production, and the license rule counts such a package as shipped; the list's heading says so. The site is covered by `just ci-web` as well as by `just ci-site`. Vitest hands a test a stylesheet imported as text empty, so the rules a stylesheet is held to are read from the file, in the build's own test. — *Where it lives:* `web/src/site/`, `web/scripts/prerender-site.ts` and its test, `web/vite.config.site.ts`, `justfile` (`site`, `site-serve`, `ci-site`), `.github/workflows/pages.yml`; RUN.md (T66, T66c).

**R156. The site's reference is the author's draft, `draft/ui/site/`, and the card's is its own code.** *(Decided by the author, 2026-10-03; it takes the place of R89.)* *(Since R197 the home page's demo comes alive: the widget's own task card on the first screen answers presses, the steps share the cards the build drew, and no card on the site leads to the progress.)* *(Since R188 the home page is ported in two steps: every step of its lesson beside a card of its own, with no script (T66a.1), and the demo that comes alive (T66a.2).)* *(Since R183 the site has an "About" page: the last entry of the menu, and in the footer the page before the documents.)* *(Since R170 a card on the site speaks the widget's own words in the page's language, as the widget's component reads them; the site's words are the card's data and what stands around it.)* *(Since R181 the draft is six pages: on 2026-10-04 the author drew "Techniques".)* *(Since R198 the page of the coach follows a draft of its own, `draft/ui/couch/`, removed once ported, and frames the prototype that draft framed.)* The draft is five pages the author drew in a design tool — the home page, "Why", "Topics", a topic's page ("Knights and liars") and "Research" — each a self-unpacking HTML file that opens in a browser, and all but the home page drawn in Russian alone. It fixes what the site's pages are, their sections and their order, the Russian words, and the look: a white page, always light, pills, the blue of R142's palette and the Onest typeface (R157). The English words are written anew from it and become the source, and the draft is deleted once every page of it is ported (T65). The card — every screen of the widget — has no picture left to be checked against: the export it was ported from (R89) was removed on 2026-10-03, once the widget matched it, and is in the history up to `8ff72f8`. Its reference is its own code, `web/src/design/` and `web/src/widget/`, seen in every state of a lesson in `just web-preview`, measured by `just web-layout` and photographed by `just web-screens`; a change to the card's look is shown to the author there before it ships, and a screen being redrawn after a mockup follows the mockup until it is ported, as the progress followed `rework/3a-ranks.png` (R147). Where the product and the draft disagree, the product wins, and SPEC 8.12 keeps the one list: the catalog's seventeen topics, not the draft's twelve, with the catalog's grades; the catalog's traps, under the names the card gives them; the card as the product draws it; the sealed answer said as it is — the current task's answer and solution, not the whole profile; languages chosen by address rather than by a script; nothing loaded from another origin, and every page readable without a script (R160); no "About" page, and no menu item leading to one; no licence or name line in the footer (R142, О-4). The cards the site shows — the demo on the home page, the progress on "Topics" and a wrong answer on "Why" — are the widget's own components, drawn at build time through one seam from invented data, in the card's own palette and the system font, in the light theme, as a chat draws them. The card's stylesheet loads only on a page that draws one, and only the home page's demo comes alive with a script (T66a). A question and the model's reply are shown as messages of the chat beneath the card, labelled as an illustration, since a card never holds the model's words (R91, R145). The demo's words are the site's, never the widget's dictionaries. — *Why:* the author's decision of 2026-10-03. The new draft is the site the author wants, and a site written against a description of it would drift from it page by page, as R89 said of the card; the card is ported, and its code is what runs in the hosts, so a picture beside it could only fall out of date. The card on the site keeps its own look by the author's choice between it and the draft, which restyles the demo in the site's palette and typeface: a parent sees on the site the card the child will see in the chat. — *Rejected:* the card restyled to the site, as the draft draws it, which would need a second palette for the card and show a card no chat draws; keeping a picture as the card's reference, when the only one left is the 4a mockup of the progress, which T74 ports. — *Consequence:* R90–R93 stand. The site's pages are ported in T75.2–T75.10 and T66a, and "Research" in T72.13. — *Where it lives:* `draft/ui/site/`, `web/src/site/`, `web/src/design/`, `web/src/widget/`, `web/src/preview/`; SPEC 8.12; RUN.md (T75, T66a, T65).

**R157. The site sets its text in Onest, served from its own domain.** *(Decided by the author, 2026-10-03; it revises R142's fonts.)* *(Since T75.2 the site's stylesheet declares the two subsets itself, since the package's own stylesheet declares all seven; it names each file by its path in the package, which the stylesheet's build follows and copies to `/assets/` under its own name, and a test of the build holds the declared ranges to the package's `unicode.json`.)* The typeface comes from npm, `@fontsource-variable/onest` at an exact version (5.3.1 now), among the dependencies rather than the development ones, since the site ships its files. The site serves only the subsets its languages need — latin and cyrillic, 49,620 bytes together — each declared with its own `unicode-range` and `font-display: swap`, from its own `/assets/`, with the font's licence beside them; a character outside them, an arrow or a tick, is drawn by the next font of the stack, and the subsets of the other languages come with them (T66b). The site's stylesheet names the font in a variable of its own, `--s-font`, which falls back to the shared system stack. `--font-sans` in the tokens is not touched, so the card, on the site as in a chat, and the sign-in pages keep the system font and load nothing; the sign-in pages' policy admits no font at all (SPEC 9.2). The font is under the SIL Open Font License 1.1 and reserves no name. The licence check lets it through for npm alone: `FONT_LICENSES := "OFL-1.1"` joins the list the npm check is held to, while the Go modules stay held to R21's. — *Why:* the author's choice: the draft is drawn in Onest. The OFL lets a font be bundled, embedded and redistributed with software under any licence, so long as every copy carries the font's notice and licence, and keeps only the font itself under its own terms: nothing of the project's code changes licence, which is what "MIT-compatible" asks of a dependency. Fontsource's files are Google Fonts' subsets of the font, a modified version in the licence's sense, and they may keep the name because the font reserves none. — *Rejected:* Google Fonts from its own domain, a load from another origin, which the privacy policy promises the site does not make; a font copied into the repository by hand, with no version and out of sight of the licence check and of Dependabot; an entry in `NPM_LICENSE_EXCEPTIONS`, which is kept for tools that ship nothing (R85); Onest in the shared tokens, which would change the card in Claude and ChatGPT; the draft's Source Serif 4 on "Research", 200 to 400 KB a page; the math and symbols subsets, 43,648 bytes for a few arrows and signs. — *Consequence:* the site's tests that refuse `url(` in its stylesheet let through the addresses of its own font files alone, and a page's weight counts every font file its stylesheets declare (R160). — *Where it lives:* `web/package.json`, `web/src/site/style.css`, `justfile` (`FONT_LICENSES`, `licenses`, `ci-licenses`), `THIRD_PARTY_LICENSES`; SPEC 8.12; RUN.md (T75.2, T66b).

**R158. A page's address is `/<locale>/<page>/`, and a topic's is `/<locale>/topics/<slug>/`.** *(Decided by the author, 2026-10-03; it revises R24, which knew one level.)* *(Since R167 every page the site serves is on the list of published addresses, and the checker refuses one that is not.)* *(Since R206 the apex no longer names the product and its languages: it sends the reader on to `/en/`.)* A page's name is the same English word in every language — `why`, `topics`, `research`, `privacy`, `terms` — and a topic's slug is the catalog's (R164); the apex stays the page that names the product and its languages. What R24 said of an address holds of both shapes: once published, it never moves. The list of published addresses the site's checker holds (R25) is written by hand, page by page, and never derived from the catalog or from the texts: derived, it would lose a topic's address without a word the day the topic dropped out, which is what the list is there to catch. Anchors that are linked from outside their page are held the same way: `#traps` and `#home` on every topic's page, which the card links (T74.6), and the home page's `#lesson` and `#connect`, which the header links. A page exists in every language of the site or in none, and the build refuses anything between, since the checker's rule only finds a page a translation lacks. — *Why:* the topics' pages are a level under one page of the site, and an address a card has handed out forgives a move as little as one Google holds. — *Rejected:* `/<locale>/<slug>/` for a topic, which sets seventeen names beside the site's own pages and lets a slug collide with one of them; page names translated into each language, which would give one page twenty-two addresses to keep. — *Where it lives:* `web/src/site/addresses.ts`, `web/tools/sitecheck/published.ts`; SPEC 8.12; RUN.md (T75.2, T75.3, T74.6).

**R159. The site's pages are Preact components drawn at build time, and their words are YAML, a file per page and language.** *(Decided by the author, 2026-10-03; it revises R143, which drew Markdown documents alone.)* *(Since T75.2: a component reads its words through the build, which notes every key it reads, and that is how keys are checked both ways. A file gives the page's title and description, which hold no slot and no markup, since the head, the apex and the footer show them as they are; YAML reads a text that begins with a star and no quote as an alias, which the parser leaves standing, so the refusal names the quotes that mend it. A page's name is its file's path without the extension — `topics/<slug>` — and a component draws it from `web/src/site/pages.ts`; a file no component draws stops the build.)* The privacy policy and the terms stay Markdown documents, read as R143 reads them. Every other page is a component, and its words are a file of their own: `site/content/<locale>/<page>.yaml`, and a topic's `site/content/<locale>/topics/<slug>.yaml`. The interface's words — the header, the footer, the labels of a page's sections — stay in the site's dictionaries, `web/src/site/locales/`. A file is read by the `yaml` package at an exact version (2.9.1 now), under the ISC licence and with no dependencies, at build time alone, in its failsafe schema, so that every value is a string; it holds one document, anchors, aliases, tags and merge keys are refused, and comments — notes for whoever translates — are welcome. Its sections and lists are laid out into keys in dot notation, the items of a list numbered from 1, and the keys are held to the English file's by the comparison the dictionaries already pass, `web/src/i18n/dictionaries.ts` — the same keys, so that no section, example or trap goes missing in a translation; the same slots; no empty text and no mark a reader cannot see — and, beyond it, by their order, so that the English and the Russian file read side by side, line by line. A page's text has no plural forms: a phrase with a number in it belongs to the dictionary, which has them. Inside a text, emphasis alone is markup, `*…*` and `**…**`, read by `marked` as CommonMark inline; any other token — a link, an image, raw HTML — is refused, as R143 refuses it in a document. A link, a name in the middle of a sentence and a number about the product are slots, which the page's component fills: it knows the language's addresses and reads the numbers from the catalog. A text drawing is a literal block, drawn in `<pre dir="ltr">` (SPEC 8.8). Keys are checked both ways: a page reads only keys its file has, and every key of the file is read by the page. Facts that do not depend on the language are no file's words — a topic's group, an example's level and the data that checks it, the numbers of "Research"; they come from the catalog, from `content/` or from one file of the site's data. What the texts may say is SPEC 8.12's. — *Why:* the author's choice of YAML over JSON, for the reading: a paragraph runs over lines of its own instead of one quoted line, and a note can stand beside a text. A file per page keeps a page's words together and the interface's dictionaries small, and laying a file out into keys lets the comparison every dictionary already passes hold a translation to the English, which is how a missing example or trap is found before anyone reads the page. — *Rejected:* JSON, the same structure with a paragraph on one line and no notes; Markdown with sections, which CommonMark gives no tables and a heading too loose a hold on a page's parts; the pages' words in the site's dictionaries, which would bury the interface's few under seventeen pages; the words inside the components, which would make every translation an edit of code. — *Consequence:* `yaml` is a new development dependency. The home page moves from `index.md` to `index.yaml` with its demo (T66a). — *Where it lives:* `site/content/`, `web/src/site/`, `web/scripts/prerender-site.ts`, `web/src/i18n/dictionaries.ts`; SPEC 8.12; RUN.md (T75.2, T75.4, T75.5, T75.10, T66a, T66b).

**R160. A page reads in full without a script, and a script only adds to it.** *(Decided by the author, 2026-10-03; it revises R143's "No page runs a script".)* *(Since R188 the home page is published first with no script, a card for each step of its lesson; the demo, its script, its place on the list and the button that copies the address come with T66a.2.)* *(Since R197 the home page is on the list: one module, `assets/demo.js`, which the site's checker holds to loading no other file and naming no way to send or keep anything; with it the home page weighs 261.2 KiB in English and 275.3 KiB in Russian.)* *(Since T75.2 the checker follows a stylesheet's `url()` and `@import` from stylesheet to stylesheet, for a page's weight and as links: a file a stylesheet loads that the site does not have is a broken link.)* *(Since R198 a document a page frames is weighed apart from the page, with everything it loads, against 2 MiB of its own, and the page keeps its 300 KiB without it: the coach's prototype, some 1.8 MB, in the frame of the coach's page.)* *(Since R200 a page is weighed without the site's photographs, the files below `/assets/photos/`, and a photograph has no budget of its own: the page "About" weighs 101.7 KiB in English and 102.7 KiB in Russian without the family's five, which add some 520 KiB.)* Every word of a page is in the HTML the build draws. A page may run a script only if it is on a list the build's test holds, which stays empty until the home page's demo joins it (T66a), and a script stores nothing and sends nothing, as the privacy policy promises.
